# TheApp - Capability Checklist

- [x] **Phase 1 — Foundation**
  - [x] TheApp installs on a clean machine - Installability treated as a 0.1.0 feature rather than a packaging afterthought, so no source-mode path ever diverges from the installed one. Per-user install, no elevation, unsigned with a published SHA-256, and one self-contained file with no runtime prerequisite. Not statically linked in the C sense - native libraries self-extract on first run - because architecture.md §9 shows that phrase has no clean referent in .NET, and NativeAOT stays deferred until the native dependency set is stable.
  - [x] TheApp keeps your key and your state safe - Settings survive a restart, unknown keys are rejected, and everything writable lives in one folder beside the executable. Two stores because one loader cannot both fail loudly and shrug; writes go to a `.writing` sibling then an atomic move, and secrets are DPAPI-encrypted at rest.
  - [x] TheApp says what went wrong - Levels, one target per subsystem, structured fields, and two sinks - one human-readable, one machine-readable so an agent can parse a session. No telemetry leaves the machine: no analytics, no metrics endpoint, no upload. Provider traffic is a separate claim with a separate row, because INARA, a cloud LLM, a paid TTS and web search all send journal-derived content off the machine when enabled.
  - [x] Turn a subsystem up without restarting - Runtime per-subsystem verbosity control.
  - [x] TheApp calls a capability - A request produces a real tool call that runs and returns a result, covering the capability registry and the descriptor declaring identity, tools, help, settings rows and display model. Descriptors are registered once and never mutated, and every tool profile's schema block is byte-identical across turns, so prompt caching survives a mode change.
  - [x] Every capability has a documentation page - CI fails if a registered capability has no documentation page, making "write the docs later" impossible rather than merely discouraged. Pages are written from real artifacts and must quote at least one code block or line of real output.
  - [x] GitHub Pages documentation - Each capability should have a documentation page. General help for TheApp outside of the particular capabilities is also available.

- [x] **Phase 2 — Journal spine**
  - [x] Journal behavior is testable without the game - A recorded session replays deterministically at 1x and 100x with no game, headset or hardware. Fixtures are byte-preserved via `.gitattributes` and scrubbed of the Commander name and real system visits, since the repository is public.
  - [x] TheApp knows where you are - TheApp answers what system you are in from the journal as part of an ordinary turn. Picks the newest journal by ordinal filename sort, opens shared for read/write/delete since Elite holds the file open, and is pull-based with no internal thread, which is what makes it testable.
  - [x] Survive a journal schema change - Elite adds and changes journal events several times a year. Unknown events are logged and skipped, a parse failure never kills the tail loop, and the machine-readable sink makes the diff findable, so a game patch costs a morning rather than an outage.
  - [x] Handle more than one Commander on a machine - A second Commander's journals must not merge into the first one's fleet, materials or location. The Commander is identified from the journal header and all derived state is kept per Commander, because a naive newest-file reader silently blends two people into one.

- [x] **Phase 3 — First conversation (typed)**
  - [x] TheApp answers a typed question - Type a question into TheApp's window and get a streamed reply from the model.
  - [x] Ship's AI Unsure - An explicit "unsure" result instead of a confidence threshold, because models produce confident-sounding scores that do not mean anything. A model-free keyword router ships alongside so the whole path is exercisable without a model.
  - [x] Capabilities as state, not guard - For example, an LLM or TTS experiencing downtime or token depletion flips a capability off and the next turn reads what is currently available, so there is no failure handler to author or keep in register. It falls out that every input path must be answerable with no capabilities at all.
  - [x] Model Level and Thinking - LLM attempts to gauge per-turn thinking effort from low through max, but no "off" unless the LLM is set to "none" or through transient degradation.
  - [x] LLM Turn Price - Per-turn token usage including cache reads, priced from a table, plus a running total. A profile switch is the only sanctioned cause of a cold prefix, so an unexplained cache miss is a regression the running total can surface rather than hide.

- [x] **Phase 4 — Settings surface**
  - [x] TheApp is configured without manually editing a file - Every setting, including the voice, is changeable in the UI with no hand-editing. Rows are generated from capability descriptors, defaults appear as placeholders rather than values, keys are write-only, and safety-critical rows are protected from the model.
  - [x] Apply every setting without a restart - Changing a setting takes effect immediately (no need to manually "save").
  - [x] Offer one searchable picker everywhere a value is chosen - A command-palette style picker with keyboard navigation, reused for voices, models and devices. Fail-soft by contract: with an empty list you can still keep the current value or type one.
  - [x] Show the controls the active provider actually has - Settings adapt to the selected provider instead of showing a hardwired set, and changing an endpoint resets the model list to that endpoint's namespace rather than leaving a stale selection.
  - [x] Settings only expose specifics of a provider (LLM, TTS, STT), such as selected voice(s), Model, etc. for the currently selected provider.
  - [x] Protect safety-critical settings from the model - Rows gating keyboard actions and macros are marked protected: never changeable through a tool the model can call, because the model consumes untrusted text and a guard it can flip is privilege escalation. Protected is a property of the caller, not of the modality. The panel, a hotkey and the model-free keyword router all reach these rows; the LLM path does not.
  - [x] Say what each provider receives - A settings row and a documentation page stating exactly what leaves the machine for each enabled provider: system and station names to INARA, turn text and game context to a cloud LLM, reply text to a paid TTS, query text to web search. What is currently leaving is answerable at any time, and every destination can be turned off. Amended in Phase 12: this used to promise local-only operation as a named configuration, which priced every later feature against a slogan rather than against the enumeration - what is owed is that nothing reaches a network without appearing in the list.
  - [x] Link each settings row to its documentation - A setup-guide link per settings row pointing at that setting's page. In-app help stays the short form; the page is the long form.
  - [x] Collapse settings cards - Collapsible sections whose collapse state is remembered as a view preference, not a setting, and applied before first paint so a collapsed card never flashes open.
  - [x] Settings Nav Menu - Is present and highlights the section based on the topmost visible setting.
  - [x] Hotkey Binding - (Only in main window) Press the key to bind it.
  - [x] Themes - Dark, light, default Elite-flavored, Guardian, and based on the current Elite Color Scheme palette, with color living in one place so no view hardcodes a literal.

- [x] **Phase 5 — Speaking**
  - [x] One audio stream - Everything audible goes through one queue with priority and supersede, including audio cues, so ducking, interruption and captioning need no second mechanism. One arbiter for every voice, because separate paths per voice are how a line gets spoken in the wrong one. The arbiter exposes a render-side reference tap from the start, since echo cancellation needs the far-end signal and retrofitting that tap means opening the one component every voice path depends on.
  - [x] TheApp speaks its answer aloud - The reply is audible in the chosen voice. Synthesis is sentence-chunked so speaking starts at the first sentence boundary - the largest perceived-latency win available.
  - [x] Say so when the model is misconfigured - An audible warning when the provider setup is wrong, rather than silence that is indistinguishable from a model with nothing to say.
  - [x] Say something when a turn is taking too long - Retry with backoff (N tries, X wait, sequential or logarithmic), and if exceeded say so aloud in the current voice rather than leaving silence.
  - [x] #20 Give every loop state its own audio cue - One default per state. Cue names come from the shipped resources rather than a hand-written table, since a wrong name goes wrong as silence nobody notices.
  - [x] #18 Play a thinking bed while the model works - Audible evidence that TheApp heard you and is working, instead of dead air while a turn runs.
  - [x] Stop - An always-available instant silence that supersedes everything on the queue and stops mid-sentence, reachable by voice and by hotkey and never gated behind a turn completing. A companion that talks is only tolerable if it can be silenced faster than it can finish a sentence.

- [x] **Phase 6 — Listening**
  - [x] Choose the microphone in settings - Pick the input device instead of inheriting the system default. A blank selection produces a silent default and a turn reporting no speech detected, with nothing indicating why.
  - [x] PTT - Hold the key, speak, release, and your words appear. The key-down path awaits nothing before recording starts, and push-to-talk is one gate policy over an audio stream so continuous listening and wake word become later policies rather than a rewrite. May be set as a "toggle" rather than true PTT via settings.
  - [x] STT Model Choice - Commander can choose which Whisper model to use and whether or not to allow it to run on the GPU. In VR the GPU is already the scarce resource, so a large model running there surfaces as dropped frames and reprojection rather than as anything that looks like a speech problem, which is the hardest kind of setting to diagnose. GPU is opt-in with that cost stated on the row, and the default is CPU whenever a headset is present - a short push-to-talk clip on the small English models absorbs that fine.
  - [x] Bias transcription with proper nouns from the journal - Feed system, station and ship names from the journal into the transcriber so they come back spelled correctly. Journal-derived and network-free; proper nouns are where speech recognition fails hardest and most silently.
  - [x] Report a key that is bound twice - A double-bound push-to-talk key currently has no symptom other than not working; detect the collision and say so. Reads the same parsed binds as keyboard-action reachability rather than keeping a second view of the Commander's controls.
  - [x] Settings by voice - Unless otherwise noted, every setting should be settable by voice. Protected rows are reachable by voice only through the model-free keyword router, so "by voice" never silently means "by the LLM."
  - [x] Answer what can you do, from the registry - Spoken help projected from the capability registry - groups, then capabilities, then detail and example phrasings - ranked by real usage. The model is never asked what TheApp can do, because asking produces confidently invented capabilities.

- [x] **Phase 7 — Knowing the game**
  - [x] Know what is happening around you - Live situational awareness attached to each turn: where you are, what you are flying, and what just happened.
  - [x] Know your location and your carrier (if owned) - Current system, station and body, plus where your fleet carrier is.
  - [x] Ship's loadout - Modules and stats from the loadout event.
  - [x] Ship metrics - Base jump range and similar, computed from what the loadout event actually reports rather than from a table of ship specifications.
  - [x] Know what ships you own and where they are - A fleet registry built from stored-ships journal events.
  - [x] Know what materials you are carrying - Materials inventory built from journal events, answerable without an external lookup.
  - [x] Know what is in your backpack and ship locker - On-foot inventory parsed from the local files Elite writes.
  - [x] Session summary - Earnings, bounties, exploration data sold, materials gained and jumps made since the session began, built from the journal already being tailed rather than from anything new.

- [x] **Phase 8 — Proactive speech**
  - [x] Call out danger without waiting for a turn - Interdiction, shields down, hull damage, dangerous heat and a full cargo hold announce themselves from journal and Status.json events. These fire on the event and never at the model's discretion, because an alert that depends on a turn completing is not an alert.
  - [x] Fuel and range safety - Low-fuel warning against the current tank, plus the case that actually strands a Commander: the next star on the route is unscoopable and the one after it is out of range. The journal and the route file supply both, and the warning is unconditional rather than something the model may decide is not worth mentioning.
  - [x] Route Progress - Jumps remaining (every N systems (setting)), next system, neutron and white-dwarf hazards ahead, and scoopable-star ambiguity resolved rather than guessed. Reads the route file Elite writes locally, so no route-planning service is needed.
  - [x] Remark on an unusually long jump - Flavor during a longer-than-normal hyperspace jump, fired once hyperspace has actually been entered rather than on the jump being initiated. Threshold is configurable but defaults to 30 seconds.
  - [x] Recognize where you have arrived - Place-aware callouts for engineer bases, notable stations.
  - [x] Home System - Home callout when you arrive in your home system (setting).
  - [x] Call out material-gathering milestones - The first unit, then 25/50/75%, a running count above 75%, and full. The tracker is primed from the session backlog at startup; otherwise starting TheApp after Elite means the milestones never fire.

- [x] **Phase 9 — VR**
  - [x] Order agnostic Overlay - The order of starting SteamVR, Elite Dangerous, TheApp, doesn't matter.
  - [x] TheApp appears in the headset - Captions render over Elite in VR through OpenVR (Elite never calls OpenXR) and clear on their own. Captions are their own unmovable, output-only, ephemeral layer with a rolling three-line window timed from the end of speech. Captions follow Netflix CC standard.
  - [x] Configure the captions - Follows Netflix CC standards.
  - [x] TheApp's panel works in VR - One widget tree renders to both the desktop window and VR, so the windowed UI can never be more functional than the headset one. Mini is a mode of the same panel - a reduced content set - not a separate surface or a scaled-down copy.
  - [x] VR Panel locking - Head-locked or world-locked placement, per surface. Choosing world-locked implies re-anchor is bound and reachable off-panel.
  - [x] Re-anchor the panels - Elite's in-game recenter moves the cockpit without telling SteamVR, so world-locked surfaces drift out of position with no event to hook. A Commander-triggered re-anchor snaps every world-locked surface back to current head pose and preserves their relative layout rather than stacking them. Reachable by voice and by hotkey, never only from the panel, because a drifted panel is precisely the case where you cannot aim at it. Head-locked surfaces and the caption layer are unaffected.
  - [x] Overlay Positioning & Look - Opacity, curvature, distance, size, and scale, configurable per surface.
  - [x] Panels can switch between curved and flat - The panel is bent {curve setting} around the Commander.
  - [x] Open at a size that fits the screen - Avalonia already matches the monitor's scaling: Width and Height are device-independent pixels, so the 820x640 default is 1230x960 actual pixels at 150%. That is the problem rather than the fix - it is nearly the whole usable height of a 1080p display and taller than a 1366x768 laptop screen outright, because the default was chosen at 100% and never checked against a scaled work area. The opening size is clamped to the working area of the screen it actually appears on, and the size and position the Commander leaves it at are remembered like the panel's collapse state already is, so the default only ever has to be right once.
  - [x] Zoom the desktop window - Ctrl and the scroll wheel, Ctrl+plus, Ctrl+minus, and Ctrl+0 for 100%, because every browser has already taught the Commander those four gestures and a panel that invents its own is a panel they have to be told about. Scales the rendered panel rather than restyling its text, so layout and spacing survive at any level, and it applies to the settings window too since that is the same widget tree. The level persists between sessions like the theme does. This is a desktop item living in a VR phase: its natural home was Phase 4 and that phase is closed, and it belongs beside *Scale the big panel* anyway - shipping a zoom for the headset and none for the window makes the windowed surface the lesser of the two, which is the same parity the one-widget-tree item is protecting from the other direction.
  - [x] Scale the big panel - Zoom the panel up and down in the headset, distinct from mini mode, which reduces content rather than shrinking the rendering.
  - [x] Keep working when the main window is minimized - In 2D or in VR, if TheApp is minimized, all 2D, VR, and voice functionality still works.

- [x] **Phase 10 — Acting on the game**
  - [x] Know which actions the Commander can actually reach - Parse the binds file and know which in-game actions have a keyboard binding at all, because the audience is HOTAS-heavy and a stick-only action asked for by voice fails as silence. Unbound actions are excluded from the advertised profile and asking for one gets a spoken reason instead of nothing. Read-only: TheApp never writes the Commander's bindings.
  - [x] Autonomous actions are opt-in per action - Every other capability acts on something the Commander said. An action that fires a game input on a journal event with nobody asking is a different category and needs its own consent, so each one is off by default and enabled individually. The category gets its rule before the second member of it exists.
  - [x] TheApp honks (discovery scanner) when you arrive in a system - The discovery scanner fires on entering a system using the Commander's own key binding, injected via scancodes with the Elite window focused. Never installs a low-level keyboard hook, and `release_all()` is unconditional because a stranded key here is a throttle that will not stop. The first member of the autonomous-action category and therefore off by default and enabled on its own row.
  - [x] Offer only the actions that work right now - Mode-gated action advertisement, so the set differs on foot, in the SRV, in supercruise and in normal space. The cockpit is not one mode: hardpoints, cargo scoop and landing gear do nothing in supercruise, and Status.json flags supply the split for free. The model is never told about a key that currently does nothing, whether the reason is the mode or the absence of a binding.
  - [x] Decide which tools ship on a turn as the count grows - At some capability count the tool schemas stop fitting comfortably in context and something must choose between them. The choice is between pre-declared profiles, not between individual tools, so budget pressure degrades to a smaller profile instead of destroying caching on the exact turn caching starts to matter. Whatever is chosen, the anti-invention guardrails are static prompt material and must never be strippable by a budget or effort setting.
  - [x] Control flight and navigation by voice - Landing gear, lights, cargo scoop, hardpoints and the frame shift drive.
  - [x] Control ship systems by voice - Power distribution, fuel scoop, silent running and heat sinks.
  - [x] Control panels, UI, and fire groups by voice - Cycle the panels and fire groups without taking a hand off the stick.
  - [x] Control the SRV by voice - Toggles only (turret, forward/back, dismiss/recall ship, board, exit SRV, etc.).
  - [x] Put something on the clipboard - A system name, route or value placed on the clipboard so it can be pasted into the game or a browser.
  - [x] Galaxy Map - Set a course by sending keystrokes to the in-game galaxy map. Best-effort rather than guaranteed, since it depends on map focus, layout and the game's language setting, so the clipboard route is the primary path and auto-plot is the convenience layered on top of it. A failed plot says so rather than leaving the Commander believing a course is set.
  - [x] Send/Receive messages to another commander or commanders - Dictate into Elite's chat: local, wing, system.
  - [x] Macros - Named multi-step voice-triggered sequences, validated against closed vocabularies and the action allowlist. Invocation is by voice; authoring is not, because composing a new action sequence is the one input whose vocabulary cannot be closed in advance. This is the exception to "every setting can be set by voice."

- [x] **Phase 11 — Persona and voices**
  - [x] Personas - Pre-built companion characters the Commander can choose between. Guardian Flavored.
  - [x] Ship AI Naming - Defaults to Persona's name, but may be set by the commander.
  - [x] Say when the persona has changed - New persona acknowledges when it has been picked. If it changes before its acknowledgement has completed speaking, it stops and the next one starts. Speech, as always, may be interrupted by the commander.
  - [x] Personality on/off - Plain answers with no persona, flavor, or ambient remarks. The anti-invention guardrails must survive the persona being switched off, so they cannot live inside the persona's prompt block.
  - [x] Commander's About Me - A Commander-provided prompt stored between sessions and personas so that the LLM remembers basic facts about the commander.
  - [x] Ship's AI Avatar - Per-state avatar imagery on the panel. Animated formats supported.
  - [x] ElevenLabs - First offered, paid voice provider alongside the free Edge Neural voices, chosen per role, so a Commander with a key hears it and nothing else changes. Other providers may be added later in development. Differences between providers, such as speed, is maintained on a per-provider basis.
  - [x] #33 Pair a default voice to each persona - At first startup, lazy-load available voices (Edge neural defaults to "en-" voices). Using LLM (if available), a sensible voice is chosen for each persona based on the persona's prompt (or custom prompt) in the background, so picking a character does not also mean auditioning potentially hundreds of voices.
  - [x] Ambient Voice - In-character ambient lines motivated by live game state, varied (by LLM if available, otherwise by initially the 10 generated stock phrases per game state covered).
  - [x] Speak incoming messages in another voice - Re-voice in-game communications so they do not arrive in the ship AI's own voice.
  - [x] Voices "stick" - Once a voice has been chosen for a sender it stays with them. NPC identities are scoped to the system, since the cast turns over on a jump. Player Commanders are scoped to the session and survive hyperspace, because a wingmate whose voice changes on every jump reads as a bug rather than as variety.
  - [x] Carrier Captain - If a player owns a Fleet Carrier, they can choose dedicated voices for the Captain and Tower Control, with varied LLM arrival and departure responses.
  - [x] Ship Crew - Multi-character roleplay with invisible crew (aside from the Ship's AI) on the conversation path, with per-ship rosters drawn from the real fleet and hired NPC pilots imported from the journal with their roles. Crew is addressable and responsive via LLM.

- [x] **Phase 12 — Soundscape**
  - [x] Three pages for the transcript - The big text area is one of three pages, chosen by tabs along the top of it: **Conversation** (the Commander and the ship's AI, and nothing else), **Technical** (the same with the diagnostics left in, which is what the panel always showed), and **Log file** (today's log, read when the page is opened). The split rests on the caller naming the kind of line as it writes it, because it is not recoverable afterwards - a version banner and a reply are both text, and separating them by pattern is guessing at somebody's prose. The page is a property of the surface like `PanelMode` is, and for the same reason: both surfaces bind one model, so a page held there would send the headset to the log the moment the window went to it. Mini shows no tabs, being the tail and the provenance line and nothing else. They have to read as tabs rather than as buttons or links, which is a property of the join and not of the label: the selected one shares the panel's fill and drops the border where they meet. Verify by looking at the captures.
  - [x] One type scale, and d47's own text sized to match its controls - A row's label reads smaller than the value it labels: every control renders at the framework default of 14 while d47 sets its own text by hand at 10 to 13, so a label is 13, its help is 11, and the combo box beside them is 14. The fix is to raise ours rather than shrink the controls - a settings surface is read at a glance from a metre away in a headset, and the answer to that is never less type. Forty-six size literals across seven files with nothing naming them is why they drifted apart in the first place, so this replaces them with a scale named by role - heading, subheading, body, secondary, small - and body is whatever the controls are. Touches `PanelView` as well as the settings surface, since the avatar landed there in Phase 11, and both surfaces have to move together or the headset and the window disagree about what a label looks like. Verify by looking at the render captures across themes at more than one zoom level: the failure this is fixing was visible in a screenshot and invisible to every test.
  - [x] **Settings is a tab of the main window, not a second window** - One visible window. Settings becomes another tab in the strip that already carries Conversation, Technical and Log file, and takes the place of the big multiline text area when it is selected; the Ask line below it gives way to the settings footer, which is where the storage line and its own controls already live. The short-lived modals stay modal - About, confirm, the picker, hotkey capture. The plumbing is nearly free: `SettingsView` is a window-agnostic `UserControl`, every dialog it opens takes its owner from `TopLevel.GetTopLevel(this)` and re-parents, and the second `ZoomHost` and window-placement slot both retire. The real work is width: the settings surface is a 224px nav column plus a 700px minimum and opens at 1180, against a panel window of 820 meant to sit beside a running game - so the nav column has to collapse below about 900, or the second window has not been removed so much as the first one made too big to keep on screen. Two behaviours need deciding rather than porting: what Escape does when there is nothing to close, and hotkey capture now tunnelling on the same top level that carries the open-settings and focus-ask gestures. The VR overlay renders the panel and must not gain a settings surface it was never sized for - mini already hides the tab strip, and full has to keep the tab out of the headset rather than showing a 1180px column at a metre.
  - [x] Search whichever tab you are looking at - One search affordance for the main window, on the tab that is selected: Conversation, Technical, Log file, and Settings once it is a tab rather than a window. One control and one gesture, not four features bolted to four pages - Ctrl+F focuses it, Escape clears it and gives the page back. What a match *does* differs by page and that difference is the design rather than an inconsistency: the transcript pages are prose, so a query highlights every hit, says how many there are and steps through them, leaving the surrounding lines in place because reading back a conversation means reading what was around it; Settings is 92 rows across 14 sections, so a query **filters** - rows whose label, help text or key match - and the nav column shows only the sections still holding something, since highlighting in place in a haystack is a scroll hunt with extra colour. The query belongs to the page and is dropped when the page changes, because one string that filters here and highlights there is a control that behaves differently depending on where you last clicked. Must not fight the log arriving underneath it - a hit found in the live log stays found as lines append, which is the same problem *Follow the live log* solves and should use whatever answer that reaches. Desktop only: the headset has no keyboard, mini shows no tab strip, and a search box the Commander cannot type into is worse than no search at all.
  - [x] Anything that might take a moment says it is working - The rule already written into the picker button - "a button that looks unchanged for a second reads as a button that did not take the click" - applied everywhere it is true rather than at the one place it was noticed. Opening the Log file tab reads a file off disk; a model list, a voice list and a key verification each cross the network; a gap reaction spends a model round trip before the core says its first word. Each of those can look exactly like nothing happening. **The affordance that was touched is the thing that shows it** - the tab, the button, the row - never a spinner somewhere else on the surface, because a Commander who clicked here does not look there. Reuse `BusyGlyph`, which exists for this. Two failure modes to hold off at once: no silence on the slow case, and **no flash on the fast one** - an operation that finishes in 40ms must not strobe, which means a short delay before showing and a minimum time on screen once shown, and those two numbers stated in one place rather than chosen per call site. The control shuts while it works, as the picker's already does, so a second click cannot queue a second copy of the same fetch. Written as something a call site opts into in one line, not as a pattern to be reimplemented: the next long operation is the one that will ship without it. Verify from the captures - both surfaces, because the headset shows the same widget tree and a glyph that only animates on the desktop is half an answer.
  - [x] #96 Ambient audio mixer - Per-category levels and mute, plus how each category ducks against speech.
  - [x] Custom Sound Cues - Drop in your own audio files and have TheApp use them, kept distinct from the set TheApp ships with.
  - [x] Ambient music - Music separate from cues and sound effects, with its own level and its own ducking behavior against speech. Ambience is situational based on journal or "general."
  - [x] Pick up dropped-in audio without a restart - Cues, sound effects, music and ambient content discovered from convention folders and reloaded live.

- [x] **Phase 13 — Hands-free listening**
  - [x] Echo Cancellation - Acoustic echo cancellation, for continuous listening and natural interruption. Consumes the render reference tap from the single audio arbiter rather than a loopback capture, which is both lower latency and correctly aligned. WebRTC AEC3 is BSD-3. The tap was built in Phase 5 with nothing subscribed to it precisely so this would be one subscription rather than surgery on the component every voice path depends on, and it was: `EchoCanceller` is framing either side of the module and nothing else, since WASAPI hands over whatever buffer size it likes and AEC3 takes exactly 10 ms. Measured against a simulated room with a 60 ms path, upwards of 25 dB removed with the near end kept within a few dB through double-talk — which is the half that matters, because a canceller that solved the problem by going deaf while d47 speaks would pass the first measurement and deliver none of the point of it. Two findings worth keeping: a **harmonic** far end defeats the delay estimator outright, so a test written with a sine wave in it measures nothing; and whether the canceller is running has to be read from the canceller rather than from the row that asked for it, because that boolean is what decides whether hands-free listening stays open while d47 talks. Uncancelled, it does not — half duplex is the honest fallback and the row says so. The disposal is the sharp edge: applying a settings row frees the module from one thread while two audio threads are inside it, and that is a native access violation rather than an exception — nothing catches it and the process simply goes. So every native call and the disposal share one lock, which costs the render and capture overlap the module would have allowed and buys the safety by construction rather than by timing. A concurrency test found the other half of the same mistake within seconds: the module was published before its stream configs, so a frame could arrive, see a module, and dereference a config that did not exist yet.
  - [x] Voice Activity Detection (VAD) - (An option) Voice activity detection as a continuous-listening gate policy over the existing stream. Phase 6's "one gate policy over a continuous stream" was written as a promise about this phase and it held: a detector on the same `Write` path, opening the same gate, with the same pre-roll covering its onset delay that already covered the polling delay on the key. **The adaptive noise floor is the whole design** — an absolute threshold is what makes every energy VAD either deaf on a quiet microphone or permanently open on a loud one, because "loud enough to be speech" is a property of a gain that varies by tens of decibels across the interfaces this will meet. So the floor follows the room, falling fast and rising slowly, frozen while somebody is talking so a long sentence cannot drag it up to meet itself, seeded from the first frame rather than climbing from silence, and floored absolutely so a muted device cannot make speech out of its own dither. The detector decides on the audio thread and the **tick thread acts on it**: opening plays a cue and closing hands an utterance to a transcriber, and neither belongs on a real-time callback. The key stays live in every mode and outranks the detector.
  - [x] Wake Word - Wake-word gating, implemented as another gate policy over the same audio stream. **It matches words rather than audio**, which is the design and not a shortcut: d47 already has a speech model and a detector that says where a sentence started and stopped, so the wake word is the cheapest possible thing on top of both — no second model to ship, no second download, no vocabulary fixed at build time, and no keyword that cannot be the name the Commander gave their ship's AI. Leave the row unset and it *is* that name, so renaming the core renames the wake word. Matching is deliberately forgiving, on a form with punctuation and spacing removed, because Whisper writes a short name however it hears it — `D47`, `D 47`, `d-47` are one word — and the name has to be near the front, since talking about d47 is not talking to it. The name alone wakes rather than asks: the listening cue sounds, and a window opens in which the next sentence needs no name, because that is how a person addresses anybody. The cue rather than the loop state behind it — entering Listening would be accurate for as long as the window lasted and then stuck, since nothing settles out of that state, so a Commander who said the name and changed their mind would leave the face listening for the rest of the session. What is waiting is the microphone, and the microphone has an indicator of its own now. The cost is stated rather than hidden: everything said in the room is transcribed before it can be discarded, so this wants one of the smaller models.
  - [x] Show that the microphone is open - Once wake word or VAD means continuous capture, listening state is visible on both the panel and the VR surface. Continuous capture with no visible state is the thing a user is right to distrust, and the indicator is a property of the gate policy rather than of any one capability. Three states rather than two, because the honest inventory has three: **open** and keeping what it hears, **armed** and deciding for itself, and **idle** — the device open with nothing kept, which is what push-to-talk has always quietly done and never shown. Filled or hollow says which before colour does. It lives in the row that carries the provenance line rather than in the header, because the header is what mini takes away and mini is the headset's normal state — and a Commander in a headset has no window to go and check.

- [x] **Phase 14 — Knowledge and external data**
  - [x] Galaxy Search - Systems, stations and bodies, distances computed from coordinates. Query parameters are validated locally because the search service ignores filter keys it does not recognize. **Measured, and worse than stated:** a *misspelled* real filter is ignored exactly as a bogus one is — `allegience` returned the same 108 systems as no filter at all where `allegiance` returned 60 — so the dangerous case is not a typo'd request failing, it is a typo'd request succeeding and reading like a filtered answer. A wrong *value* shape is a loud 400; a wrong *key* is a confident answer to a question nobody asked. Hence a closed vocabulary in Core, checked before a request is built. The other half of the item is the projection: one system record is 268 KB and a three-result search is 44 KB, so what reaches the model is six fields per system and the raw record never leaves the client. Distances are computed from coordinates here, because there is no lookup-by-name endpoint at all — `api/system/name/…` is a 404 — so a spoken name becomes a position by naming it as a *search reference* and reading back what it resolved to.
  - [x] Find Nearest - Nearest module, nearest ship for sale, stored ships and modules, plus body and signal lookups. Module and ship names are matched against the real catalogue (48 ships, 132 modules, read from the service's own `field_values` on 2026-08-14) before a request is built. The catalogue is validated but deliberately **not advertised** — an `enum` that large sits in prompt position 1 where one byte invalidates the whole cached prefix, so the parameter is free text and the matching happens locally. It handles both ways a spoken name arrives wrong: a fragment ("shield generator") and a transcriber's misspelling ("Frame Shift Drve"). Stock age is part of every answer, because outfitting is crowd-reported — a live search returned a carrier last reported in 2024 beside stations reported that morning. Stored *ships* came from the fleet tool in an earlier phase; stored *modules* are now `get_stored_modules`, folded from `StoredModules` and grouped by where each one is with what fetching it costs — read off disk, so it answers with the galaxy search switched off, which is what a fresh install is. **Three wire shapes, and two of them lie.** The station `modules` filter is a *group* whose members each take their own value array, and the flat spelling is silently ignored exactly as a bad system filter key is: the group shape returned 3,402 stations where the flat shape returned the unfiltered 10,000. The body `signals` filter is a *third* shape — `{"name":{"value":["Biological"]},"count":2}`, a choice member beside a bare number — and the range spelling returned the unfiltered 1,315 bodies within 20 ly of Sol. Worse, `count` is **exact rather than a minimum**: 1 returned 41 bodies, 2 returned 14, 3 returned none and 4 returned 2, each carrying precisely the number asked for, so the tool says "exactly how many" — a "three or more" that quietly meant "exactly three" is a wrong answer that reads like a right one. `distance_to_arrival` is read off every result and **never offered as a filter**, because the service ignores it: 0-10 light seconds returned the same 1,315 bodies as no filter at all. Body types are matched rather than listed (61 of them) and the matcher now takes a *unique* fragment — "earth-like" names exactly one subtype and is what a person says, while "gas giant" names six and is refused with the candidates rather than answered about whichever came first.
  - [x] Route Planning - Neutron and long-range plotting, trade loops, Road to Riches, and mining routes. A plot is a different protocol from a search — submitted, queued and polled — so it is its own seam and its own capability, sharing the galaxy search's one setting because it is the same host and the same decision. Measured on 2026-08-14: Sol to Colonia came back on the third poll, a Road to Riches loop on the first, a four-hop trade route in forty-eight seconds; hence a ninety-second budget against a search's fifteen, and a sentence saying what to shorten when it runs out. **Efficiency is backwards from its name** — it is how strictly the plotter holds to the direct line, so lower wanders further, finds more neutron stars and finishes in fewer jumps: 10 took 156 jumps, 25 took 157, 60 took 168, and 100 was accepted and then found no route at all, which is why it is clamped to 99. **The route endpoints echo back only the parameters they understood**, which is a local oracle the search endpoints do not offer and how every name here was established rather than guessed: `capital`, `cargo_capacity`, `capacity` and `loop` are all silently dropped from a trade plot where `starting_capital` and `max_cargo` survive — and a dropped capital is not an error, it is a plot that finds nothing affordable and reads exactly like a correct answer about a Commander with no money. Your ship fills in its own jump range, origin and hold from the journal; **your balance never does**, because it is the one figure here that is about you rather than your ship, so a trade route asks for a number instead of reading one. "No route exists" arrives as a *completed* job whose status is failed and is reported as an answer, since retrying fails identically every time. **Mining routes do not exist:** `api/mining/route` is a 404, and what the service actually has is the ring index — which is `find_body` on the galaxy search, with hotspot material, overlap count, ring composition and reserve level. A mining-route tool that quietly ran a body search would be a worse answer than not having one. **Deferred with a reason:** the galaxy plotter (`api/generic/route`) takes no jump range — it wants the drive's physics, `optimal_mass`, `fuel_power`, `fuel_multiplier`, `max_fuel_per_jump`, `range_boost`, and answers `ship details are invalid` without them. Part of that is in the `Loadout` engineering modifiers and part needs a module specification table, so it belongs behind the item that provides one rather than bolted on with invented numbers.
  - [x] Elite Dangerous Ships - Ship and module specifications from a dataset lazy-queried at runtime and generated when new ships are detected. None of this is in the journal — the journal says what *this* Commander is flying, not what a hull can do before they buy one — so it was a table or no feature, and a hand-written one is the confidently-invented game data the guardrails exist to prevent: a wrong top speed reads identically to the feature working. `tools/gen-elite-specs.py` derives it by joining EDCD/FDevIDs (the naming authority, and the source of the symbols the journal writes) with EDCD/coriolis-data (the figures) on Frontier's own ids, and **the join is the check** — 44 ships and 925 modules, 80 KB, embedded and parsed on first use rather than at startup, which is what "lazy-queried at runtime" buys. **The new-ship case is real and already firing:** Caspian Explorer, Corsair and Kestrel Mk II are in the ship data and not yet in the id list, so nothing can key them to a journal symbol. Their names are carried into the shipped table anyway, so d47 says "that is a ship I know of and have no figures for" rather than "I have never heard of it" — the difference between a table that is stale and one that is wrong. **The failure this is shaped to avoid showed up in the sources themselves:** `outfitting.csv` calls both `int_hyperdrive_size8_class5` and `int_hyperdrive_overcharge_size8_class5` a "Frame Shift Drive", which would have put three different 8A drives in the table under one name — two lines that look identical and carry different numbers. Colliding rows now take a qualifier derived from the symbol rather than a guessed one, and Frontier's `int_missing_*` placeholders are dropped, because a "0Z Frame Shift Drive" in a list of the sizes a drive comes in is a lie about the game. Modules are keyed by the symbol `Loadout` writes, so "what have I got fitted" and "what is that thing" are one lookup; a name with no size returns the sizes that exist rather than picking one, since 35 drives differ by an order of magnitude in every figure worth quoting. No setting: nothing leaves the machine, and a row that protects nothing is a row a Commander has to read and decide about for no reason.
  - [x] Engineers - Who unlocks what (and who in the chain of unlocks), current unlock status, where each engineer is, and what they grade. Two halves that only mean something together: a shipped directory and the Commander's own standing. "Who grades frame shift drives" has one answer for everybody and "who can grade *mine*" has one answer for this Commander, so both arrive together — the best engineer for a job and the best one reachable today are frequently different people, and an answer giving only the first sends you somewhere you cannot go. `tools/gen-engineers.py` derives the directory from three sources, each the authority on exactly one thing: FDevIDs `engineers.csv` for the id the journal writes plus the id64 and market id of where they work; spansh.co.uk to turn those two ids into a system and a station name, **at generation time**, so asking "where is Farseer" at the controls reaches nothing at all; and EDEngineer's MIT blueprint list for what they grade and to what. Standings are keyed by id, never by name. **`EngineerProgress` comes in two shapes and they mean different things** — a startup event carrying an `Engineers` array is a complete snapshot, and an in-play one naming a single engineer is a delta — so the array replaces and the single merges; treating the second as a snapshot would wipe the other thirty-seven the first time somebody ranked up. **The chain of unlocks ships, and the reason it did not for a while turned out to be wrong.** No referral graph is in FDevIDs, coriolis-data or EDEngineer, whose ship `Unlock` rows name only the engineer being unlocked — and from that it was concluded that no source had one. **The wiki has had it all along**, as a collapsed table covering ship and on-foot engineers alike, on the same page as the roll table, behind the same 402 that hid that. So has the price of rank: reputation rises by buying modifications or by selling exploration data and commodities at the workshop, at 500,000 credits of net profit for grade 2 rising to 16,000,000 for grade 5. Deliberately **not transcribed from a scrape** — the table expresses a tree through row and column spans and the flattened text is ambiguous in at least one place, and a referral graph whose failure mode is grinding the wrong engineer for a fortnight deserves a careful read rather than a fast one. It is wiki knowledge, and writing it from memory is the invented game data every table in this repo exists to avoid; a wrong unlock requirement costs a Commander a trip. **And it no longer has to be a scrape at all.** EDDiscovery's `EliteDangerousCore` is Apache-2.0 and carries the graph machine-readably for all 38 — the referring engineer *and* the grade needed with them — so the join against the wiki's rendered per-engineer table is the check rather than the transcription. They agree on **37 of 38**; the Marco Qwent ambiguity was an artefact of the collapsed table and both say Elvira Martuuk; and the one conflict, Bill Turner, is decided by journal in favour of the wiki. The threshold both state is worth carrying into the answer, because it is a real ordering constraint rather than a footnote: an engineer names their network only at **grade 3 with them plus roughly half the bar to grade 4**. Two sourced things stood in its place while it was missing, and both still stand beside it: the **tribute** for the 26 engineers whose invitation is a delivery, which the blueprint list models as a blueprint whose ingredients are what they want; and the **chain as observed** — an engineer who has invited the Commander is a referral that has already happened, and the journal says so, which is the same information from the one source that cannot be wrong about it. Four buckets account for every engineer, including "heard of, no invitation yet", because a report whose job is to add up must not let one person vanish. The two id lists spell one man differently — `Tod 'The Blaster' McQuinn` against `Tod McQuinn` — and without normalising the quoted nickname away his whole speciality list is silently lost, which reads as an engineer who grades nothing; the same rule now works at runtime, so a Commander can ask for him by the name they say. The chain itself landed later, in Phase 14's `#102` plan: `find_engineer` now names who refers an engineer and at what grade, where the Commander stands on that path, and the published price of the rank they lack.
  - [x] #102 Know what engineering actually does - Blueprint and experimental effects, what a roll costs, and what the engineering on this ship actually did. The facts layer; nothing in it needs a plan to exist. **Measured, and it collapses the hard problem into a small one:** an application costs *exactly one* of each ingredient, always — 786 graded blueprints and 1,885 ingredient entries in EDEngineer's list, 926 components in coriolis's, and not one size other than 1. So d47 is not uncertain about ingredients, grades, engineers or effects. It is uncertain about **one integer** — how many applications a grade takes. **And the answer is published in full.** The Type-8 update made rolls deterministic, and Frontier's notes carry the whole table: progress per roll is 20/25/34/50/100% on the difference between the Commander's access level and the recipe's grade, so **rolls needed are 5, 4, 3, 2, 1 on `rank - grade`** and nothing else is involved. Two rules fall out. A total is exactly computable — ingredients × rolls, and d47 already reads `EngineerProgress.Rank` — so this item quotes a **number rather than a floor**, and the Commander's `EngineerCraft` history becomes corroboration rather than the only source of one. And **grade N is unreachable below rank N**: a grade 5 blueprint cannot be rolled at rank 4 at all, which turns a material shortfall into an ordering problem no amount of gathering fixes. Both pages state this and both refuse an automated fetch — 403 and 402 — which is the only reason the first pass concluded the number did not exist. `ShipModule` currently drops `Engineering.Quality`, `Modifiers` and the engineer, which is why "how good is my roll" is unanswerable today and why "from where this module *is*" cannot even be expressed; `Modifiers` is the only place the actual roll exists in real units, because a blueprint table gives the grade's span and not this module's result. **`Quality` is now measured rather than guessed at, across 6,272 real rolls, and it is a cumulative fill** — 1,609 of 1,609 multi-roll runs non-decreasing. Two corrections fall out and both change what d47 should say. Frontier's published 34% is **`1/3` in the game**, so three rolls sum to 0.99 rather than overshooting; and **a grade completes at 0.85, not 1.0** — of 68 grades left short, the 45 the game let a Commander move on from all sat at 0.85, 0.95 or 0.99, and the 23 genuinely abandoned all sat at 0.8 or below. So `Quality == 1.0` is the wrong test for done, and it fails in exactly the direction that tells a Commander their finished module is unfinished. **`Level` is the fact** — it names the grade the module has — and `Quality` is progress within it, quoted as rolls remaining only below the band. Findings in [docs/spikes/journal-corpus-engineering.md](docs/spikes/journal-corpus-engineering.md). Two generated tables in the established shape — `Materials.tsv` (symbol, name, **ledger**, category, grade, line, origins) and `Blueprints.tsv` (per-application ingredients, grades, engineers, effects, experimentals) — from EDEngineer joined against FDevIDs, with the join as the check. Three traps that live in the sources rather than in the code: keying materials on EDEngineer's `FormattedName` **silently loses 22 of the 45 Encoded materials** (`anomalousbulkscandata` against the journal's `bulkscandata`, and 21 more by the same rule), which surfaces as a shortfall the Commander does not have in the category that is already worst to gather; experimentals must come from EDEngineer because **11 of 62 effects have a different recipe per module type** and coriolis models one recipe per effect, which is structurally wrong rather than merely stale; and 27 blueprint plus 11 experimental cross-source disagreements are printed by the generator rather than silently resolved. The planning half — a target, what it costs and how far along it is — is **Phase 17**, where per-ship checklists already live and where the ship's AI proposing a completed item was already ruled on. Findings in [docs/spikes/engineering-data-sources.md](docs/spikes/engineering-data-sources.md).
  - [x] Spike: what Operations pays out, and what a pre-engineered module looks like - **Read at source since**: Merc Coin caps at 9999 with a 1,000 weekly allowance, and buys "new special ship modules, new blueprints to engineer them further, and exclusive on-foot gear mods that are otherwise difficult to acquire in the galaxy". Note what that does *not* say — the word **pre-engineered came from a community write-up, not from Frontier**, and the wiki's own pre-engineered section describes community goal and tech broker modules without mentioning Merc Coin at all. If those modules do arrive already modified they break the "unmodified → blueprint → grade" model everything else assumes, and their blueprints will not be in the community datasets yet — the same lag that already left Caspian Explorer, Corsair and Kestrel Mk II in the specification table with a name and no figures. Answer four things from the journal, which is the one source that cannot be wrong about it — what event records the purchase; whether such a module arrives carrying an `Engineering` block and what `BlueprintName` it names; whether that blueprint appears in FDevIDs `outfitting.csv` or EDEngineer's list; and whether it can be engineered further on top. The answer decides whether a build intent may name one at all, and whether the blueprint table needs a second source. Throwaway probe under `spike/`, finding written to `docs/spikes/`, per `spike/README.md`. **Answered 2026-08-16, and no probe was needed — the reason both questions were open was wrong.** The locker cap is **1,000 per category**: `ShipLocker.json` is indeed a state file, but Elite *also* writes a `ShipLocker` **event carrying the whole locker**, 28,148 times in the very corpus this item recorded as unable to help. Components total exactly 1,000 in 7,931 of them and never once more, while the largest single component ever held is 94 — which refutes a per-item cap outright. Consumables carry a separate per-item cap of 100. And `BarterCost`/`BarterValue` compose exactly (see *Go and get it*). **Two answers nobody asked for, and both changed the phase:** the credit cost of an upgrade is a fixed multiplier on the base price, and **EDEngineer's on-foot recipes are pre-patch by factors of two and three** — which is the one thing this phase was most confident about and would have shipped wrong. Findings in [docs/spikes/journal-corpus-on-foot.md](docs/spikes/journal-corpus-on-foot.md). **Answered 2026-08-16** in [docs/spikes/colonisation-sources.md](docs/spikes/colonisation-sources.md): the depot event is a **snapshot**, not a delta, so tracking is subtraction over one event and needs no source at all; **several sites can be open at once**; a **claim carries no expiry and no service holds one**; and **no licence-clean facility table exists** — every rendering of the figures is GPL-3.0 or an unlicensed spreadsheet, so the planning half does not ship as a costed table. **Answered 2026-08-15 against 912 journals — [operations-pre-engineered.md](docs/spikes/operations-pre-engineered.md).** The premise survives and the blueprint table is what breaks. **They are community goal rewards**: the module appears in `StoredModules` at the awarding station at `BuyPrice: 0`, **32 seconds** after `CommunityGoalReward` — which the wiki line in this very item predicted and the first pass failed to search for. No *module* event carries engineering at acquisition, though: an `Engineering` block appears on `Loadout` and nowhere else, across 692,631 events. The marker is **`EngineerID: 399999` with no `Engineer` name**, 320 of 20,526 blocks — and it means *engineer not recorded*, which is a superset of pre-engineered rather than a synonym: `CargoRack_IncreasedCapacity` carries it 294 times and was never once crafted, while `PowerDistributor_PrioritySystems` carries it on one module and was rolled by The Dweller on another. `Quality` is **not** a marker — 19,609 named-engineer blocks also read exactly 1.0. EDEngineer, d47's authority, has **no cargo rack blueprint under any name**; coriolis has the rack with one grade, no components and its own module name, which is the structural signature. **A build intent must not name one because there is nowhere to get one** — and that scoping is what makes the rest moot: a module nobody can buy is met in only two states, already stored or already fitted, and both are answered from the journal without the tables. The outfitting stock list is three fields — `Name`, `BuyPrice`, `id` — with no field in which engineering could be expressed, so what a station sells is always the plain module; note the *symbol* is purchasable even though the module is not. **And "exactly two names" is the wrong frame**: the class grows with each goal, so an unknown blueprint is a permanent condition rather than a special case. It is detectable rather than enumerable — exactly **one of coriolis's 81 blueprints has no ingredients at any grade**, and it is this rack, so "not something you make" is readable from the shape of the data. The **module** lag is the live one: FDevIDs and `EliteSpecifications.tsv` have never heard of `int_fighterbaymk2_size5_class1`, so a Fighter Hangar Mk II falls back to its symbol today, exactly as armour did before Step 6 closed it. **Merc Coin appears nowhere in the corpus** — this Commander has not done Operations — so it was read at source instead: it buys **the same modules the goals hand out**, offered to Commanders who missed them. Two routes, one blueprint, and the detector covers both. The community's **+34.4%** for the rack matches coriolis's `cargo: [0.344, 0.344]` exactly, from an unrelated source, and it settles the fourth question the other way the corpus could not: cargo racks cannot be engineered normally and have no experimental to receive. And the probe found unclaimed ground: `StoredModules`, `ModuleStore` and `ModuleRetrieve` carry `EngineerModifications`, `Level` and `Quality` on 24,451 entries, so "which of my stored modules are engineered" is already answerable.
  - [x] #102 Go and get it - Where the materials are, and the trip that collects them. Mostly reuse of what Phase 14 already shipped. **The sourcing table exists and nobody expected it to:** EDEngineer's `entryData.json` carries `OriginDetails` on 365 of 371 entries across 77 distinct origin strings, including `Signal source (High grade emissions, Boom)` — and spansh's system index has a `state` filter whose values are exactly those states, so grade-5 Manufactured sourcing is end-to-end derivable from two sources d47 already uses for the price of one new filter row. State turns over on the BGS tick and the index is a snapshot, so the sentence is "systems *reported* in Boom", carrying the same crowd-report framing `StockLastSeen` already does. Raw materials come from `find_body` with a **fourth** member of the silently-ignored group-filter family: `{"materials":{"name":{"value":["Yttrium"]}}}` returned 152 landable bodies within 20 ly of Sol where the obvious flat spelling returned the unfiltered 703. Share is neither filterable nor sortable and arrives on every result, so the ranking is **local** and the answer says what it ranked over — the best of what was fetched, never the best in the galaxy. Rhenium, Lead and Boron are absent from the index entirely and must be **declined by name**, because a search that returns nothing reads as "there is none near you", which is a wrong answer that looks like a right one. Material traders need the `services` group filter (the flat shape returned the unfiltered 10,000, identically to a bogus service name), and their *type* comes from a heuristic labelled as one — FDevIDs' own rule read literally classifies 152 of 200 real traders as Raw — overridden by `MaterialTrade.TraderType`, which is ground truth per market id. **The trade rate is published *and now measured*, so netting a surplus against a shortfall is arithmetic rather than a guess:** one grade down returns 1 to 3, one grade up costs 6 to 1, a different line costs another 6 to 1, and combinations multiply — confirmed exactly across 1,096 real trades. **The published wording contains a trap worth naming here**, because getting it wrong is silent: the extra 6× is described as applying to a different *category*, but "category" in the journal names the Raw/Manufactured/Encoded type, and **no trade crosses those — zero in 1,096**. The 6× applies across material **lines** within one type, so reading `Category` off the journal to price a trade computes the wrong rate for the commonest trade there is. That makes the `line` column load-bearing rather than decorative, and a table without it cannot price a trade at all. Two things fall out of that. **A cross-*type* surplus is still worth nothing** — each trader deals in Raw, Manufactured or Encoded and only one of them — and **some defined trades are physically impossible**, because the wiki's own table marks the conversions whose input exceeds the storage cap, which `MaterialGrades.CapacityOfGrade` can already check. And the trader's *location* rule is published too, which retires the economy heuristic entirely: Raw sits in Refinery and Extraction, Manufactured in Extraction and Industrial, Encoded in High Tech and Military, with medium or high security, a population between one and twenty-two million, and no anarchy control. Every one of those is already a filter d47 has, so the right implementation is a filtered system search rather than a rule nobody could make behave. Batching is the real ask and it is a set cover over origins across every active plan rather than a per-material lookup. And help should **speak up** — arriving at an engineer, or picking up the last unit of something a plan needed, are moments rather than questions, and the callout engine already reads `StationServices` for engineering. Findings in [docs/spikes/engineering-data-sources.md](docs/spikes/engineering-data-sources.md). **Shipped 2026-08-15**, except for the two halves that need a *plan* to exist and therefore land in Phase 17: batching as a set cover “across every active plan”, and speaking up when the last unit a plan needed is picked up. The other half of speaking up was already there — `ArrivalCallout` announces engineering on arrival from `StationServices`, which is why no table of engineer bases was ever needed. **Both landed on 2026-08-16 with Phase 17**, as the plan-wide costing and the `checklist` callout.
  - [x] Know the current community goals - Active community goals and their tiers. CGs the commander is not currently participating in can only be surfaced if an INARA API Key is available and correct. **The split this item was written around is not the split the journal makes**, and the corpus said so: `CommunityGoal` fires off the board *at a station*, so it already carries goals the Commander merely docked near — 952 of 16,999 entries across 912 journals report a contribution of zero. What no journal can carry is a goal running somewhere they have not been, which came to ten goals in thirteen months. So the key buys **everywhere I have not been**, which is wider than "everything I have not joined", and that is the wording the settings row, the disclosure and the answer all use. One event carries the whole board — `TierReached`, `TopTier`, `PlayerPercentileBand`, `PlayerInTopRank`, `NumContributors`, `CurrentTotal`, `Bonus` — so the tiers this item asks for were on disk the whole time. **It is a board snapshot rather than a list of live goals**, and the corpus holds one reported four days after its goal expired, carrying `IsComplete: true`; the event fires on every dock, so the stale entry is the common case rather than the edge one, and every goal is checked against injected time with an expired one saying when it ended in the same breath as its name. `CurrentGoals` **merges by `CGID` rather than replacing**: it is the board at one station and no station in the corpus ran two goals at once, so replacing on an untested assumption loses a goal the Commander is running, while merging keeps one that has ended — which the expiry check already handles. Signing up and being paid come from three events carrying only `CGID`, `Name` and `System`, so they are merged onto the entry and survive the next board; taking a fresh read's defaults would un-join a goal every time the Commander docked. Three things the INARA half needed, none of them guessable. **The listing carries no `CGID`** — a name, a system, a station, two tiers, two totals and the GalNet copy, and no id — so the only field the two sources can be matched on is the name, which is exactly the field two sources spell differently; they merge only on an exact match and anything else appears twice, because a duplicate is visible and a wrong merge is silent. **`tierMax` is 0 when unknown**, since the journals carry no maximum tier and a journal-sourced entry reads zero, which announces a goal whose top tier is zero. And **HTTP 200 is not a success** — a rejected key, a malformed request and "no results" all arrive as 200 with the real status inside the body, so reading the transport code reports a bad key as an empty board, the one wrong answer here that looks exactly like a right one. The goal's GalNet article, several hundred words apiece in `goalDescriptionText`, is dropped at the seam rather than trimmed: it is the largest piece of untrusted third-party text d47 could put in front of a model, and nobody asked for it. **There is no on/off toggle, and that is deliberate** — every other third-party destination needs one because a fresh install could otherwise reach it unasked, and this one cannot be reached at all until somebody pastes in a credential, so the key is the switch and clearing it is how it is turned off; the tool still answers from the journal either way, which is what keeps a missing key a state rather than a guard. Findings in [docs/spikes/community-goals.md](docs/spikes/community-goals.md).
  - [x] Web Search - Live web search as a tool, when determined necessary by the LLM or when specifically asked to by the commander. **A sequencing dependency for the engineering items above**, not merely a nice one: the build target comes from a conversation with the ship's AI, and the AI reaching the community's zeitgeist is what makes that conversation worth having rather than a survey of what d47 happens to ship a table for. It is also the honest escape hatch for the things engineering help refuses to assert — what a Crystalline Shard site yields, which settlement type drops which component — where the rule is that the result is a sentence in the turn, spoken as a search result, and **never a row written into a shipped table**. *"How many applications a grade really takes" was the third example here and is no longer one:* it is published, and now measured across 6,272 rolls, which is a fair illustration of why this item is an escape hatch rather than a source — the list of things d47 cannot assert is shorter than it looks, and shrinks every time somebody checks. Untrusted input like every other outside source. **Built as the provider's own server-side tool, not a tool of d47's**, which decides the boundary structurally rather than by convention: the model asks, the provider searches, and what reaches d47 is prose in the turn — there is no code path by which a search result could arrive as a row, because d47 never receives one. It also needs no key, opens no new destination, and costs nothing in prompt position 1, so the tool-profile valve is untouched. The price is that it is off on a non-default endpoint, which is capabilities-as-state. **Two defects it surfaced that predate it:** `pause_turn` had no case and fell through to "completed", so a turn cut short would have been reported as a finished answer; and the per-turn usage accumulator rebuilds its record positionally, so the new search count was silently dropped and searches were billed as free until a test priced them.

- [x] **Phase 15 — Warnings that arrive in time**
  - [x] Warn about an attack that has been announced but not yet landed - NPC comms carry the intent before the shooting starts, as localisation ids rather than prose, so this is an allowlist of ids and never a keyword match on text. Measured over 912 real journals: `$Pirate_StartInterdiction*` precedes an attack 88% of the time, `$Pirate_OnDeclarePiracyAttack*` 66%, `$BountyHunter_StartInterdiction*` 1 for 1, with a median of 10 seconds' warning and 315 of 406 inside 30 seconds - enough to boost, deploy hardpoints or high-wake. The allowlist is what makes it work: `$Trader_OnEnemyShipDetection*` fires 2,399 times at 1%, and `$HostileScan*` 48 times at 0%, so anything matching on "sounds hostile" cries wolf a hundred times per real event. Each id group gets its own spoken line and its own alert cue rather than one generic warning, because the game has already told us which situation it is. In-game comms are untrusted input, so the match is a local comparison against a fixed set and the message text never reaches the model.
  - [x] Warn that you are exposed in a rival Power's territory - In normal space, in a system whose `ControllingPower` is not the Power you are pledged to. A standing condition rather than an event: it announces on entering the condition and is silent while it holds. Deliberately not on arrival, because arriving is a supercruise state and nothing can reach you there - measured at 0% across every Power security contact in supercruise, against 67% in normal space. Suppressed while an attack is already in play: "be careful, you are in enemy territory" arriving as a Thargoid or a pirate opens fire is worse than silence, so anything on the danger path outranks it. The Commander's pledge is tracked as history from the `Powerplay` event rather than read once, because it changes. **What this cannot be:** the thing actually wanted here is a warning when a Power Security ship appears in the contacts panel, and no third-party app can see that - across 221 event types Elite has ever written there is no contact, spawn or proximity event, no state file holds a contacts list, and every signal about another ship (`ShipTargeted`, `ReceiveText`, `Scanned`, `UnderAttack`) requires it to have already acted. `$PowersSecurity_OnAttackStart*` is real and names the Power, but arrives 0-4 seconds before the shot, so it is confirmation rather than warning. This item is the reachable half and should say what it knows - that you are exposed - rather than implying something is near.

- [x] **Phase 16 — Before the rest of it**
  - [x] Spike: what can be known about exobiology before you land - Four questions, and three of them decide whether the items below can promise anything. **Is there an exobiology route planner**, and if so what does it send — spansh has no published API, so the endpoint and its request shape are established against the live service and recorded, as `SpanshRequest` already does for the other four plot types. **Is there a licence-clean, machine-readable table of what conditions each genus needs** — atmosphere, pressure, temperature, gravity, volcanism — or is that prose in forum guides; EDDiscovery's `EliteDangerousCore` is Apache-2.0 and already vetted here, so it is the first place to look, and a null result sends the whole prediction half to web search rather than to a table. **Does the mass code correlate with what actually pays**, measured against this Commander's own `ScanOrganic` history joined to the system name at the time — the one question no source can answer and the strongest reason this spike exists, because "more mass, more Stratum" is currently folklore and shipping folklore as a heuristic sends a Commander the wrong way with confidence. And **what the first-footfall multiplier actually is**, because it lands in a payout figure and reciting it from memory is exactly the invented game data every table in this repo exists to avoid. Two smaller ones ride along: whether `Status.json`'s `GuiFocus` distinguishes the DSS panel, and how closely a `ScanOrganic` line tracks the position `Status.json` held at that moment, since the second is what the spacing figure rests on. Throwaway probe under `spike/`, finding written to `docs/spikes/`, per `spike/README.md`. **Answered 2026-08-16, and no probe was needed — the reason both questions were open was wrong.** The locker cap is **1,000 per category**: `ShipLocker.json` is indeed a state file, but Elite *also* writes a `ShipLocker` **event carrying the whole locker**, 28,148 times in the very corpus this item recorded as unable to help. Components total exactly 1,000 in 7,931 of them and never once more, while the largest single component ever held is 94 — which refutes a per-item cap outright. Consumables carry a separate per-item cap of 100. And `BarterCost`/`BarterValue` compose exactly (see *Go and get it*). **Two answers nobody asked for, and both changed the phase:** the credit cost of an upgrade is a fixed multiplier on the base price, and **EDEngineer's on-foot recipes are pre-patch by factors of two and three** — which is the one thing this phase was most confident about and would have shipped wrong. Findings in [docs/spikes/journal-corpus-on-foot.md](docs/spikes/journal-corpus-on-foot.md). **Answered 2026-08-16** in [docs/spikes/exobiology-sources.md](docs/spikes/exobiology-sources.md): the **route planner exists** at `api/exobiology/route` and returns per-body species with counts and values, with `from` echoed back as `source`; the **first-footfall bonus is exactly 4x the value**, so 5x total, over 79 rows with no variance; **`GuiFocus` 10 is the DSS**; **`ScanOrganic` carries no position at all**, so sample spacing needs live `Status.json` pairing and cannot be backfilled; EDDiscovery has **no genus conditions table**, though spansh narrows that to the first-footfall case; and the **mass code question is not answerable from this corpus** - 374 scans, mass code `b` at n=5, and a Commander's own route choices are not a sample of the galaxy - so the heuristic does not ship.
  - [x] Spike: what is already known about colonisation, and by whom - **The community has built this three times over, and Frontier published the rules**, so the question is which of it is data and which is prose. Read Frontier's own System Colonisation Guide first: it states the mechanics outright — a claim lasts 24 hours, facilities move population, standard of living and tech level, and a starport specialisation decides the economy it produces, Commercial giving a Colony economy and Industrial raising tech. That is a primary source and it outranks everything below it. Then establish whether a **licence-clean, machine-readable table of facility costs, effects and link rules** exists inside `gaborauth/ed-colonisation-planner`, which models station service activation, or EDSC, or Raven Colonial — the same question EDEngineer and coriolis answered for blueprints, and the same rule applies: a source's own licence is checked and never mistaken for permission over Frontier's figures. **If it is prose rather than data, the planning half does not ship as a table at all** and the strategy advice moves to web search. Three journal questions ride along, none of them measured here yet: what `ColonisationConstructionDepot` carries and whether its `ResourcesRequired` is a snapshot or a delta — the trap that has now caught `EngineerProgress` and `StoredModules` and is silent both times — whether it fires only while docked at the site, and whether more than one site can be active at once. And one that decides what the item below may promise: **whether a claim is visible to anybody outside the game**, because a 24-hour claim is live state that no crowd-fed index will hold. Throwaway probe under `spike/`, finding written to `docs/spikes/`, per `spike/README.md`. **Answered 2026-08-16, and no probe was needed — the reason both questions were open was wrong.** The locker cap is **1,000 per category**: `ShipLocker.json` is indeed a state file, but Elite *also* writes a `ShipLocker` **event carrying the whole locker**, 28,148 times in the very corpus this item recorded as unable to help. Components total exactly 1,000 in 7,931 of them and never once more, while the largest single component ever held is 94 — which refutes a per-item cap outright. Consumables carry a separate per-item cap of 100. And `BarterCost`/`BarterValue` compose exactly (see *Go and get it*). **Two answers nobody asked for, and both changed the phase:** the credit cost of an upgrade is a fixed multiplier on the base price, and **EDEngineer's on-foot recipes are pre-patch by factors of two and three** — which is the one thing this phase was most confident about and would have shipped wrong. Findings in [docs/spikes/journal-corpus-on-foot.md](docs/spikes/journal-corpus-on-foot.md).
  - [x] Ask for the keys on the first run that needs them - A fresh install has no language-model key, so the first thing a Commander does is find the right row in a surface with fourteen sections. This is the guided path: the language-model key, the voice key as optional, and INARA when that arrives. **Driven by state, never by a "have we done this?" flag** - the condition is *there is no usable language-model key*, which is also true for a Commander who restored a `data\` folder onto a new machine, because `secrets.json` is DPAPI-scoped and `SecretStore` treats an undecryptable value as absent and says so in the log. A flag would show this once on a machine that could not read its own secrets and never again. Reuses the existing secret rows rather than re-authoring them: the settings surface is descriptor-driven, and a parallel copy of these three fields is a second thing to keep in step and the one that drifts.
