What leaves the machine per key is **computed from `EgressDisclosure`, not written by hand here**, for the same reason the privacy section already computes it - hand-written copy goes stale and this is the one place a Commander is deciding whether to trust the thing. Not a wall: capabilities are state rather than guards (Phase 3), so declining every key leaves d47 running and typed rather than a dead app behind a form, and skipping the optional ones must not dead-end. A key is **verified, not merely stored** - the row makes the real call and says whether it worked, because a key that is wrong, revoked or pasted with a trailing newline is otherwise indistinguishable from a key that works until the first turn fails; and this is where `AppHost`'s known fault lives, where a voice list did not refetch when the key arrived, so entering a key has to take effect without a restart. Paste-friendly and trimmed, masked with a reveal, and **never logged at any level**. Re-openable from About afterwards, since keys get rotated and revoked and the state that triggers this is a state a working install can return to.

- [x] **Phase 17 — Checklists**
  - [x] TheApp keeps "The Ultimate" checklist - CRUD + complete/uncomplete and read checklist items by voice, with the panel reflecting changes live. Commander-authored, mutable, list-shaped state, which this repo has exactly one precedent for: `MacroStore` — a file under `data/`, polled on its write time so a hand edit is live with no restart, saved through `AtomicFile`, and **problems reported rather than items silently dropped**, because a checklist that quietly loses a line is worse than one that refuses it out loud. Nothing persisted today is keyed per Commander and a checklist has to be; the key goes **inside** the document rather than into a path, because the Frontier id comes out of the journal and journal content is untrusted input — turning it into a filename buys a path-traversal surface for an organisational convenience. **One surface, two kinds, three groups.** "What am I working on" must have exactly one answer, so this panel is where the derived lists show up too — a ship's build, a system's construction — rather than each growing a surface of its own. What stays separate underneath is **how an item's "done" is decided**, and it is a property of where the item came from rather than something the Commander picks: an *authored* item is a sentence nobody can compute, which is why marking it complete needs a person (and why the LLM item below exists at all), while a *derived* one is a diff against live journal state. Mixing those in one bucket gives a list where some ticks are computed and some are opinions with nothing on screen saying which — the failure this repo already names elsewhere, that a list whose entries mean different things depending on a flag is a list that gets read wrong. So a derived item **cannot be ticked by hand at all**, and trying says why rather than quietly doing nothing, because the next journal read would either undo it or leave it standing and lying. Grouping is a third axis and orthogonal to both: universal, this ship, this system. Derived items belong where they came from; authored ones file anywhere, which is what lets "ask Jim about the Krait build" sit beside the Krait's plan. **Completing is not removing** — a finished item stays, checked, because on something that runs for weeks seeing how far you have come is most of the point, so completed items sit below the line or collapsed with their count visible rather than burying the six that are still open. Deleting is changing your mind, which is a different act and can happen to an item whether or not it was ever finished. Filters are **generated from the three axes rather than hand-listed** — kind, source, state — so authored, ship, colonisation, complete and open all exist today and a fourth kind of plan appears in the row without anybody remembering to add it, the same way the settings surface is a projection of the capability registry. **Shipped 2026-08-16.** Item identity is `(scope, key)` and the key is content-addressed on the intent, never on position — and the separator is where it nearly went wrong: `MainEngines` and "main engines" slugged differently, so a plan restated in a differently-worded conversation would have read as every item tombstoned and an identical set opened. Keys therefore compare with separators stripped, and a spoken slot is resolved against the live `Loadout` first, so "thrusters" and `MainEngines` are one item. An authored key is the lowest unused integer in its scope, counting tombstones: deterministic, clock-free, and it survives an edit to the wording, which a key derived from the text would not. One correction the tests forced: **a line written before any journal has been read has no Frontier id**, and refusing it would silently lose exactly the note somebody jots while Elite is still loading — so it lands in an unowned document and is adopted by the first Commander to appear.
  - [x] Per ship build planning/tracking - Checklists that belong to a specific ship and follow it, instead of the universal checklist. **This is where #102's planning half lands.** The shape it needs is a list of independently abandonable *intents* rather than a target loadout: a Krait has around twenty slots and a conversation about a build produces opinions about six, so a target loadout means the model inventing the other fourteen and then reporting "6 of 20" where the honest number is "6 of 9 things you asked for". Null on an intent means **wildcard, not unknown**, so "grade 5 dirty drives and I don't care which thrusters" is expressible rather than permanently unmeetable; abandoning leaves a tombstone rather than deleting, because "why did you stop tracking the shield boosters" is a real question three weeks later; and **no number is ever stored** — every figure is recomputed from a table with a generator behind it, since a stored number gets recited in October with nothing left to say where it came from. Keyed on the Commander's Frontier id and the journal's `ShipID`, carrying the hull as well, so a `ShipID` that now reports a different hull makes the list **stale and says so** rather than quietly diffing an exploration Krait against a Cutter. Progress is a diff against the live `Loadout`, so nobody ever types in what they have already done — and **`Elsewhere` is its own state**, because "you own that already, it is in Deciat, and moving it costs 2.1 million" is a completely different next action from "go and grind it", which `get_stored_modules` already carries the transfer cost to say. **No longer waits on the `Engineering.Quality` spike — it is answered.** `Quality` is a cumulative fill, and the grade completes at **0.85 rather than 1.0**, measured across 6,272 rolls. That matters here more than anywhere else in the repo, because this is the item that gates "done": testing `Quality == 1.0` produces precisely the module a Commander can see is finished and d47 will not call finished, and it would have passed testing, since 93% of grades do reach 1.0 and the bug lives in the last 7%. So `Level` decides which grade is held, the 0.85 band decides whether that grade is complete, and rolls-remaining is quoted only below it — carried with its sample size, because 0.85 is the lowest *observed* completion rather than a proven threshold. **Changing the plan is a supported operation, and it is a diff rather than a rebuild.** Burst lasers instead of multi-cannons is a revision, not a failure, and it has to be told apart from the world moving underneath a plan that did not change — selling the engineered multi-cannon un-completes an item and d47 says so once, because a computed tick going backwards is something the Commander wants to know rather than a glitch to hide. A revision keeps the history of everything present in both versions, tombstones what the new plan drops and opens what it adds; rebuilding the list from scratch would wipe a fortnight of progress the first time somebody changed one weapon, which is the failure that would quietly kill "see how far you have come". That makes **item identity the load-bearing decision** — two plans can only be diffed if an item knows what it is independently of its position in a list, which is the slot plus the intent here and the body or orbital slot plus the facility type for a system. Get that key wrong and every revision reads as everything removed and everything added. One case decides whether the history tells the truth: an item **completed and then designed out** tombstones as done-then-superseded rather than vanishing, because the Commander really did spend that fortnight. **Shipped 2026-08-16, with one honest gap.** Progress diffs against the live `Loadout`; `Elsewhere`, `Blocked` and `Stale` are their own states, and 0.85 rather than 1.0 decides a finished grade. But **the blueprint's *name* cannot be checked at all**: Elite writes `Engine_Dirty` and the shipped table says "Dirty Drive Tuning", and nothing d47 ships carries both spellings — not the four TSVs, not `MaterialGrades.g.cs`, not FDevIDs. So the comparison answers true or *cannot say*, never false, because guessing false tells a Commander their finished module is unfinished. That becomes `Unverified` and a sentence naming both spellings, said once. A plan built from the ship itself carries the journal's symbol and confirms exactly; an experimental effect confirms exactly too, because that is the one name Elite localises. Where the looking stopped, and the one place nobody opened, is in [docs/spikes/blueprint-name-join.md](docs/spikes/blueprint-name-join.md).
  - [x] An engineering plan writes the checklist - The seam between Phase 14's tables and this phase's lists: a build costed into the things a Commander actually has to *do* — unlock this engineer, gather that, fly there, fit this. **A total is exact, and it is a multiplication d47 is entitled to make.** An application costs exactly one of each ingredient (measured across 786 blueprints and 1,885 ingredient entries) and the roll count is a published function of the recipe grade and the Commander's rank with that engineer — 5, 4, 3, 2, 1 as rank exceeds grade. Known unit cost times known count is a real number, so the plan states one. **Rank also gates rather than merely slows:** grade N cannot be rolled below rank N, so an engineer the Commander is rank 3 with is not a slow route to a grade 5 blueprint, it is no route at all, and the plan has to say that instead of listing materials nobody can spend. Caps run **first** and against the exact floor rather than a projection, because `floor > cap` is a flat certainty — at least two trips however the rolls go — while anything above the floor is a possibility and has to read like one. Caps are shared, so two plans that each fit can be jointly impossible, which is precisely the arithmetic nobody can do in their head. Unlock tributes join the same list and **must not join the same ledger**: Meta-alloys are a material, Gold ×200 is two hundred tonnes of cargo, and Opinion Polls ×40 are ship locker, so totalling them together produces a feasibility verdict that is nonsense delivered confidently. **Rank blocks as surely as materials do, and the block now has a price.** Grade N cannot be rolled below rank N, so an engineer at rank 3 is no route at all to a grade 5 blueprint — and reputation is bought at a published rate, 500,000 credits of net profit sold at that workshop for grade 2 rising to 16,000,000 for grade 5. So the plan says what is missing *and* what it costs to clear, rather than naming a blocker and shrugging at it. Findings in [docs/spikes/engineering-data-sources.md](docs/spikes/engineering-data-sources.md). **Shipped 2026-08-16.** Totals are `ingredients × RollsFor(grade, rank)` and exact; a rank that cannot reach the grade lists no materials at all and quotes the reputation price instead; caps are reported first and against that exact total, because `needed > cap` is a flat certainty; and the three ledgers are summed apart. Costing takes **every live plan at once** rather than one, which is also where Phase 14 `#145`'s deferred batching half landed — the origin grouping is greedy and says so rather than claiming a cover nobody computed.
  - [x] A colonisation plan writes the checklist - The same seam as the engineering one, keyed on a **system** instead of a `ShipID`: an objective and a set of facilities costed into the things a Commander actually has to do — claim it, haul that, build this, and in what order, since a facility's effect depends on what is already there and where. Progress is a diff against `ColonisationConstructionDepot` rather than anything typed in, so Phase 18's tracking item is the half that already works and this is the half that knows what it is for. **It rides the same substrate as the ship builds and that is the whole point** — intents rather than a target state, null meaning wildcard, tombstones on abandonment, no number ever stored, revision as a diff. Building a second checklist engine for colonisation would double every hard part in this phase and then let the two drift, so that "what am I working on" has two answers depending on which one is asked. What is genuinely different is only the key and the table behind it, and whether that table exists at all is **Phase 16's** colonisation spike. **Shipped 2026-08-16, as the smaller item the spike left it.** No costed table, no build order — both would need figures nobody publishes licence-clean. A facility item is `Open` until the depot says `ConstructionComplete`, and the hauling items come off the site's own `ResourcesRequired` rather than out of a table. **The `ColonisationConstructionDepot` fold was built here** because the plan needs something to diff against; it is a snapshot, several sites can be open at once, and every figure carries "as of your last visit". Phase 18's tracking item is the reporting surface over that fold and is still open.
  - [x] LLM Ship AI may propose that a checklist item is done - Where the journal can tell, TheApp asks whether a checklist item is complete and marks it only after the Commander agrees. **The distinction that makes this work is between observing and asserting.** A build intent naming a blueprint and a grade is checked against the `Loadout` and the result is simply stated, because that is reading the journal back to the Commander; a checklist line they wrote in their own words is something no table can settle, so d47 proposes and waits. The same rule governs authoring: **proposing is model-callable and committing is not**, into two different files so the trust boundary is inspectable by looking at `data/` — a malformed model write then cannot corrupt the Commander's own list, and the worst a hostile in-game message achieves is a proposal that gets declined. Provenance survives into the wording rather than sitting in a flag nobody reads: what a shipped table confirmed is **asserted**, what a conversation settled on is **quoted**, and the Commander's own note is **attributed**. An item no table covers is kept and marked, never refused — a macro refuses an unknown action because it presses keys and would half-configure a ship, and a checklist line presses nothing — with a *contradicted* one said every time and an *unknown* one said once, since nagging about every line d47 has no table for is how a Commander learns to stop listening. **Shipped 2026-08-16, and it needed one new thing in the capability surface.** Every tool that would change the Commander's list writes `data/checklist-proposals.json`; committing writes `data/checklist.json` and is `ToolDefinition.Protected` — **new, and the tool-level twin of the settings row flag that already existed.** Enforced twice on purpose: `ToolProfiles` leaves it out of the advertisement so no cached prefix pays for a refusal, and `CapabilityRegistry.InvokeAsync` refuses it when the caller is the model, because advertisement alone is a lock on the front door and none on the back. A derived item is never proposed at all — its state is read out of the journal and simply stated, which is the observing-versus-asserting line.

- [x] **Phase 18 — Activity assistants**
  - [x] Read a system name - What the name itself says about a system, computed offline from the string. **This is the only question in the family that does not need somebody else to have been there first**, which is what makes it the answer to "where do I go looking for a first footfall": an index only knows what has already been scanned and uploaded, so the moment a system is in it, it is not undiscovered any more. A procedural name carries a **mass code** — the lone letter before the digits, `Dryafea PO-X `**`d`**`2-0`, running `a` to `h` from least to most massive, with the boxel doubling in size at each step — so d47 can decode a name a Commander is reading off the Galaxy Map, and the one they are standing in, with no network call at all. The ladder of box sizes and the `a`-to-`h` ordering come from a source with its provenance recorded, never from memory. **What the mass code is worth is a separate claim from what it means**, and only the first is settled: whether the heavier boxels really do pay better is measured in the spike above, and if the correlation is not there the feature says what the letter means and stops rather than inventing a strategy on top of it. Star class rides along, since a variant's colour follows the star and the variant is what sets the price. **Shipped 2026-08-16, and the ladder came from the game rather than from a page about it.** A name encodes a boxel index in its letters and boxel number, so regressing that index against real `StarPos` — with each sector's own mean removed, so no sector grid origin has to be assumed — recovers the box size as the slope: **9.99, 20.02, 39.51, 78.23 and 165.32** for `a` through `e` over 2,854 procedurally-named systems, against a nominal 10, 20, 40, 80, 160. The slope is stable across candidate decompositions, which is what makes it a measurement rather than a curve fitted to an assumption. **`f`, `g` and `h` are not measured** — the corpus holds two, one and none — so they rest on the doubling the five rungs establish, closed at the far end by the published rule that `h` is the sector itself, since 10 × 2⁷ is exactly 1,280; community documentation corroborates both ends independently, and **the answer says out loud which rungs are measured and which are not**. The payout heuristic **does not ship and is declined in the answer rather than by omission**, because a Commander asking what the letter means is usually asking exactly that. Star class comes from **`FSDTarget` rather than the arrival scan** — measured at 99.7% of 7,412 arrivals against the auto-scan's 28.6%, the obvious source being the one that is usually not there — and produces no answer at all, rather than a plausible one, for a jump that ended somewhere other than its target or for a name merely read aloud. The grammar was checked against 4,746 real names, 2,854 procedural and 1,892 hand-named, with **0 hand-named names containing a boxel-shaped designator**: a grammar that silently rejects a real name reports "no mass code here", which is a wrong answer wearing the shape of a right one. Findings in [docs/spikes/exobiology-sources.md](docs/spikes/exobiology-sources.md) §8 and §9.
  - [x] Find the exobiology - The known-good half: a circuit, or the next few hops, through systems somebody has already scanned. Mostly reuse — if the planner exists it is a fifth plot type on `IRouteService`, which already carries the submit-queue-poll protocol for neutron, Road to Riches, trade and mining, so this is a day rather than a phase. Beside it, the arithmetic once a body has actually been surface-scanned: **`SAASignalsFound` names the genera outright**, so "is this worth landing on" is a table lookup and a sum rather than a prediction, and the game's own answer outranks any inference d47 could make from `Scan` properties. Prediction is what happens *before* the DSS, and it is only as good as the source the spike finds. Everything sourced here carries its age and its origin the way `StockLastSeen` does — a crowd-fed index is a report about where people have been, not a survey of the galaxy. **Shipped 2026-08-16, and it was the day the spike promised.** The plotter is the fifth plot type on `IRouteService`, `api/exobiology/route`, on the submit-queue-poll protocol the other four already carry; the two traps are that **`from` is required, works, and is echoed back as `source`** — so a caller reconciling its parameters against the echo would "fix" the origin into something genuinely ignored — and that `use_mapping_value` is silently dropped where the Road to Riches plotter honours it. The origin returns as a stop with no bodies, and a loop adds the return leg the same way, so bodyless stops are dropped or "three systems worth landing on" reads as five. **The item's two halves turned out to be two different kinds of claim, and that is the shape it shipped in.** The plotter reads an index that knows *species*, so it quotes figures — taken from the response, never computed, because a Commander's own sales cannot price a species. `SAASignalsFound` names the **genus and never the species** on all 792 events measured, and the species is what sets the price, so the journal half lists what is on the body and **refuses to quote a value at all**. Letting those look like one kind of answer would put a number next to a genus that cannot carry one. And the structural limit is said in the plot's own last line rather than discovered after the flight: **everything an index holds has been visited, so no plotted body can be a first footfall** — which is exactly why *Read a system name* sits beside this rather than under it. A scan that found nothing is worded as its own answer, because "nothing is down there" saves a landing and "I have not looked" does not.
  - [x] Exobiology sampling - Genus, the required sample spacing for that genus, whether the Commander has moved far enough for the next sample, and what has already been scanned on this body. The spacing rule is the whole feature: it is the number nobody can eyeball and the reason a sample gets wasted. **d47 reads no position at all today** — `GameStatus` names the `HasLatLong` flag and parses fuel, cargo, heat, body and balance, and no latitude or longitude, so the plumbing comes first and the body radius comes off `Scan`. **`ScanOrganic` carries no position either**, so d47 stamps one itself from `Status.json` when the event lands, which makes the spacing figure rest on how closely those two writes track each other rather than on the journal alone — measured in the spike rather than assumed. "What has already been scanned here" is the repo's first per-body state that has to outlive a session. **What this cannot be:** reacting to the signal filter the Commander has selected in orbit. `GuiFocus` says which panel is open and `SAASignalsFound` says what the body holds; nothing anywhere says which entry is highlighted inside the panel, and in VR the panel is rendered in world space so there is not even a fixed place on the screen to look. The reachable half is naming the genera the body actually has and asking which one is being hunted — one question, remembered, and it works identically in the headset. **Shipped 2026-08-16, and the two assumptions above about where things live were both wrong in d47's favour.** The position plumbing arrived as specified — `GameStatus` now parses `Latitude`, `Longitude` and `Altitude`, and the spine stamps a fix onto `ScanOrganic`, which carries none on any of the 632 events measured. But **the body radius does not come off `Scan`**: `Status.json` writes `PlanetRadius` beside the position, which matters because taking it from `Scan` would have made the distance uncomputable on any body the Commander had not scanned. Distances are great-circle, so the same angle is a shorter drive on a small moon than on a large planet. And **`Body` is an id rather than a name** — `"Body": 37` — so per-body state is keyed `(SystemAddress, BodyID)`. A run is `Log, Sample, Sample, Analyse` on 94 of 101 runs, which is three specimens and a fourth event that banks them, so `Analyse` completes rather than counting as a fourth. **The spacing rule ships as a measurement rather than as a table.** The required distance is published by the game in the species' own Codex entry and every machine-readable copy outside it is a community wiki — which this phase's plan already ruled on, that what web search finds stays a sentence and the same sentence copied into a shipped table is d47 laundering a forum post into its own voice. So d47 reports the two things it can compute exactly, **how far you moved for that specimen** and **how many you have taken**, and refuses to judge whether it was enough. What it does instead is **learn**: a specimen Elite accepted proves the gap was sufficient, so the smallest accepted gap is an upper bound on the requirement, measured from the Commander's own play and quoted with its sample size — a figure that needs no source at all and improves with use. That bound is deliberately **not** persisted, because rebuilding an inference from stored end points compounds one estimate on another with nothing left to say which samples it rested on. **The per-body state does outlive a session**, as this item asks, in `data/sampling.json` keyed per Commander with the key inside the document — the rule the checklist established, since a Frontier id out of an untrusted journal must not become a filename. It is deliberately far smaller than `ChecklistStore`: that file holds the Commander's own words and reports problems rather than dropping lines, and this is derived state, so a bad file is discarded with a log line and rebuilt by playing. One guard is load-bearing and unmeasurable: **a position read now must never be stamped on an event from two hours ago**, so a fix is used only when the `Status.json` write and the journal line fall within ten seconds of each other — a guard against the startup replay rather than a precision claim, since `Status.json` is overwritten in place and no corpus of journals can contain it.
  - [x] Find somewhere worth colonising - Candidate systems for a stated objective rather than a list of nice ones. **The objective is the input**, because the recurring community finding is that the obvious choice is the wrong one: a Colony primary economy is a poor pick for self-sufficiency, since a colony economy consumes what High Tech and Industrial produce, so a system that feeds itself has to build those in-system. The rest of that archetype — give each economy its own body and its own market rather than stacking them, keep one or two dominant, do not level everything equally — arrives **attributed** rather than in d47's own voice, which puts it in the same place as exobiology terrain lore: web search, or Frontier's guide where they say it themselves, and never a hand-written table. What d47 can assert is the shape of the system, because that is in the index it already queries: does this system hold the bodies an objective needs, how many, and how far apart. **What this cannot be:** telling you a system is unclaimed. A claim lasts 24 hours and lives in Frontier's servers, so the honest sentence is "this system has what your plan wants" and never "this one is free" — subject to the spike, which asks the question properly. **Shipped 2026-08-16, and the spike it was subject to answered every part of it.** The claim rule is checkable and the index will not check it: `population` is a real field whose range filter is *silently dropped* — every spelling returned the unfiltered 51 systems within 15 ly of Sol, where 48 are populated — so the scan **sorts** on population and applies Frontier's rule to what comes back. That measurement removed the `population` filter from `search_systems` as well, where it had done nothing since Phase 14. `is_colonised` and `is_being_colonised` are presence flags whose value is discarded, so asking for "false" returns the sixteen that are true; they are read off the result and never sent, and read **one way only**, since absence is "nothing reported" on 79 of 84 unpopulated systems. **What this cannot be is now a paragraph on every answer**, not a caveat in a document. Body shape comes from the bodies the systems search embeds — which carry no `is_landable` and no `rings` across 2,355 of them — so landability and rings are a second call keyed on `system_name`, exact and case-sensitive, and never the zero-width distance range that looks like the same idea and took 120 seconds to match the whole galaxy. Nothing is scored: filters narrow, distance orders, and the arrival spread is reported because a system whose planets run from 3,958 to 81,811 ls is a different build from one that runs 11 to 3,490. And a fact the item did not anticipate, which the crowd-fed index forces: **a star with no known planets is an unsurveyed system, not a small one** — 23 of the 109 within claim range of Ratraii — so those are counted out loud rather than recommended or quietly dropped, since on a frontier they are often where a Commander would go and look.
  - [x] Colonisation and construction tracking - Commodity requirements and delivered-so-far for an active construction site, sourced from the journal. This is where a large part of the current playerbase spends its hours, and the running arithmetic is exactly what a Commander should not be keeping on paper. **The cheapest item in the phase and the one with no external source at all** — `ColonisationConstructionDepot` carries what is required and what has been provided, so what is left, what is in the hold and what is sitting on the carrier is subtraction over data already on disk. It is the first of the three to ship and waits on no spike, which only sharpens it. The planning half is **Phase 17**, beside the per-ship builds and on the same checklist substrate, because a colonisation build is the same shape of long-lived intent as an engineering one and the second checklist engine is the one nobody should write. **Shipped 2026-08-16**, and the sentence above overpromised by exactly one clause. *What is in the hold* is real and is now read, but **not from the journal** — the `Cargo` event carries its manifest on 1,151 of 13,762 occurrences, essentially the first of each session, so a journal-only reader is right for a minute and stale after it; the manifest comes from `Cargo.json`, which is the seam `Backpack.json` and `ShipLocker.json` already established. *What is sitting on the carrier* **is not subtraction over data on disk and cannot be made into it.** `CarrierStats` publishes a cargo tonnage and Elite writes nothing anywhere saying what those tonnes are; the one derivable model, accumulating `CargoTransfer`, was built and reconciled against that very figure and came out **wrong 679 times against right 347**, driving 11 commodities negative — transfers out of stock the journal never saw arrive, by routes it does not itemise. So d47 quotes the tonnage and declines the manifest out loud, which is the honest half rather than the useful-sounding one. The third measurement is the one that would have failed silently: Elite spells a commodity three ways, and `ColonisationContribution` is mixed case on **30 of 30** symbols against 0 of 31 for the depot and 0 of 64 for the hold, so a fold that strips `$` and `_name;` without lowercasing joins the depot to the hold perfectly and matches no contribution at all — reporting a delivery just completed as never having happened. The same lesson as Phase 17's `MainEngines` against "main engines", found the same way. Every answer carries the freshness caveat rather than a settings page holding it, because a site reports only while docked at it and an exact figure about a moment that has passed is this capability's one way to be wrong while looking right. Findings in [docs/spikes/colonisation-sources.md](docs/spikes/colonisation-sources.md) §7.
  - [x] Spike: does the mass code predict the biology, measured against a denominator - Phase 16 asked this of the Commander's own `ScanOrganic` history and could not answer it, and named the reason it could never be answered that way: **a flight log is a record of where somebody chose to fly**, so a Commander who already believes the folklore produces data that agrees with the belief. This asks it of **bodies rather than routes**. `POST api/bodies/search` filters on `landmark_subtype` and returns, per body, its subtype, atmosphere, gravity, `landmark_value` and — the part that makes it free — `system_name`, which the shipped parser decodes offline. **The design decision that makes it valid is the denominator**: `P(mass code | good biology)` is not `P(good biology | mass code)`, and counting only the good systems would have "discovered" that the common mass codes are the best ones because they are common. So numerator and denominator come out of **one** query stream — every landable body in a ball, landmarks attached — over six references spread across the galaxy, each reported separately as well as pooled so a bubble-only result is visible as one. **Answered 2026-08-16 over 13,000 landable bodies, and the folklore is directionally right about the wrong quantity.** Whether there is biology barely moves — 5.4% to 7.5% across `a` to `d`, no trend. What it is *worth* moves a great deal: the chance of a body paying 5 million or more runs **0.17%, 2.88%, 3.05%, 4.19%**, a **25× rise from `a` to `d`**, and mean credits per landable body runs 79k to 682k, **8.6×**. So *"more mass, more biology"* is unsupported and *"more mass, more valuable biology"* is supported. The mechanism shows in the genus mix rather than the totals — `a` is almost all *Bacterium Acies* where `d` is *Tussock Catena*, *Frutexa Flammasis* and *Stratum Paleas* — which is the point: mass code shapes what bodies and atmospheres generate, those gate genera, and the code is a **proxy that is readable off a name with no network**, which is the one case a proxy earns its keep. Three limits kept with it: it appears to turn over at `e` on 627 bodies and that is the weakest claim, `f` `g` `h` are unmeasurable at 9, 0 and 0 bodies, and the residual bias is upload coverage rather than anybody's beliefs. **The direction is solid and the magnitudes are not yet quotable**, so nothing ships into the ship's AI's mouth on this alone. Findings in [docs/spikes/exobiology-sources.md](docs/spikes/exobiology-sources.md) §10; the same run turned up §11, that `CodexEntry` carries a latitude and longitude on 130 of 354 events where `ScanOrganic` carries none, which re-scopes *Exobiology sampling* before it is built.
  - [x] Prospector and core callouts - Material percentages from prospector limpet results and core asteroid detection, spoken in the ring where mining actually happens. Route planning gets the Commander to the ring; this is the part that happens after they arrive. **Shipped 2026-08-16, and the obvious implementation would have been wrong.** Elite grades every rock `Material Content: Low/Medium/High` and passing that on is the first thing anyone would do — but measured over 1,633 prospects, **`Low` and `High` are the same distribution** (best-material medians of 19.9% and 20.3%, maxima 66.7% and 66.4%) and **45% of the rocks holding a material at 40% or more are graded `Low`**; the corpus has a 58.3% Platinum rock labelled Low. The grade is about the engineering-material fragments a collector limpet picks up rather than the commodity being refined — two questions sharing a word — so the callout **ignores `Content` entirely**, because repeating it flies a Commander past the best rock in the cluster with d47 sounding authoritative. The obvious fallback fails too: **no single percentage is meaningful**, since Platinum's median of 26.6% sits above Water's 90th percentile, so a fixed cut-off is wrong per material and silently so, and no licence-clean table of per-material distributions exists to threshold against. What ships instead is **relative to what the Commander has seen this session** — no source needed, adapts to whatever they are mining, and honest about being relative; the first rock is never a "best" because it is the only one. **Two callouts and two settings rows, on a measured asymmetry**: a prospect arrives every 48 seconds at the median and a core is **3 in 1,633**, so silencing the running commentary must not cost the rare announcement somebody is actually mining for. A core is `Routine` rather than `Urgent` — urgent speaks over the top and is for danger and fuel, and a core announced across a hull warning is the priority backwards. And the identity fold appears for the **third time**: 13 of 27 materials are written both `gallite` and `Gallite` depending on whether Elite wrote a localised name, 40 raw spellings for 27 materials, which would keep two session bests for one material. Findings in [docs/spikes/mining-callouts.md](docs/spikes/mining-callouts.md).

- [x] **Phase 19 — Session tooling and release polish**
  - [x] Remember which voice you chose for each provider - Voice choices are dropped when the speech provider changes, because a voice id means nothing to a provider that did not issue it, so switching to ElevenLabs and back re-picks eleven cores from scratch and forgets the ship's own voice twice. `SpeechSettings.ProviderRates` already keeps the speaking rate per provider and is the shape to copy; voices are a larger structure - the ship's, two named roles and one per core - which is why the fix that stopped the silence dropped them rather than waiting on a schema that can hold all of it twice. **Shipped 2026-08-16.** `SpeechSettings.ProviderVoices` is `ProviderRates`' shape with a record for a value — `VoiceChoices`, holding the ship's, the two named roles', the per-core map and `Paired`. The clearing the old code was named for still happens; only the discarding stopped, so a switch is a stash and a restore rather than a drop. **`Paired` is the field that makes it worth having**: restoring eleven pairings and then letting the pairing pass re-derive them puts the Commander back where they started one launch later. Two edges decided by hand: the provider being switched *to* is removed from the stash as its choices go live, or it would be restored a second time over anything chosen since; and a file written before d47 recorded whose voices these are has nothing to file them under, so those are still dropped rather than filed under a guess — filing them wrong is the failure the whole mechanism exists to stop.
  - [x] Hear a voice before you choose it - The voice picker lists several hundred names and a Commander is picking a character's voice from them, which is a judgement no label supports: "Bill - Wise, Mature, Balanced" and "George - Warm, Captivating Storyteller" are both true and neither tells you which one is Warden. Audition from the dialog: the highlighted voice speaks a line, without closing the dialog and without committing the choice, so the Commander can walk the list and listen. **The line is the core's own** - its intro, or the first sentence of it - because a voice is being cast for a character and a generic sample is a different question than the one being asked. Through the one arbiter like everything else that makes a sound (architecture.md D7): an audition ducks and is cut off by the shut-up hotkey exactly as speech is, and starting a second audition drops the first mid-word rather than queueing behind it. **Auditioning a paid voice costs money** — each press is a synthesis request billed by the character — so it is a press rather than a hover or a selection-changed, it is never automatic, and it is counted by whatever *What the voices cost* lands as. Cache the clip for the session, since walking back and forth over four candidates should not be four purchases each way. Free where the provider is free, unavailable with no provider at all, and the button says which of those it is rather than being silently inert. **Shipped 2026-08-16, and "the first sentence of it" was wrong about the corpus.** Six of the eleven cores open by addressing the Commander, so Warden's first sentence is the single word *"Commander."* — a sample too short to tell one voice from another, which is the entire point. So whole sentences are taken until there are sixty characters, still cutting on a sentence boundary; the eleven produce 71 to 171 characters, median 134, and the button's price estimate is asserted against that range rather than guessed. Declared on the `SettingRow` rather than special-cased in the panel, so the surface still renders what a descriptor declares and no second list of voice rows exists. The audition is `AuditionLine.For(core)` synthesised through the one arbiter into its own group, so a second press drops the first mid-word, and the clip is cached per `(provider, voice)` for the session and deliberately not to disk.
  - [x] Spike: what voices does a *new* ElevenLabs account actually offer - `ElevenLabsTtsProvider` lists `GET /v1/voices` and treats the result as "what the provider offers". Nobody has checked what that returns for an account created five minutes ago. If it is My Voices only, a new Commander's picker is **empty** on the provider they just paid for, the per-core pairing has nothing to pair from, and the named default that gives Warden "George" silently does not apply — three failures that all look like "d47 is broken" and none of which the current code can tell apart from an unreachable API. Answer four things: what the endpoint returns on a fresh account; whether the premade voices (George, Matilda, Callum and the rest) are in it or only in the shared library behind a separate endpoint; whether a shared-library voice can be spoken directly by id or has to be added to the account first; and whether adding one is a call d47 could make, or a thing the Commander must do on the website. The answer decides whether the picker needs a second source, whether the named default needs to survive the voice not being listed, and what the empty-list case should actually say. **Answered 2026-08-16, the feared disaster does not happen, and the real bug was the one the item did not ask about.** `GET /v1/voices` **needs no account at all** — 200 and 21 premade voices to a caller with no key, George, Matilda and Callum among them with exactly the ids the pairing tests already assert. So the picker is not empty, the pairing has something to pair from and the named default applies. `show_legacy=true` returns a byte-identical 102,976, so there is no parameter to start sending, and `/v1/shared-voices` is no second source worth having: it is other people's voices, capped at three to an anonymous caller, and unavailable via the API to the free tier. A library voice **can** be spoken by id without being added, so the add call is one d47 could make and should not. **What ships is the third failure**, which is real: `ListVoicesAsync` answered `[]` for no key, a refused key, an unreachable service and a genuinely empty account, and nothing above the seam could tell them apart — the distinction is in the response and was being discarded one line above where it arrived. Two things kept with it: the fresh-account column is **documented rather than measured**, because a probe cannot create an account and the help centre says defaults are for accounts made before March 2026 — which the anonymous call contradicts, and only a fresh key settles; and **George expires 2026-12-31**, published, so a named default that stops resolving has to degrade to the model's choice rather than to silence. Findings in [docs/spikes/elevenlabs-voice-sources.md](docs/spikes/elevenlabs-voice-sources.md).
  - [x] What the voices cost, beside what the model costs - A running total for paid speech, kept for the session and shown next to the LLM turn price so "what has this cost" has one answer rather than one per subsystem. The unit is **characters, not tokens**: ElevenLabs bills per character, Edge bills nothing, and reusing the token shape would quote a number whose basis is wrong. Counted at the `ITtsProvider` seam, where the text is actually handed over, because that is the only place every caller passes - the ship's AI, a callout, a re-voiced in-game sender and a core's own introduction all converge there, and counting at any caller means missing the next one. Counted on the synthesis that **succeeded**: a refused voice or a failed request costs nothing, and a turn cut off by the shut-up hotkey has already paid for the sentences that were synthesised before it - which is the case that makes "count what was sent" different from "count what was said", and the one worth a test. **Characters are a fact and dollars are an assumption**, and the row has to keep them apart: ElevenLabs' per-character price is a property of a subscription tier that the API does not report, so the character count is always shown and a dollar figure only where the rate is known - stated on the row and editable, defaulted rather than invented, in the same spirit as the model price table declining to model introductory pricing it cannot date. A provider that costs nothing reads as **free**, never as an untracked zero: "$0.00" from Edge and "$0.00" from an ElevenLabs run nobody measured are the same string for opposite reasons. Nothing to model for caching, since there is none - the same sentence spoken twice is billed twice, which is itself worth surfacing if a callout ever repeats itself in a loop. **Shipped 2026-08-16, counted by a decorator rather than by a line in each provider** — so the next provider is counted without anybody remembering to count it, and the two that exist cannot disagree about what a character is. Recorded after the await and outside any catch, which is the whole rule: a refused voice, a rejected key and a cancelled turn all leave through the exception and none is billed, while a turn cut off part-way keeps what it already sent. Per provider rather than one total, because a session crosses switches and an hour of Edge added to ten minutes of ElevenLabs is a figure that means nothing. The rate defaults to **$0.10 per 1,000 characters**, the published API list price for `eleven_multilingual_v2`, which is the model d47 pins — and the row says out loud that it is a list price rather than a bill, since a subscription burns bundled credits at an effective rate whose tier and remainder the API reports neither of. The line appears on the turn's provenance row and in `get_model_status`, beside the model's price, in characters rather than tokens.
  - [x] Give the composition root a test harness - `AppHost` is where the app is actually assembled, and it has no tests at all: nothing constructs one, so every behaviour that lives in it is covered only by running the app. Two of the three faults found in one afternoon's hand-testing were in it - stale voice ids surviving a provider switch, and a voice list that never refetched when the key arrived - and both were provably wrong from settings alone once the decision was lifted out into Core. The seam worth having is that lift: decisions that are pure functions of settings belong where they can be asserted, leaving the root to wire things together. Not a rewrite, and not a mock of every dependency. **Shipped 2026-08-16 as two pure functions and no mocks at all.** `SpeechWiring.Plan` answers *rebuild the client* and *refetch the voices* from what the root holds and what is selected; `VoiceMemory.Reconciled` answers what happens to the stored voices. The root still builds the client, still owns the secret store and still starts the fetch — what it no longer owns is *whether*. Both faults are regression tests, and the harness that matters is the sequence one: five applies of an afternoon's hand-testing driven through the planner, asserting three rebuilds and four refetches, which is the shape no single call could have caught. **It found a third fault on the way**, in the code it was lifting past: the speech key check reported "accepted the key — 0 voices" for a key the provider had just refused, because `ListVoicesAsync` answers an empty list rather than throwing and the check counted rather than read.

  - [x] The published docs need a left nav, not a wrapped banner - `docs/` is a Jekyll `minima` site, and minima's header lists **every page** as one inline run across the top. That was fine at four pages and is unreadable at thirty-odd: it wraps to five lines and breaks titles mid-phrase, so the banner reads "Talking to Directive / 47", "Installing Directive / 47", "Panels and / interface" and "Ship and module / specifications" — entries that look like separate links and are not. Nothing marks the current page, and there is no order to scan by, because a flat alphabetical run of every capability is not an order a reader has any use for. **A left nav grouped by section**: general help first, then capabilities under their groups. The grouping is not a new thing to author — `CapabilityDescriptor.Group` already exists, already orders the settings surface, and already groups the table on `index.md`, so the nav derives from the same source rather than becoming a second hand-maintained list that drifts from the first. Prefer a layout override to swapping themes: a theme is a dependency, and this is a nav. Acceptance: vertical and grouped, the current page marked, no title wrapping mid-phrase, usable at phone width, and **every published page reachable from it** — the documentation gate asserts a page *exists* and asserts nothing about it being reachable, which is how a page can pass CI and be invisible. **And a site-wide footer, which is a rules matter rather than a polish one.** Frontier's media usage rules ask that attribution sit somewhere "easily located", naming the header, footer or terms as the places for a community site, and permitting the short form where the long form is impractical. Today the long form is on `index.md` and **nowhere else** — so a reader landing straight on `capabilities/engineers.md`, a page made almost entirely of Frontier's data, sees none of it. The site uses stock `minima` with no `_includes`, so there is no footer to inherit from and one has to be authored: short form on every page, linking to the long form. Frontier also state a preference, not a requirement, for a link back to an official source — `elitedangerous.com` — which costs one line in the same footer. **Shipped 2026-08-16; the footer half had landed the day before, and the nav is the rest.** A `_layouts/default.html` override and a `_includes/header.html` that keeps the site's name and drops the page run, with the grouping in each page's own front matter and `DocumentationGateTests` asserting it still matches `CapabilityDescriptor.Group`, `Name` and registry position — so it is checked rather than merely derived once. Reachability is its own test, and it caught the thing the item predicted: **nine capability pages had no front-matter title at all** and were reaching the header only because GitHub Pages runs `jekyll-titles-from-headings`, which made the page's heading and its nav entry the same string by accident. Two things the item did not ask for and the work made obvious: `index.md`'s hand-written capability table listed **sixteen of thirty-three** and is gone, because the nav is that list now and a second copy is what had already drifted; and the shared `.wrapper` width had to grow, since a 15rem nav inside minima's 800px would have left a capability page narrower than it was before the nav arrived.
  - [x] Follow the live log, or stop following it - A floating jump-to-latest control with follow and scroll-lock, so reading history does not mean fighting new lines as they arrive. **Shipped 2026-08-16, and the lock is inferred rather than declared** — scrolling away from the end stops following and scrolling back starts it, which is what every log viewer a Commander has used already does, so there is no mode to remember to leave. Following is a property of the surface like the scroll position it is about, so the window can be reading history while the headset stays live, and it resets on a page change because "I have scrolled up" does not mean anything about the page just arrived at. Two things only a running control shows: the first layout pass raises a scroll change with the *viewport* growing from zero and the offset still at the top, so reading that as a gesture switched following off on every surface the moment it was shown — it keys on the offset alone; and `ScrollToEnd` scrolls to the extent it currently knows about, so the layout has to be forced before it or every append lands one line short.
  - [x] Live log selectable and copyable - Read back what was said and copy it out. Free-form drag-selection across a continuously appending log is the hard part; rendering it as one read-only block is the way around it. If it is free due to the nature of the controls being used, then don't invent a workflow that's unneeded. **Shipped 2026-08-16 as nothing at all, which is the answer the item asks for.** The transcript has been a `SelectableTextBlock` since Phase 4 — drag-selection and Ctrl+C are the control's own, across the appending log included — so what this item ships is a test asserting it stays that way, because it is free only until somebody swaps the control for a plain `TextBlock` and the hard part has to be built by hand.
  - [x] Copy log - One affordance for copying the entire session's log as currently filtered. **Shipped 2026-08-16**, beside the search box, taking the page as currently shown — the conversation without the diagnostics, or with them, or the log file. A query **highlights** on these pages rather than filtering, so it deliberately does not narrow what is copied: copying only the matches would be a different feature and a surprising one. Confirmed on the button itself, since a fault here is otherwise indistinguishable from having worked.
  - [x] Check for Updates on start - On start, when a new release is available, the user is given an opportunity to exit, install it, and restart.
  - [x] A real installer, not an archive - `d47-setup.exe` is the normal way in, with the zip still published for portable use and still what the in-app updater fetches. Per-user into `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Programs\d47` at `PrivilegesRequired=lowest`, so the no-elevation property survives; a proper Add/Remove Programs entry; rerunning a newer installer upgrades in place rather than stacking a second copy. The install directory is fixed rather than versioned, which is the reason Squirrel and Velopack are not used despite otherwise fitting: they install into per-version folders, so the exe moves on every update and orphans the `data\` folder beside it - the Commander's keys, settings and a several-hundred-megabyte model. Uninstall asks once before removing `data\` and defaults to keeping it, because none of that is recoverable by reinstalling. The Start Menu entry is written to exactly the path `StartMenuShortcut` tests, so the app's own offer stands down with no notion of having been installed. Pulled forward out of order: it was drafted for later in this phase, and the Commander was unpacking archives by hand in the meantime, which is a poor reason to wait. Not covered: code signing, so SmartScreen still warns - that needs a certificate and is a spending decision, not a build one.

- [x] **Phase 20 — On-foot engineering**
  - [x] d47 knows what you are wearing - Today it has none of this: no suit, no weapon, no grade, no mods — only the four `Backpack.json` and `ShipLocker.json` lists that `SuitInventory` already reads. `SuitLoadout`, `BuySuit`, `UpgradeSuit`, `UpgradeWeapon`, `BuyWeapon` and `LoadoutEquipModule` are every one of them unread. **The journal is still the id authority, but it is no longer the only source** — FDevIDs has no suit list and no on-foot weapon list, and `outfitting.csv` is ship modules only, which inverts the ship arrangement where the id list named things and the journal merely agreed. EDDiscovery's `EliteDangerousCore` (Apache-2.0) closes the other half: `Items/Suits.cs` is keyed on the very `SuitFDName` the journal writes, and `Items/HandItems.cs` covers hand weapons. Its per-grade stats are a **lead rather than a table** — the provenance varies per figure, one row is annotated as a guess, and its `SuitStats` has a transcription bug that assigns health multipliers to the shield fields — so the names and ids join and the numbers get checked before any of them are spoken. So the table is keyed on what `SuitLoadout` writes and joined to community names through the same relaxed matcher that already reconciles `Tod 'The Blaster' McQuinn`. Independently useful before any table exists, because "what am I wearing and what grade is it" cannot be answered at all right now. Reuses the `ControlContext.OnFoot` prompt-cache profile, which is already a first-class tool profile. **Shipped 2026-08-16.** `OnFootLoadout` folds all six events; `OnFoot.tsv` joins the symbols to names through EDDiscovery, with the stats deliberately left behind. **The localisation trap is not an edge case and that is the finding:** 269 of 768 `SuitLoadout` events carry an unresolved token and *every one says Class1*, so a build that spoke `SuitName_Localised` would name the wrong grade more than a third of the time — and look like it was working. Two things the item did not anticipate. `flightsuit` has **no class suffix at all**, so reading a grade off a symbol needs somewhere to stop. And the journal's modification symbols **do not join the recipe names**: 13 observed, 5 join on a relaxed match, 8 do not, and nothing in any checked source carries both spellings — the `Engine_Dirty` problem again, answered differently only because 25 modifications is a table a person can author and 786 blueprints is not.
  - [x] Know what on-foot engineering does - **Two axes, and neither is the ship model.** Grade 1 to 5 is bought at **Pioneer Supplies**, not from an engineer: a fixed material list buys a fixed stat ladder, with no roll and no quality anywhere in it. Modifications come from engineers and are **ungraded, binary and permanent** — four per item at most, one slot earned per grade, and a grade 1 item has **zero** slots and cannot be modified at all. **The material cost of an on-foot build is therefore exactly and completely knowable**, which is the inverse of the ship problem: on-foot ingredient sizes are real quantities (5/10/15 for mods; 1/5/10/15/25/35 tiered by grade for upgrades) rather than the uniform 1 that made ship totals need a roll count at all. Generated in the established shape, from EDEngineer's 90 on-foot rows — 34 named mods with exact costs and engineer attribution, plus 56 `@Merchant` rows which are not engineering at all but the grade-upgrade recipes for 3 suits and 11 weapons — joined against FDevIDs `microresources.csv`, where 83 of the 84 ingredient names already match and the one that does not is `Ballistic Data` against `Ballistics Data`, an alias `tools/gen-engineers.py` carries already. That generator currently drops every one of those rows: `@Merchant` and `@Bartender` are filtered as `NOT_PEOPLE`, so **the whole on-foot vocabulary is being discarded by one line**. Two data traps to code around rather than discover: `Manticore Oppressor` is spelled `Opressor` at grades 3 to 5 and will split that weapon on a name join, and three pairs of mods appear under both an old and a new name with byte-identical ingredients. Findings in [docs/spikes/on-foot-engineering-sources.md](docs/spikes/on-foot-engineering-sources.md). **Shipped 2026-08-16, and the sentence above was true of the game and false of the source.** *"Exactly and completely knowable"* holds — but **every on-foot quantity in EDEngineer is pre-patch**, by a factor of two for modifications and three for grade upgrades, plus Power Regulators removed outright. Measured against the game itself over 78 ingredient comparisons on 16 real upgrade events and a zero-remainder cover of 41 material lines across four `ShipLocker` deltas, each corroborating the other's leftovers, and later corroborated a third time by per-modification wiki pages that cannot have copied either. So this is the one place a generator *changes* a source's numbers rather than printing the disagreement — because here one of the two sources is the game — and it refuses to run on a size outside the measured tables, since one uncorrected number among corrected ones is invisible. The generator no longer discards the on-foot rows (that line was fixed in Phase 14) and the `Opressor` typo is corrected with an assertion that it still exists. **Two things fall out that the item did not ask for:** the credit cost of a grade is the item's base price × {4, 15, 30, 50} per step, exact on 12 of 12 weapon upgrades; and the locker cap is **1,000 per category, not per item**, settled by Components sitting at exactly 1,000 in 7,931 of 28,148 snapshots with the largest single component at 94. Findings in [docs/spikes/journal-corpus-on-foot.md](docs/spikes/journal-corpus-on-foot.md).
  - [x] Who unlocks whom, on foot - Worth its own line because **the referral chain is in the data here**, which is exactly what Phase 14 could not find for ship engineers: six of the thirteen Odyssey engineers have an `Unlock` row naming the *referring* engineer alongside the tribute — Domino Green refers Kit Fowler refers Yarden Bond, Hero Ferrari refers Wellington Beck refers Uma Laszlo, Jude Navarro refers Terra Velasquez refers Oden Geiger. Three roots, three chains of three. The non-material half of each unlock (a hundred light years by Apex shuttle, ten on-foot conflict zones, Unfriendly with Sirius) is in community sources and is **read from one source so far**, which is not enough for something whose failure mode is a wasted trip. **Two corrections from reading the wiki at source, both of which would have shipped wrong.** The four Colonia engineers — Baltanos, Eleanor Bresa, Rosa Dayette and Yi Shen — are not data-less at all; they are missing from EDEngineer and they carry the *widest* mod lists of the thirteen, so a Colonia Commander reaches nearly everything through three of them where a Bubble Commander needs nine. And **EDEngineer's unlock quantities are stale**: the Type-8 update cut four of them and the file still has the old numbers, Kit Fowler's Opinion Polls by a factor of eight (40 against 5). That makes the unlock column the part of the generated table most likely to be quietly wrong, and it is also the part whose failure wastes a trip — so it wants a source that tracks patches, or a staleness note said out loud. Two mods have a single Bubble source each and are therefore routing-critical: Night Vision at Oden Geiger, Quieter Footsteps at Yarden Bond. **Shipped 2026-08-16, and it found a bug nobody was looking for.** The referral chain was already in `Engineers.tsv` — and **every Odyssey tribute was filed one link down it**. EDEngineer's `Unlock` rows name the engineer being unlocked on all 18 ship rows and the *referring* engineer on all six Odyssey ones, so keying on that array had Kit Fowler asking for what Yarden Bond wants, on all six. It read as correct because both are real engineers with real tributes. Filed on the row's **name** now, which is right on all 24. **The stale quantities are stated rather than patched**, which is the second thing that changed: correcting them per ingredient was tried and abandoned, because the two sources are not counting the same thing — EDEngineer's Odyssey rows are *cumulative along the chain* and Frontier's notes are per engineer, so EDEngineer's 40 Opinion Polls is not a stale version of Frontier's 5 but a different quantity, and replacing one with the other would produce a number belonging to neither. Four tributes carry a note saying what Frontier cut, which is the *"staleness note said out loud"* this item offered as the alternative. The four Colonia engineers now carry their modification lists, and the nine Bubble ones stopped saying "Suit:0, Weapon:0" — an on-foot speciality is the **modification's name**, because there is no grade to reach and the old shape said nothing that told one engineer from another.
  - [x] Plan a suit or a weapon - The planning half, keyed by suit and weapon rather than by `ShipID`, riding whatever the Phase 17 checklist machinery turns out to be. **Permanence leads, because it is the whole reason this needs planning more than ships do:** four slots, no undo, no way to practise, and a wrong mod recoverable only by buying and re-upgrading a fresh item. Ships forgive a bad roll; this does not forgive anything. The plan also carries an ordering constraint ships have no analogue for — grade before mods, always, since a grade 1 item cannot be modified — so "upgrade at Pioneer Supplies first" is a step in the list rather than a footnote under it. And since the totals are exact (above), the shortfall arithmetic needs none of the floor-and-record hedging the ship plan is built around. **Shipped 2026-08-16 on the Phase 17 substrate unchanged**, as two new scopes (`Suit`, `Weapon`) and two new intent kinds (`Grade`, `Modification`). **Keyed on `SuitID` rather than on the symbol**, which is the decision the whole item rests on: a Maverick's symbol changes at every grade, so a plan keyed on it would abandon itself at the moment the upgrade it was written to describe landed. Grade before modifications is the list order rather than a note under it. Three states the ship plan has no use for: grade 1 is **Blocked** rather than open because gathering fixes nothing, all four slots spent is **Blocked** and says it needs a fresh item, and a modification whose spelling cannot be joined is **Unverified** — guessing "not fitted" would have a Commander spend four permanent decisions twice. One thing declined: three modifications have a different recipe per weapon manufacturer and the journal has one symbol for all three, so a plan that does not name the weapon is refused a total rather than quoted a Takada price for a Manticore.
  - [x] Go and get it, on foot - **The sourcing is more specific than anything the ship items can offer.** Every micro-resource carries `SettlementType`, `BuildingType` (16 codes, from AGRI to STO) and `ContainerType` (22 kinds of locker and data port), so "which settlement, which building, which locker" is answerable rather than approximated — where the best a ship material gets is "Planetary Settlement". The **Bartender** is the on-foot material trader and a restricted one: Components only, with Goods and Data sold for credits rather than exchanged. **The rate is computable**, which the ship material trader's never was: the Bartender takes Assets for a fixed "Barter Value" and sells them for a fixed cost, and those two numbers are exactly the `BarterValue` and `BarterCost` fields sitting on the 33 Components. So "trade these for those" is arithmetic here rather than a refusal. Illegal goods are refused outside Anarchy-controlled systems. And **an on-foot engineer's base has no Pioneer Supplies** — only an Apex taxi desk — which turns "grade before mods" from an ordering preference into a routing fact: the upgrade cannot be done when you get there. Findings in [docs/spikes/on-foot-engineering-sources.md](docs/spikes/on-foot-engineering-sources.md). **Shipped 2026-08-16, and the Bartender's rate is now measured rather than promised.** `floor(Σ(offered × BarterValue) ÷ BarterCost)`, exact on **49 of 49** real trades — with two corrections to how it was described. There is **no carry-over** between trades: modelling the leftover as credit at the same market drops the score from 49 to 30, so whatever the last unit does not consume is lost and a Commander should trade in one lump. And **one published figure is wrong** — Graphene's `BarterValue` is 13, not 12, pinned to exactly that by two independent trades; the wrong number is the plausible one, because 12 fits the `2v−1` pattern the other 32 follow and 13 does not. The settlement/building/container detail is in `Materials.tsv` and drives a second batching pass keyed on the building rather than on "Planetary Settlement", which is all the origin string ever says. Findings in [docs/spikes/journal-corpus-on-foot.md](docs/spikes/journal-corpus-on-foot.md).
  - [x] Spike: what the journal says about a suit - **Two of the four are answered; the spike is what is left.** A 912-journal corpus on a second machine settled that `SuitName` **does** encode the grade (`explorationsuit_class1` across 768 `SuitLoadout` events, which also carry `SuitMods` and per-module `WeaponMods`, so what is fitted is observable with no table at all), and that the Pioneer Supplies upgrade **does** charge credits — 4,500,000 for a class 4, alongside five resources. Still open: whether the locker cap is 1000 **per category** or per item type, where the sources genuinely conflict and where the corpus cannot help because `ShipLocker.json` is a state file rather than an event; and whether `BarterCost` and `BarterValue` compose into a usable rate. **The blocker was a corpus and it was never a design** — it was recorded as "no journal has this" when the truth was "this laptop has none", which is the same mistake the 402 made in a different costume. A trap found on the way, and it belongs in whatever reads these events: an `UpgradeSuit` carries the suit's **old** symbol beside its **new** `Class`, and Frontier's own localisation returns `$UtilitySuit_Class1_Name;` for a class 4 suit — so the localised string names the wrong grade and must never be spoken. Findings in [docs/spikes/journal-corpus-engineering.md](docs/spikes/journal-corpus-engineering.md). Throwaway probe under `spike/`, finding written to `docs/spikes/`, per `spike/README.md`. **Answered 2026-08-16, and no probe was needed — the reason both questions were open was wrong.** The locker cap is **1,000 per category**: `ShipLocker.json` is indeed a state file, but Elite *also* writes a `ShipLocker` **event carrying the whole locker**, 28,148 times in the very corpus this item recorded as unable to help. Components total exactly 1,000 in 7,931 of them and never once more, while the largest single component ever held is 94 — which refutes a per-item cap outright. Consumables carry a separate per-item cap of 100. And `BarterCost`/`BarterValue` compose exactly (see *Go and get it*). **Two answers nobody asked for, and both changed the phase:** the credit cost of an upgrade is a fixed multiplier on the base price, and **EDEngineer's on-foot recipes are pre-patch by factors of two and three** — which is the one thing this phase was most confident about and would have shipped wrong. Findings in [docs/spikes/journal-corpus-on-foot.md](docs/spikes/journal-corpus-on-foot.md).
  - [x] Read the sources nobody has read yet - A standing item rather than a feature. **First pass done 2026-08-15 and it paid for itself several times over:** `elite-dangerous.fandom.com` answers **402** to an automated fetch and `forums.frontier.co.uk` answers **403**, and both render perfectly in a browser — so every "no source has this" in the first two passes meant "a user agent check stopped me". That one session produced the complete engineering roll table, the full mod-to-engineer map for all thirteen on-foot engineers, the barter mechanic, the credit cost of a grade upgrade, and the discovery that EDEngineer's unlock quantities are a patch out of date. **Still unread and worth a second sitting:** Inara's per-item stat ladders, and its flat effect figures for the 26 of 34 mods EDEngineer leaves empty. The habit this item exists to enforce is the one that failed twice already — a fetch returning 402 is a fact about the fetcher, never about the galaxy. **Second pass 2026-08-16, and the habit paid again.** The wiki has a **page per modification**, all twenty-five, carrying the in-game description, the effect as a percentage, the full material cost and the credit price — for weapons as well as suits, which closes the longest-standing row in section 7. Its material costs match the corpus measurement exactly on the pages checked, from a source that cannot have copied it, which is the strongest warrant this investigation has produced. **And it is only partly updated**, which is the part worth carrying: *Greater Range*'s three per-manufacturer sections disagree with each other about which patch they are from, so a row of this source is corroboration and never authority. **Four of twenty-five pages were read and the effects are not shipped**, because quoting four as though the set were complete is the failure this document exists against — the remaining twenty-one are the next sitting, and they are cheap. Remaining gaps in [docs/spikes/on-foot-engineering-sources.md](docs/spikes/on-foot-engineering-sources.md) section 7 from lead to fact.

- [x] **Phase 21 — HOTAS switches**
  - [x] Read the Commander's controllers - `Windows.Gaming.Input.RawGameController` reads button state from the desktop process with no driver, no window and no elevation, which is what keeps this inside the per-user no-elevation install and off the vJoy/ViGEmBus row in architecture.md §10. The device list is not a startup query: it fills in over time, and the spike's first reader waited for it to become non-empty and confidently reported three of six devices. The reader waits for the count to settle and stays subscribed afterwards, since hot-plug and slow enumeration are the same code path. Hardware lives in App behind an interface; Core sees settled switch positions through a `Poll()` in the same shape as the journal reader, so the replay harness can drive a switch without a stick attached. Findings in [docs/spikes/hotas-switch-read.md](docs/spikes/hotas-switch-read.md). **Built. The framework cost is measured and the settle rule earned itself on the first real run.** `D47.App` moves to `net10.0-windows10.0.26100.0` for the SDK projections — the one thing the spike left unanswered — and that costs **6.4 MB, 10% of the published exe**, recorded with the decision in [docs/plans/phase-21-hotas-switches.md](docs/plans/phase-21-hotas-switches.md). The projection is a managed assembly plus a COM activation reaching a published build through the single-file bundle, which is a third layout no automated test can tell from `bin\`, so **`--selftest` gained a third check** against exactly the bug class that once shipped a d47 whose transcriber had never worked. Run against the published build it passes — and the fifth controller interface arrived *after* the first read, which is finding 1 happening in front of the gate that was written for it.
  - [x] Assign a switch by walking its positions - The settings row says *move it to each position in turn and pause at each*, samples after a settle delay at every stop, and stores what it saw. **The walk is what makes this device-independent**, and it is why the instruction is not the shorter *flip it and leave it there*: walking discovers how many positions the switch has, which button index each one holds, and whether every position holds one at all — none of which can be assumed, and all of which a single sample silently inherits from whatever hardware happened to be on the bench. Indices were consecutive within a switch on the bench; that is a grouping hint during capture and is not load-bearing. There is no per-device profile table because none can be authored: Windows reports `HID-compliant game controller` for every device, so nothing exists for a human to key a table to — learn-by-flip is the only option rather than the convenient one, and the capture is the whole of what d47 will ever know about a Commander's stick. The instruction also carries the maintained/spring-return classification — a maintained switch is still held when the sample lands, a spring-return one has gone home, and a spring-return cannot be *left* anywhere — because hold duration cannot do it. Measured, switch flips ran 407-1611 ms against push buttons at 206-1751 ms, so the ranges overlap outright and any threshold is a coin toss. A spring-return switch or a hat is declined with the reason rather than accepted and left to behave oddly, and a walk that cannot be made sense of is declined the same way rather than guessed at — with an exportable capture report, since most of the devices this has to work on are ones nobody here will ever hold. **Built, and the walk found a second decline the spike had folded into the first.** *A candidate button that was never held at a stop* has two causes and they are not the same problem: the control went home by itself, or the Commander walked past a position without pausing. They are told apart by what is held after the release — back to the previous position is spring-return, a different button is a rushed walk — and each gets its own sentence, because the fixes differ. **The starting position is captured retroactively**: the switch is already at one of its positions when the walk begins, and which of the sixteen buttons held at rest belongs to it cannot be known until something moves, so it is resolved backwards out of the baseline once the candidate set exists.
  - [x] A switch position means a state, not a press - The flip is the question and the game's own status is the answer: on a flip, d47 asks whether Elite is already in the state that position means, and presses the Commander's binding once only if it is not. Gear already down from a voice command and the switch moved to down does nothing at all, which is the entire point — every existing remapper is edge-triggered and blind, and that is why a maintained switch desyncs the first time the game changes its own mind on docking or a relog. Between flips nothing is touched, so voice, the game's own automation and the switch never fight. This is not an autonomous action: the Commander flipping a switch is the Commander asking. **The position encoding is captured, never assumed.** Every maintained switch on the bench held exactly one button at all times including at centre, which makes the input an index rather than a boolean — but nothing in `RawGameController` requires that, and a three-position switch reporting nothing held at centre is an equally legitimate design that cannot be ruled out from one vendor's hardware. So no-button-held is stored as a position like any other, named by its absence: a reconciler carrying the bench's encoding as a constant would read that centre as *still where it last was* and drive the wrong state silently, which is the exact failure this phase exists to prevent. Mode-gated by the same `ControlContext` that decides what is worth advertising, and reachable from the panel only — never from the tool surface, because a hostile in-game message must not be able to remap a switch. **Built, and the assignable set is closed to the ten actions Elite reports the state of.** `GameAction.Reports` and `AlreadyIn` already answered *is it there already*; an action with no reported flag cannot be asked, so a switch meaning it could only press — which is the remapper this item replaces. Enforced in validation, so a hand-edited file naming `boost` is refused by name with the reason. `SwitchPosition.Button` is `int?` and there is no code path that can express *nothing held means unchanged*: fault-injecting one fails two tests.
  - [x] A mapping that no longer fits its device says so - Stored against `NonRoamableId`, never against VID+PID.
