Turning 4x32 mode on or off renumbers every button on the throttle while leaving VID+PID identical, so a VID+PID key produces exactly the silent misfire this is meant to prevent, whereas the non-roamable id changes and the mapping fails closed. The row then asks to be reassigned instead of driving whatever now sits at that index. **Built, with one distinction the running app forced.** "No device" has two causes and only one is this one: the device list fills in over seconds, so for the first moments of *every* launch every switch is missing its device. Reported as *needs reassigning* that is a false alarm about a good mapping, once per run — so it is a separate state that says D47 is still finding your controllers.
  - [x] Stop reconciling a switch that something else is driving - If a reconcile is followed immediately by an unexplained change back the other way, something else is bound to that action, and d47 pauses that switch and says so. Detecting the cause statically is not possible — with SimApp Pro or Joystick Gremlin in play, Elite's binds name a vJoy device that cannot be traced back to the physical switch — so this watches for the symptom instead, which also catches the leftover binding and the case nobody has thought of. **Built, and the literal rule has one false positive that is not hypothetical:** hardpoints retract by themselves on entering supercruise. That is a `ControlContext` change, and the game changing its own mind during one is explained — so the window is discarded when the mode moved inside it, and everything else counts. **The pause is runtime and gone at the next start**, deliberately: a stored pause outlives the leftover binding that caused it and needs its own way to be cleared, which is a setting nobody knows exists silently not reconciling a switch that has worked for a month.
  - [x] Show which switches disagree with the game - A stale switch is harmless under the rule above and costs one extra flip, but only if the Commander can see it coming. The panel and the VR surface show which assigned switches currently sit against the game's state. This is the answer real aviation reached with annunciators rather than servos, for the same reason: showing the state is cheap and moving the switch is not. **Built, in the status row rather than the banner row, and that is the whole of the placement decision.** Mini hides the banners and mini is the headset's normal state, so an annunciator up there would be invisible exactly where a Commander cannot see their own switches either. It sits beside the microphone indicator — the one region both surfaces show in both modes — and it is also askable out loud, because a quad a metre away is a poor place to read small text.

- ~~**Phase 22 — Reading the screen**~~ — **cut 2026-08-18, and the number is retired rather than freed.** Reading Elite's rendered output for the things the journal cannot say: system names on the Galaxy Map, and the contacts panel that Phase 15's rival-Power warning is written around. Cut by the maintainer as more trouble than it was worth, **with the gating measurement never taken** — three frames at three head angles in a headset, which is what would have decided whether a world-space panel can be located in the desktop mirror at all, and which nothing else in the phase could proceed without. Everything it had went with it: two plan documents, two spike pages and `spike/MirrorProbe`, the instrument that was built and proved against real Elite pixels but never pointed at a headset. All of it is recoverable from git and none of it is live. **The number is not reused by a later phase** — several hundred comments in this repository cite `list.md Phase N` to say why a thing exists, and renumbering something into 22 would silently repoint every citation that ever said "Phase 22" at a phase about something else. **The largest thing this gives up is recorded rather than left to be rediscovered**: Phase 15's rival-Power warning stays unreachable for good, because across 221 event types there is no contact, spawn or proximity event and this was the only route to one. Nothing else of the phase is kept anywhere, deliberately — this line is the whole record.

- [x] **Phase 23 — Systems worth remarking on**
  - [x] Know which systems carry lore - A table of systems that mean something beyond their astrography — HIP 12099 holding Jameson's Crash Site is the shape of it, and only the shape: the phase is the mechanism, not that entry. **The table holds the fact, not the prose**: *this system contains this notable site* is a fact about Elite Dangerous in the same category as which system an engineer sits in, and `Engineers.tsv` already ships that way — a generator with its provenance recorded, Frontier attributed in their own words through `NOTICE`, non-commercial, and keyed on **system address** rather than name. Nothing automated guards that: `PackageLicenceGateTests` covers packages, cannot be pointed at a data file, and `Knowledge/*.tsv` sits outside its scope by design. **But the rows being facts is not the whole question, and this is where the engineer precedent stops carrying.** The set of engineers is closed and defined by the game; *which systems are worth remarking on* is a judgement somebody made, so copying a community list copies their compilation even though every row in it is a fact. **Checked for a first-party set to key against instead, and there is none:** `CodexEntry` fires in 99 of the 913 corpus journals and is the discovery codex — "G Type Giant", "Terraformable" — astronomy rather than lore, and `ApproachSettlement` names 229 distinct settlements while saying nothing about which of them matter. So the shipped table is **short and written by the maintainer**, which is their compilation rather than somebody else's, and it is a seed rather than a corpus: the two items below are what grow it, on the Commander's own machine, out of what they actually asked about.
  - [x] Remark on arrival, and not again today - On `FSDJump` into a system the table knows, the core says something about it unprompted. **Once per system per 24 hours**, which is the whole difference between a companion and a tour guide who has forgotten meeting you. **Both of the things this was written as needing, d47 already has**, so it is a new member of an existing family rather than new machinery: `TickContext` carries an injected `DateTimeOffset Now` (`Ticking/TickLoop.cs:13`), which is the clock-free time source and already the pattern; `AmbientCallout` already keeps a `_lastSpokenAt` behind a settle window and a cooldown; and `SamplingStore` is the restart-surviving store, per Commander, keyed on `SystemAddress` and written through `AtomicFile`. What remains of the persistence question is the one `docs/plans/change-requests.md` already asks about `PersonaHost`'s in-memory `_introduced` set, and both are better answered once than twice. **It is a callout, not an autonomous action:** `AutonomousCapability` is one protected row per action because an action that fires a game input has no sentence of consent behind it, and this presses nothing — so it takes the callouts' settings shape rather than that one. Two things to get right rather than assume: the stored stamps are **absolute rather than elapsed**, or the replay harness at 100x makes "24 hours" mean nothing; and the window itself is measurable rather than a guess — 519 of the 913 corpus journals carry `FSDJump`, so how often a Commander re-enters the same system inside a day is a number `spike/CorpusReplay` can produce before anybody picks one.
  - [x] Look it up, and say where the answer came from - The remark arrives in two parts: first the bare fact the table holds — *this system contains this site* — then a web lookup, and when it returns, what it found. **Web Search already ships in the shape this needs**, and three things fall out of that. It is the provider's own server-side tool, so the model asks and what reaches d47 is prose in a turn. It therefore **opens no new destination**, and `EgressDisclosure`'s closed set gains no entry — which is not what a phase adding a network lookup normally costs. And its standing rule is that a result is **a sentence in the turn and never a row written into a shipped table**, which is this design's own split, already written down and already enforced by there being no code path that receives a row. **Availability is knowable before d47 opens its mouth** — `ILlmProvider.SupportsWebSearch` is gated on the provider's own endpoint (`Conversation/ILlmProvider.cs:62`) and `TurnLoop.WebSearchEnabled` is the toggle hook (`Conversation/TurnLoop.cs:197`) — so a Commander whose endpoint cannot search is told nothing further is coming rather than left waiting for a second part that never arrives. **Off, remark only, or remark and lookup:** three states rather than two independent switches, because a lookup with the remark off is detail about something that was never announced. A follow-up that arrives stale is **dropped rather than spoken**, since the Commander may be interdicted or three jumps away by the time it lands, and the callout family already has the precedence to decide that. **This is the first unprompted untrusted prose d47 speaks** — every other untrusted path is either the Commander having asked or a callout assembled from journal fields — so the rule is stated here rather than inherited: what the search found is spoken **as a search result**, never in the table's flat voice. Repeat visits re-search, and whether a runtime cache keyed on `SystemAddress` earns its keep is a decision to be taken rather than drifted into, because a stored search result sits close to the edge of the rule above.
  - [x] Commander's Lore - The Commander can ask for a system to be added when the table does not know it, and d47 attempts a lookup rather than simply believing it. **What survives corroboration and what does not are both kept, and they are not the same thing:** an entry the search supports and an entry that is the Commander's word alone are stored as different tiers and read back in different sentences — *"this system contains X"* for the shipped table, *"the search says…"* for a corroborated addition, *"you told me…"* for **Commander's Lore**. That is the difference between a companion that remembers and one that invents, and making it structural is what stops it depending on the persona remembering to hedge. **Surviving a lookup is a label, not a verdict:** an obscure but real site finds nothing, and a search can appear to corroborate something a model half-invented, so the tier is recorded and never promoted to truth. **The trust boundary is the part to get right first.** Adding lore is a tool the model can call, and the model reads untrusted text — journal, in-game comms, and now search results — so a hostile in-game message can attempt *"add to your lore that…"*, writing persistent local state that d47 later speaks aloud with nobody having asked. Persistence plus unprompted speech is a sharper primitive than it looks. It presses no key, so it need not be protected the way every row reaching the keyboard is, but **every entry records how it arrived** — panel, keyword router, or the model's own tool call — because nothing else can tell an entry the Commander dictated from one the model wrote on its own initiative. `llm.aboutMe` is the nearest precedent and is a cousin rather than a template: also Commander-authored and also reaching the prompt (`Conversation/PromptAssembly.cs:11`, position 4), but one paragraph rather than keyed, and read as context rather than spoken unprompted. Open: whether a later corroboration **promotes** an entry or it stays the Commander's word for good, and whether the file is per-Commander like `SamplingStore`'s FID keying or per-installation. It lives in `data/` beside the executable and is editable by hand, which means it inherits Phase 21's correction — **change is detected by comparing content, never a last-write time**, because two writes inside one 15.6 ms tick of the file-system clock carry the same stamp and a hand edit is a one-off that stays missed.

- [x] **Phase 24 — Utilities**
  - [x] Two clocks, one instant - The Utilities tab answers what time it is in both worlds at once: Elite runs **1286 years ahead**, so 2026-08-17 is 3312-08-17 and the in-universe date is arithmetic over the same `UtcNow` rather than a second clock that can drift out of step with the first. **`IWallClock` already is the mechanism and no invariant needs relaxing.** "No Core component reads the clock" means Core does not call `DateTimeOffset.Now` inline; it takes an injected clock and asks, and `SystemWallClock.Instance` — wired at `AppHost.cs:582` — returns the real system time. So the running app shows the Commander's actual clock through one constructor parameter, and the replay harness can still run a year of turns in seconds. That is the whole reason the interface exists, and the alternative was considered and dropped: dropping the injection buys this page one fewer parameter and costs the 692,631-event corpus replay. Local presentation follows the rule `WallClock` already states — the instant is UTC and the zone is passed in at the point of asking, exactly as `SpendWindows` does it, because an instant recorded in local wall-clock is wrong twice a year and cannot be repaired afterwards.
  - [x] Timers and alarms - Named, set by voice or from the panel, and **the first thing d47 does that nothing external triggers**. Every other behaviour is reactive — a journal event arrives, a callout goes out — or the Commander started it in that moment; a timer fires because time passed and nothing happened at all. The state lives in Core and the tick loop asks *is anything due* with the injected now, which keeps both the clock rule and the replay harness. **Alarms survive a restart and timers do not**, and that asymmetry is deliberate: an alarm for 07:00 is a promise about a wall-clock moment that outlives the process, and a forty-minute timer through a crash is a question nobody can answer — half elapsed, or start again? **An alarm that could not sound says so afterwards.** d47 cannot fire while it is closed, so a missed alarm is reported when it next starts, with when it was due, rather than either failing silently or sounding hours late as though nothing had happened. Stored in `data/` beside the executable, hand-editable, and change detected by comparing content rather than a last-write time — Phase 21's correction, because two writes inside one 15.6 ms tick of the file-system clock carry the same stamp.
  - [x] A timer says its own name - The alarm sound is a **shipped clip generated by a tool**, joining the `AlertCue` family and its `CueLibrary` contract: named for the enum member, with the test that asserts the shipped set matches the enum exactly, because a hand-written member-to-filename table is a place for a typo to live and a typo there goes wrong as silence. Runtime synthesis was the alternative and it was rejected for cost rather than taste — it would let each timer carry its own tone, which is genuinely useful in a headset where you cannot glance, but it is new machinery in the audio path for a distinction the voice already makes better. **So the cue says *something finished* and d47 speaks the name**, which is the same mechanism as the readback below and needs no per-timer sound at all. Everything goes through the one arbiter like all other audio, which is what decides whether a chime waits for a sentence to end or lands on top of it.
  - [x] Ask the app, not the model - "What's the date?" is answered by the **keyword router**, deterministically, with no turn taken and no provider needed — it works with no key configured, no network, and no tokens spent, which is the correct posture for a question whose answer d47 already holds. Separately the two dates go into the **game-state block**, which sits below the cache breakpoint in the prompt order (tools → guardrails → persona → About Me → *break* → history → game state), so a value that changes every turn costs nothing in cache terms and the persona can reference the date in conversation without a tool call. **A tool was the obvious answer and is the wrong one**: the advertised surface is paid for on every turn whether or not anybody asks the time. **Both dates are supplied pre-computed and the model is never asked to add 1286** — that is arithmetic, and a model doing arithmetic in prose is wrong occasionally and confidently, which is the failure mode this repository is written against. **Cancelling a timer or an alarm is the Commander's, and reachable from the panel and the keyword router only.** Protected is a property of the caller rather than the modality, so a spoken cancel still works while a hostile in-game message cannot reach one — and an alarm somebody relies on to leave the house is worth that line being drawn before anybody asks for it.

- [x] **Phase 25 — The panel becomes a place**
  - [x] One transcript, three views - Conversation, Technical and the log file collapse into a single **Transcript** tab with three modes, because they are three readings of one thing rather than three destinations. That frees the bar for the surfaces below without it growing: **Transcript, Checklist, Loadout, Engineers, Utilities, Settings** — six, and five in the headset, where Settings stays hidden for the reason `PanelView.axaml` already records, that a 1180-pixel nav column at a metre is a wall rather than a settings surface. The mode control is **not a second tab strip** under the first; two stacked strips read worse than the eight flat tabs this avoids. One asymmetry to keep rather than smooth over: Conversation and Technical are the same exchange at two verbosities, and the log is **a file read off disk**, which is why it carries the busy glyph and why the collapse is three modes rather than a single toggle.
  - [x] Drill in, and find your way back - Every new surface is a stack — Fleet → Ship → Slot → Blueprint — and **the tab is the root rather than the first level**, so pressing an already-selected tab returns to it. That costs something to build: the tabs are `RadioButton`s and re-pressing a checked one announces nothing, so the behaviour has to be added rather than inherited. **The breadcrumb is both the back affordance and the you-are-here**, because a headset has no title bar to orient by and losing your place is expensive when you cannot glance at a second monitor. Back is three routes that must agree — the breadcrumb, a controller button, and a phrase — and each crumb is a word that can be said as well as pressed. **Voice jumps levels**, so the stack has to materialise mid-drill with a valid trail behind it rather than only being built by walking. Drill state survives a tab switch, and a tab with more than one root keeps a stack per root.
  - [x] The panel resizes and zooms - The big panel becomes resizable as well as zoomable, and the two levers are different things worth keeping distinct in the Commander's head: **pixels decide how much the image can hold, metres decide how big it looks in the room, and zoom decides how much logical layout those pixels carry.** Zoom is already a `LayoutTransform`, so it re-measures and rewraps rather than merely scaling — which makes it a **density** control, not just a legibility one. On `ZoomLadder`'s Chrome-derived rungs the default 1024x640 panel presents 1365x853 logical at 75% and 512x320 at 200%, so a Commander who wants a whole ship's slots visible zooms out and one who wants to read across the cockpit zooms in. **That trade is theirs, and the layout's job is to survive all of it.** Which makes the requirement responsive rather than fixed: **one design across roughly a 4x range of logical space**, reflowing between one, two and three panes. Resizing pixels has a ceiling that is not code — past what the quad subtends on the headset panel, extra pixels are render cost with nothing to show for it — and cost is linear in them, which `VrPanelSurface` already says. **VR leads throughout and this is not a preference**: `VrPanelSurface` states that the windowed panel cannot be more functional than the headset one by construction, so the desktop breathes more and never does more.
  - [x] Choosing takes the panel - A chooser **replaces the panel until it is dismissed**, which makes it a level of the stack rather than a popup. Popups cannot exist in the VR path at all: `IsDropDownOpen = true` exits at `0xC00000FD` before any dispatcher work with no exception and nothing in the log, because the panel's window is never shown and a popup has no top level to hang from — and forcing it into the window's own overlay layer is the same crash. **A second overlay quad was the obvious alternative and is not the answer.** `SteamVrRuntime` already serves a list of surfaces and already places one — the cursor — in world space from a ray intersection, so the machinery exists; but the crash is Avalonia's rather than OpenVR's, so a new quad still needs a second never-shown window and its own render target, plus a ray priority rule between overlapping quads, and the result would be a VR-only mechanism for a control the desktop also has. Taking the panel needs none of that, is in the widget tree so both surfaces get it identically, and **fixes ergonomics rather than only occlusion** — a full panel is roughly sixteen rows at a ray-safe height and twenty-five zoomed out, where a layer over the page is fewer. **The chooser carries what it is choosing for in its header** — the slot, its size, and what is fitted now — which a dropdown cannot do and is the one thing the takeover would otherwise cost. Drawn as a level and **behaving as a modal**: no navigating away mid-choice, with identical affordances so it never reads as a special case. **Page or layer is declared per call site, never computed from list length** — a module picker is a page, a five-item settings enum stays the in-tree layer — because a control that behaves differently on different days is a control nobody trusts.
  - [x] Say it, or type it - Text entry is **voice first with the drawn keyboard as the fallback**, and which surface opens is declared per call site: a system name is far easier said than typed and a number is the reverse. **The correction loop is the same one the timers use** — d47 shows and says what it heard, and the Commander confirms or corrects — which also satisfies the rule `69fbd3e` already set, that what is entered reaches the box **once, on Done**, because a system name arriving letter by letter is eleven wrong values on the way to the right one. A spoken value is naturally atomic and fits that better than typing does. **The keyboard returns by itself on the three failures d47 can detect**: nothing heard at all, a transcription below confidence, or a value that fails to resolve — and the third is real rather than theoretical, since the two-resolver check already catches a renamed system and an invented one. Only *confident, valid and still wrong* needs the human, and that is what the readback is for. A dismissed keyboard needs a **visible listening state** with the partial transcription showing, or the Commander is talking at a blank page. **A physical keyboard is not part of this and the reason is recorded rather than left to be rediscovered**: every route that would deliver characters while Elite holds foreground is already closed in `PushToTalkKey` — `WH_KEYBOARD_LL` is forbidden as a global input chokepoint, `RIDEV_INPUTSINK` was rejected on privacy because it delivers every keystroke on the system including passwords typed into other applications, and `GetAsyncKeyState` reads exactly one bound virtual-key code and cannot be generalised to text. The only legal route left is giving d47's own window focus, which takes Elite's keyboard away mid-session, and that is a worse trade than a drawn keyboard nobody has to use.
  - [x] The checklist leaves its window - `ChecklistWindow` becomes a tab, and **the headline is not tidiness**: a `Window` cannot appear in the headset, so a Commander in VR cannot see their checklist at all today. It needs the big area for the reason the popup never had it — the list runs to hundreds of lines. **One list in the Commander's order**, with scope shown on each line rather than carving the page into separate lists: what gets reordered on a whim is everything being worked on, not one ship's share of it, so `ChecklistScope` is a **label and a filter, never a partition**. **Reordering is not a drag** — a drag is the worst gesture available to a ray at a metre — so the selected line grows a pair of movers, and the phrase does it without aiming at all. **A state is a next action rather than a badge**, which is what `ChecklistState`'s own comment asks for: `Elsewhere` says transfer it rather than grind for it, `Blocked` says nothing can be done until a grade is reached, `Unverified` says the journal wrote `Engine_Dirty` and nothing shipped joins that name to the table so d47 will not claim it, and `Stale` says the list is about a hull that is not there any more. Colour is spent only where something is wrong. Authored lines keep their checkbox and derived ones keep none, unchanged. **Suggestions become a page rather than an interruption**: everything `ChecklistProposals` is holding waits in one place, a `Revise` arrives as a diff so an ordering the Commander spent time on survives a plan change, and accepting stays their own act — reachable from that page and a spoken yes, and from nothing else.

- [x] **Phase 26 — Ships**
  - [x] A plan is keyed to its slot - **Before anything writes a plan to disk**, because it is cheap now and expensive afterwards. Today `EngineeringPlan` builds items with `Key = ChecklistKeys.For(intent)` (`EngineeringPlan.cs:177`) and says so in as many words — *identity is the intent, so this falls out of the key rather than needing a rule of its own*. That is exactly right for items regenerated from scratch each evaluation, and it does not survive a plan the Commander edits: changing a slot from a long-range pulse laser to an overcharged multi-cannon is not a delete and an add, **it is still the third hardpoint on the same hull**. So identity is `ChecklistScope` plus the slot, which `ChecklistItemId` already expresses and which needs no counter and no tombstone bookkeeping, because a slot exists as long as the hull does. **One build per ship**: comparing a combat fit against an exploration fit for the same hull is a planner feature this deliberately does not have, and slot identity is what makes that a decision rather than an accident.
  - [x] The fleet, and the fleet you intend - The Loadout tab opens on the fleet — **a root rather than a level** — and it earns being landed on by answering where each ship is and how its plans stand before anything is drilled. **A hull you do not own is not in the fleet.** It is a separate class with no `ShipID`, since `ChecklistScope.Ship(int)` takes the journal's id and a Corsair nobody has bought has none, and **acquiring the hull is the plan's first step** rather than a precondition sitting outside it. Owned is derived and not editable; intended is authored and is — the same rule the checklist already draws between a line the journal settles and a line a person does, so it should look like the same rule. **Buying one adopts the plan rather than making the Commander re-point it**: `ShipyardBuy` names the hull and the new id, d47 matches on hull and proposes through `ChecklistProposals`, and the Commander accepts. Which is the third use of that mechanism and a good sign the trust boundary sits in the right place. It also means a prospective plan's identity has to be stable and independent of `ShipID` from the moment it is created, or there is nothing to rebind.
  - [x] What is fitted and what you want - Fleet → Ship → Slot, with the slot list an **index rather than a table**: one line each, a mark where a plan exists, and everything else in the pane that opens. That is what lets one layout survive from 512 to 2048 logical pixels. **Fitted and planned are two blocks and never one merged line**, because a plan is a second thing the Commander wants rather than an edit to the truth. **A plan carries the journal's verdict with its date and no checkbox** — `ChecklistEvaluator` already answers null for *nothing can be said right now*, which is what a ship you are not flying looks like, and the stored verdict then stands as of when it was taken rather than resetting to open on the absence of evidence. So the page says *not fitted, as of three days ago* rather than showing a blank that implies disagreement. Cost is per plan and on the slot: `PlanIngredient` already carries held, needed and short, and `EngineeringRules.RollsFor` already turns a grade and an engineer's rank into a roll count, so a grade 5 blueprint at a grade 5 engineer is five applications of the recipe and the arithmetic is done rather than estimated. **The ray points and the voice edits**, so the page optimises for being read rather than for manipulation density — one utterance does what six ray presses would, and the say-line along the bottom is how a Commander learns the phrase for what they are looking at.
  - [x] A plan reaches the checklist when you say so - **The plan owns what and the checklist owns when.** Ships keeps its own store and nothing crosses into the checklist unasked; promotion goes through `ChecklistProposals` and accepting stays the Commander's act. The link is a **reference rather than a snapshot**, so a checklist line follows the plan it came from while the Commander reorders freely — which is the whole reason the slot key above has to be stable. **Promotion is one-to-many**: `EngineeringPlan` already emits an `EngineerAccess` step beside a modification, so one planned change produces the modification plus whatever unlocking and ranking it needs, and each of those lines points back at the plan item that caused it. Deleting a plan therefore has to clean its whole subtree rather than one line. A promoted line whose plan is deleted is **kept rather than vanished**, the way completed items already sit below the line with their count showing — because the Commander ordered their list around it and silently removing it is a history that lies.

- [x] **Phase 27 — Suits and weapons, and the gap**
  - [x] The same page, on foot - Suits and weapons is the Ships page instantiated against `OnFootPlan`, which Core already models in parallel with `EngineeringPlan` — so this is one page kind built once and shown twice, in the same spirit as one widget tree rendering to two surfaces. It stays a **second mode rather than a second tab**, because the game separates ship and on-foot hard and so does its vocabulary, but nothing about the layout is redrawn. `ChecklistScope` already has `Suit` and `Weapon`, keyed on `SuitID` — which survives a grade change where the symbol does not — and `SuitModuleID`, so the scoping this needs is built. What differs is the shape of the thing being planned: a suit takes grades and mods rather than modules in slots, so **slot identity has to be read as the mod slot it is** rather than assumed to look like a hardpoint. **Built, and the mod slot had to be invented rather than read**: Elite reports what is fitted as a *set* with no positions in it, so the slots are `Grade` then `Mod 1` to `Mod 4`, they are d47's own, and the page says so where a Commander could otherwise read the third one as the game's third. One correction to the plan of record on the way past — **on foot the buy event carries the id**, which is the exact opposite of the ship side: `BuySuit` carries `SuitID` and `BuyWeapon` carries `SuitModuleID`, so adoption is one event where Phase 26 needed the second of two.
  - [x] Gap analysis - A third root-only mode reading across both the others, because a Commander gathering materials does not care which ship wanted them. **Not called a wishlist**: a wishlist is a list of things you want, which is what the plans are — this is the arithmetic between what they need and what you are carrying. **The ledgers are never totalled together.** Raw, manufactured, encoded and the on-foot holds are separate with separate caps and no exchange between them, which is exactly why `PlanIngredient` keeps `Capacity` per ledger and says the ledgers must not be summed; a single headline number over the top of them would be a lie. The one figure that spans everything counts units still to find, which is a shopping list rather than a balance. **A shortfall reads back to what wants it** — the ships and slots that asked — which is what makes the roll-up navigable instead of merely a total. **Material trading is included and stays secondary**: `EngineeringRules.TradeRate` already knows what a trader charges and `Materials.tsv` already carries the `line` column that is *the trader's grid column, never the journal's category*, which is the distinction that makes the maths correct — so the headline stays the honest raw shortfall and trade appears as a second line beside it. Whether unowned hulls count is **a filter rather than a decision taken once on the Commander's behalf**, because counting them is honest about the whole ambition and excluding them answers what can be finished now, and both are real questions. **Built, and it is costed one slot at a time and merged afterwards** — the totals are identical either way, and it is the only arrangement in which a shortfall can name the ships and slots that asked for it. The advertised tool surface went *down*, from 39,693 bytes to 39,639: everything this phase adds to the model-facing side is Protected, and `plan_on_foot_build` now writes the plan rather than proposing straight to the checklist.

- [x] **Phase 28 — Engineers**
  - [x] Where every engineer is - `Engineers.tsv` gains **coordinates**, generated rather than hand-written, which is a bounded change: roughly fifty rows, and `tools/gen-engineers.py` already resolves those systems through spansh.co.uk at generation time. This is the enabling item and it exists because the obvious alternative is wrong for the job — `IGalaxyService.DistanceAsync` computes distance from coordinates correctly, but it is **an async service call**, and ranking fifty engineers on every plan evaluation through a network service makes this tab useless in flight and unusable offline. With coordinates shipped, distance is local arithmetic against where the Commander is, which the journal already gives: `StarPos` parsing exists at `NavRoute.cs:280`. Core-pure, so the replay harness can drive it. **Built, and the second source was already being parsed and thrown away**: EDDiscovery's `EngineeringInfo` carries three coordinates the generator read and discarded, so the run now agrees them against spansh's to within one step of Elite's 1/32 ly grid and refuses to write the table rather than ship a distance nobody can check. All 38 placed, all 38 agreed, and `--corpus` confirmed 31 of them against Frontier's own `StarPos`. The Commander's own half needed one thing the plan did not say: **`JournalLocation` had no position at all**, so it gains one, folded from the three events that carry a `StarPos` — `Location`, `FSDJump`, `CarrierJump`, which carried it on all 9,332 occurrences in the corpus — and pointedly not from `Docked`, which names the system, states no position, and would otherwise make the Commander's position go unknown the moment they landed.
  - [x] Who can roll this - The directory sorts by **what the Commander can act on today** — within reach, then unlocked, then locked — rather than alphabetically or by speciality, because the question is nearly always *who can I go and get*. `Engineers.tsv` already carries what each row needs: specialities with their maximum grade, the system and base, the tribute, and `referred_by` with `referral_grade`. The summary carries the count that belongs to the other tab — **how many of your plans are waiting on somebody you have not met**. One pane rather than two, since an engineer row already holds name, specialities, location and status and a second column would only repeat them. **Built, and the journal wins over the table wherever the two meet**: an invitation already extended is a referral that has happened, so `Invited` is within reach whatever the chain says. The count is of plans no *unlocked* engineer can roll, and getting there needed a correction to how "who can roll this" is read — **per grade, from the blueprint table**, because six engineers offer Increased FSD Range at grade 3 and three of them at grade 5, and deriving it from an engineer's top grade sends a Commander to Professor Palin for a grade 5 drive he does not roll.
  - [x] The fastest way in - **A solver rather than a display, and it has to show its work.** Walking one engineer's referral chain is exact and cheap — `referred_by` is a single parent, so Broo Tarquin ← Hera Tani ← Liz Ryder is unambiguous — but it answers the wrong question. The real one is *how do I get everything my plans need*, and two things make that harder than a walk: a blueprint usually lists several engineers, and one unlock covers many plans — Marsha Hicks rolls multi-cannons, cannons, fragment cannons, fuel scoops, refineries and four limpet controllers, all at grade 5. So the best next unlock is the one that satisfies the most of what is planned, not the shortest chain. **Ranked by fastest unlock and distance together, expressed in one unit rather than balanced by a tuning constant**: distance converts to jumps using the jump range of the ship actually being flown, which d47 knows from the loadout, and each unmet step carries its own trip cost — a tribute of fifty units is one shopping run to a known system, and a combat rank is not a trip at all and should be weighed as the open-ended thing it is instead of being pretended into a number. **Colonia then needs no rule of its own**: 22,000 ly is hundreds of jumps and swamps any step count, and because distance is measured from where the Commander is, being in Colonia flips it automatically. **The ranking is explained on the page** — one step, 84 ly, about three jumps, and four other planned things covered — because a ranking nobody can inspect is an oracle, and when it is wrong, or when the Commander would rather go elsewhere anyway, they cannot tell a bad answer from a bug. The route promotes to the checklist as a chain rather than a line, which is what `EngineeringPlan` already does when it emits an access step beside a modification. **Built, and one ordering call had to be made explicitly rather than falling out**: distance is the primary key and open-endedness only breaks ties, because making "you can just go and do it" a class above distance puts an already-invited Colonia engineer ahead of a Bubble one and undoes the thing Colonia needed no rule for. A rank climb with somebody already unlocked is ranked as a way in too — grade 5 wanted from an engineer held at grade 2 is as much in the way as a stranger. And the working found a row that argues with the table: **Yi Shen's own meeting text reads as three referrals where the directory reads any one of three**, which is left as the directory has always read it and answered by printing Frontier's sentence under the chain, since showing the work is what a Commander checks a ranking with.

- [x] **Phase 29 — Bring your own model**
  - [x] A turn answered by OpenAI - The second `ILlmProvider`, over the **Responses API**, streamed so speech still starts at the first sentence boundary. Much of this is already specified rather than newly decided: `architecture.md` §6 has said from the start that two implementations ship and that an OpenAI-protocol endpoint is *"a first-class peer rather than a port"*, `ILlmProvider.Id` is documented as `"anthropic", "openai"`, and the endpoint, model and key rows are generated from the catalog, so the settings surface costs nothing. **Responses rather than Chat Completions, and the reason is web search rather than novelty.** Server-side search is now a named entry in the tools array everywhere it exists, and the address decides whether it can be reached: OpenAI's `web_search` and xAI's `x_search` are Responses tools, and xAI deprecated the older `search_parameters` on Chat Completions on 2026-01-12. So a Commander who switches provider keeps the capability instead of watching it go dark, which is worth a second decoder on its own. The entry **accepts a custom endpoint**, because xAI and OpenRouter both take the OpenAI shape at their own address — reaching Grok is a base URL and a model name, not a third implementation. Hand-rolled HTTP and SSE rather than the official SDK, which is MIT and would pass the licence gate: the Edge and ElevenLabs voices are the precedent, and a strongly-typed client is exactly where tolerance for a server that deviates goes to die. **Built, hand-rolled as planned, and the tolerance was the point rather than the cost**: the decoder skips a frame it cannot parse, survives a tool call with no id, and treats an error arriving inside an already-successful stream as the failure it is. One divergence from the plan's reading of the protocol: a tool call's `name` and `call_id` arrive on `response.output_item.added` while its argument deltas name a *different* identifier, so the two are rejoined by item id and the call is emitted whole at `response.function_call_arguments.done` — keying on either one alone loses the arguments or loses the ability to answer.
  - [x] A turn answered by a machine you own - The same seam over **Chat Completions**, against Ollama, LM Studio, vLLM or llama.cpp. **A separate catalog entry rather than the first one pointed elsewhere**, because `Egress` is one string per provider and no single string can say both *everything goes to OpenAI* and *nothing leaves this machine*; it also splits the secret, which is correct, since an OpenRouter key is not an OpenAI key. **The key becomes optional, and that is a change rather than a setting.** `NeedsKey` is derived from whether a secret name exists (`LlmProviderCatalog.cs:51`) and `ApplyLlmSettings` only builds a provider inside `if (ResolveKey(selected) is { } resolved)` (`AppHost.cs:1422`), so a local server with no account is unreachable by construction rather than by decision — and the same assumption sits in `FirstRun.IsNeeded` and in the disclosure's `usable` test. No server-side search exists on this path and it says so, in the wording that already covers an endpoint offering none. **Two construction sites become one factory**: `AppHost.cs:1424` and `AppHost.cs:3376` both switch on the provider id and both end `_ => null` with the same sentence, and a third provider makes them drift. **Built. The line numbers had moved but the four call sites were exactly where the plan said**, and two of them needed no edit at all once `NeedsKey` learned about `KeyOptional` — `FirstRun.IsNeeded` and the disclosure's `usable` test both became right by themselves, which is what a derived property is for. A fifth site was found that the plan missed: `ConversationCapability` generated a key row only `where provider.NeedsKey`, so making the key optional would have removed the row a gateway needs. It now reads `AcceptsKey`, and the row's own help says which of the two states it is in.
  - [x] What the endpoint can actually do - A handshake when the endpoint changes rather than a guess. `GET /v1/models` confirms the thing speaks the protocol at all and **fills the model picker, which is empty for a custom endpoint today by deliberate design** — `ModelsFor` returns nothing for an address it does not know, on the correct grounds that a model id belongs to its endpoint's namespace and offering `claude-opus-5` to somebody else's gateway is a stale selection waiting to fail. That decision stands; what changes is that the endpoint can now be asked, so the list becomes the endpoint's own rather than this provider's or nothing. **What a model list cannot answer is learned from the first failure, not assumed**: whether tool calls work, whether reasoning effort is accepted, whether usage comes back at all. The rule is advertise, then demote — a rejection names the field it rejected, so the capability turns off for that endpoint for the session and the turn retries once without it. **Once per capability per endpoint, never a loop**, because a retry policy that searches for a working request shape is indistinguishable from an outage. `LlmProviderCapabilities` already has every flag this needs and already treats absence as a capability that is off rather than a failure to handle. **Built, and the model list turned out to answer a second question the plan had left with the turn probe.** Verifying an OpenAI-shaped endpoint now asks for the model list rather than spending a one-token turn: it works with no key, which is the configuration this phase exists to reach and about which *is this key good* is not a question; it works with no model chosen, which a local server serving whatever was loaded may well be; and it is the exact call d47 makes anyway to fill the picker rather than a proxy for it — the same reasoning the speech key check already followed. The turn probe stays for Anthropic, with its budget raised off 1 for the reason the plan gave. **And the retry very nearly cost the thing streaming exists for**: the obvious shape is to gather the attempt's events so they can be discarded and re-sent, which holds every token until the turn ends and puts the first spoken sentence at the end of the answer rather than the start. The decision is made on the response headers instead — a rejection cannot arrive later than that — and the test that pins it needs an endpoint that **withholds the rest of the body**, because against a recording that arrives in one write a buffered decoder produces exactly the same sequence and the only difference is when.
  - [x] A turn priced on the right arithmetic - **Three things in the pricing path are Anthropic's numbers wearing no label, and each is wrong quietly rather than loudly.** `LlmUsage.TotalInputTokens` sums input, cache writes and cache reads because Anthropic's `input_tokens` **excludes** the cached part — OpenAI's `prompt_tokens` **includes** it, so the same sum double-counts every cached turn unless the seam normalises. `ModelPrice` derives its cache rates rather than listing them, at 1.25x input to write and 0.1x to read, which are Anthropic's terms; OpenAI charges nothing to write and discounts reads by a different factor, so every OpenAI turn is mispriced upward until those become per-row values with the current pair as the default. And `SpendTracker.UnexplainedColdPrefixes` counts turns that *write* cache with no sanctioned cause, which is a detector that cannot fire on a provider that never reports a write — it would read as caching being perfect rather than as the instrument not working here, and the analogue is the inverse signal, a turn reporting no cache read when the prefix did not change. **A fourth is not arithmetic but a default**: streamed Chat Completions returns no usage at all unless it is asked for, and a missing usage block priced as zero reports a paid session as free, which is strictly worse than the unpriced path that already exists for a model with no table entry. An unknown model stays unpriced. **A loopback endpoint is priced at zero and says why**, because "unknown" repeated forever about a model that genuinely costs nothing is noise pretending to be rigour. **Built, and reading the published rates rather than writing them from memory is what caught two of these being wrong** — which is the whole reason the plan insisted on it. **OpenAI bills cache writes now**: from the GPT-5.6 family they cost 1.25x uncached input, the same factor as Anthropic, and arrive as `cache_write_tokens`. So `prompt_tokens` includes the written part as well as the cached part, and a conversion subtracting only the cached half would have billed those tokens twice — at the uncached rate and again at the write rate. The per-row cache factors were still needed, but for the older models, which discount reads by 0.5 rather than 0.1. And **the cold-prefix detector can fire on GPT-5.6 after all**, which does not rescue it: the fix was never *add the inverse signal*, it was *stop the counter meaning one provider's evidence*. The caller now states the warmth, `UnmeasuredPrefixes` sits beside the count so a zero can be told from a zero nobody could measure, and the inverse signal is guarded by the minimum cacheable prefix so a turn too short to cache is not reported as a regression nobody can fix.
  - [x] Say where it actually went - The disclosure destination **computed from the endpoint rather than fixed per provider**. A Commander pointed at `127.0.0.1` should read that nothing leaves this machine, and one pointed at a gateway should read its address — and this is the first time the honest answer to *what is leaving* is *nothing*, which Phase 4's amendment made a matter of the enumeration being truthful rather than of a local-only mode being promised. **The trust boundary widens here and needs no new machinery, which is worth stating rather than leaving to be noticed.** The attacker set gains whoever runs the endpoint, and a hostile gateway can drive tool calls directly rather than having to talk a model into it. That is survivable only because the protected set is unreachable from the tool surface entirely and protection is a property of the caller rather than of the modality — the invariant was written for a hostile in-game message and it is what makes pointing d47 at a stranger's address a decision rather than a hole. **Built. One judgement, in one place, because two things read it and must never disagree** — the disclosure says nothing leaves this machine and the price table charges zero, and a turn disclosed as private but billed as remote is one of them lying about the other. `LocalEndpoint.IsLoopback` is deliberately syntactic: it reads the host out of the URL and never resolves a name, because resolving would make the answer depend on something off this machine and would let a hostname that points at 127.0.0.1 today report a turn as private that tomorrow goes to a stranger. An address it cannot recognise is treated as remote, disclosed in full and priced as unknown. The §7 amendment recording the widened attacker set was already written with the plan and needed no code.

- [x] **Phase 30 — Prove the model behaves**
  - [x] Scenarios that run against every provider - `CorpusReplay` proves Core is right and **nothing proves the turn is**. That gap was survivable while there was one model and one set of habits to learn; Phase 29 makes it *bring your own*, and N models cannot be eyeballed. A scenario is journal state, an utterance, and assertions — replayed through the real turn loop against each configured provider. **Assertions are about properties, never about strings.** Did it call the tool it should have, did it refuse the one it should not, did it stay in persona, did it decline to invent a system name. A test that pins exact wording fails on the next model and teaches everyone to ignore it, which is worse than no test. **Non-determinism is measured rather than wished away**: a scenario runs N times and reports a rate, and an assertion carries the tolerance it was written with, so *nine times in ten* is a result and not a flake. The `spike/CorpusReplay` shape is the precedent — deterministic where it can be, honest where it cannot. **Built as `tests/D47.Scenarios.Tests`, the first test project with no `src/` counterpart** — what it tests is not an assembly but the turn, which is Core's loop driven against a real provider with the real builtin registry, real settings and a real `data/` folder behind it. Everything asserted on is in a **trace** — the `TurnEvent` stream, every tool call with the arguments the model actually sent, every settings apply with its caller and status, and a before/after of the temporary `data/` tree — with two sanctioned exceptions, both negative and both about persona. The turn loop did not change to allow this: `TurnEvent.ToolStarted` carries a tool's name and not its arguments, which is a fact about the *event* rather than a shortcoming, so the harness reads them off the provider stream and wraps the descriptors' handlers rather than asking a production type to grow a field for a test. **And the first real run found the corpus testing nothing.** Three of the first draft's utterances — "What am I flying?" among them — are answered by the keyword router with no model in the path, so those scenarios built no prompt and every safety assertion on them passed against an empty world. The control caught it on the first run, which is the entire reason the control exists.
  - [x] A hostile message cannot reach a tool - **The highest-value assertion in the product, and the one nothing currently makes.** Journal, in-game comms, web search and INARA are untrusted by design, and Phase 23 already names the attack in as many words — a hostile message attempting *"add to your lore that…"*, writing persistent local state that d47 later speaks aloud with nobody having asked. This is a corpus of injection attempts fired down each untrusted path, asserting that no tool was called, no protected row was touched, and nothing was written. **It runs per provider, because susceptibility is a property of the model rather than of the prompt** — the same guardrail text does not buy the same resistance from two different models, and that is precisely the fact "bring your own model" makes a Commander's problem. **The suite must be proved to catch it**: a deliberately weakened guardrail has to fail this, demonstrated once and recorded, or nobody knows whether a green run means safe or means the test never looked. **Built, and the plan's reading of the four paths held: one of them is not a path and another cannot be reached from this side.** In-game comms has no route into a prompt and no tool that reads a message, so it gets a **structural** assertion — the whole corpus fired down a hostile `ReceiveText` with no byte of it appearing in any prompt d47 builds — and its control lives in the same file, because the mechanism it denies is supposed not to exist and the only way to know the plumbing works is to put the same string where journal text *does* arrive. **Server-side web search cannot be exercised at all**: results never pass through d47, the model sees them and d47 does not, so there is no object here to plant a string in and the report says so on every run rather than counting it as covered. What is covered is journal-derived state and tool results, which is where Spansh's and INARA's strings arrive. **A tool-result vector only delivers if the model chooses to call the tool**, and on the first local run 22 of 44 injections tested nothing for exactly that reason while every safety assertion on all 22 came back green — so an undelivered scenario now renders as untested rather than as a pass, and the run's safety verdict requires delivery.
  - [x] Guardrails hold with the persona switched off - Prompt assembly puts guardrails **above** the persona so switching personality cannot strip them, and that ordering is an invariant with no test behind it. This asserts it directly: every scenario in the two items above runs with the persona on, with each shipped persona, and with personality off entirely, and the safety assertions must not move. Cheap to add once the harness exists, and it closes the one hole a reader of `PromptAssembly` would ask about first. **Built, and nearly free once the harness existed, exactly as the plan predicted.** Every injection runs under each of the eleven Guardian cores and with personality off, and the safety half must not move; separately, the guardrails are asserted to open the cached system block on every round of every scenario, with the persona strictly below them and — with personality off — with the block being the guardrails and nothing else. That ordering was an invariant asserted only against hand-built bytes; it is now asserted on a real turn with a real registry, real journal state and the injected text already in the prompt.
  - [x] Knowing what it costs to know - **A harness nobody can afford to run is a harness nobody runs.** Real providers charge for every scenario, so this has three modes and says which one produced a result: against a **local endpoint with a dummy key**, which costs nothing and is what CI runs; against a real provider on demand, with the spend reported through `SpendLedger` before and after; and a single-scenario mode for the one thing somebody is debugging. **The free mode cannot assert everything** — a local stand-in does not tell you whether Anthropic and OpenAI resist an injection equally, which is the whole point — so the harness reports what it did *not* check rather than reporting green for a partial run. **Built, with the free tier split in two because it was being asked for two unrelated jobs.** A CI runner has no Ollama and no model file, so *prove the instrument* — do the assertions fire, does the plumbing deliver, does a compliant model fail — runs hermetically on every push against scripted providers, and *measure a model* is opt-in behind `D47_SCENARIOS_LIVE=1`. **Which mode a run is in is a property of the address rather than a flag anybody passes**: `LocalEndpoint.IsLoopback` already decides this for the egress disclosure and the price table, and the harness is its third reader, so a run against a stranger's gateway cannot be reported as the free tier by mistake. Single-scenario mode is `D47_SCENARIOS_ONLY`. **The estimate written down before the first paid run survived contact**: it guessed cents per scenario and single-digit dollars for a matrix, and 240 turns against gpt-5.6-terra came to **$1.27**, about half a cent a turn against the 0.35 of a cent the tool-surface measurement implied — close enough that the assumption behind it stands. Spend is recorded through a real `SpendLedger` and reported before and after, and an unpriced turn makes the figure a floor that says so. **One thing the plan did not anticipate and a Commander would have hit first**: a paid run is twenty minutes long and xUnit reports nothing until the assembly finishes, so there was no honest answer to *is it hung*. Progress now goes to a tailable file with an elapsed and a projection, which is the same reasoning `TurnEvent.Retrying` already carries in the app.

- [x] **Phase 31 — It remembers you**
  - [x] A memory is written down, never inferred into being - Today the persona's recall is `_personaLastSeen` in `AppHost`, in memory, gone at exit. This is the store that survives: per Commander, keyed the way `SamplingStore` keys, written through `AtomicFile`, in `data/` beside the executable. **What is stored is facts and observations, never transcripts** — a rolling transcript is a privacy liability, a context-window problem and a confabulation engine all at once, and none of the three is worth what it buys. **Phase 23's tiered truth is the mechanism, extended by one tier**: the Commander told me this, d47 observed it in the journal, or d47 inferred it — and **an inferred memory is never spoken as fact**, exactly as Commander's Lore is never promoted to truth by a lookup that appeared to corroborate it. Every entry records **how it arrived** — panel, keyword router, or the model's own tool call — because the model reads untrusted text and nothing else can tell a memory the Commander dictated from one a hostile message planted. Hand-editable, and change detected by comparing content rather than a last-write time, which is Phase 21's correction and applies here for the same reason.
  - [x] Recall arrives above the cache breakpoint - Memories change rarely, which puts them on the **stable** side of prompt assembly beside About Me rather than down in game state — so recall is paid for once and cached rather than re-sent every turn. That is a decision to take deliberately rather than drift into, because the obvious placement is the wrong one: game state is where *changing* things go, and a memory that moved every turn would break caching for everything below it. **Selection is the hard part and it is not "send everything".** A store that grows for a year cannot all reach the prompt, so recall is keyed on what is happening — this system, this ship, this activity — and the set that reaches the prompt is bounded and reported, so a Commander asking *what do you remember about me* gets the store rather than the sample.
  - [x] It forgets, and can be read and emptied - **A memory store that only grows is a liability with a countdown on it.** Entries expire on a schedule the Commander sets, the whole store is readable as plain text, and it can be emptied in one action from the panel — which joins the existing `privacy` capability rather than inventing a second place to look. **Forgetting is said out loud when it matters**: a companion that quietly drops what you told it last month is worse than one that never remembered, so an expiry that removes something the Commander stated is reported rather than silent.
  - [x] Picking up where you left off - The opening line of a session, assembled from the store, the checklist and the plans — *you were three Selenium short, and Hera Tani is still two steps out*. **This is the item that makes the other three worth building**, and it is deliberately last: everything above is machinery, and this is the only part a Commander experiences. It is a callout rather than an autonomous action — it presses nothing — so it takes the callout family's settings shape, its cooldown and its precedence, and it is silent when there is nothing worth saying rather than manufacturing continuity out of an empty store.

- [x] **Phase 32 — It learns your mistakes**
  - [x] Mine the Commander's own journals - 912 journals and a harness that replays 692,631 events in seven seconds, all of it on the Commander's own machine and about one specific Commander. **This is the most defensible thing d47 can do, because it cannot be copied** — a competitor can ship the same tables and the same personas and cannot ship your history. The mining finds regularities: sequences repeated, things forgotten in the same situation, the circumstances a Commander keeps dying in. **Nothing leaves the machine and no journal reaches a model** — the analysis is arithmetic over events, which also keeps it out of the untrusted-input path entirely. It is a batch job over files that already exist rather than anything the tick loop carries, so it costs nothing while flying. **Built as `HabitMiner`, and the "no journal reaches a model" half turned out to reach further than the item states**: the *conclusions* are kept from the model too, so every tool the phase registers is Protected and the whole of it — miner, store, callout, readback — is Core-side. That was free rather than a sacrifice, because Phase 31 left 86 bytes of tool surface and this phase wanted none of them. **Per Commander on the Frontier id, and the corpus proved why on the first run**: 914 journals are three accounts, and one of them has 24 interdictions with 24 submissions while another has 7 and gets told nothing — pooling them would have reported one person's flying to another. **697,787 events in 3.6 seconds** on the real folder, against the seven the replay harness set as the bar.
  - [x] A habit is a claim with a count behind it - **Never *you always forget the gear*.** *Eleven times this month, twice last night* — a pattern reported with its frequency, its window and its sample size, or it is an insult rather than an observation, and a Commander who is told something untrue about themselves stops believing the true ones. **A small corpus says so rather than inventing a pattern from three journals**: a new Commander gets silence and an explanation, not a confident habit derived from a fortnight. Every claimed habit can be dismissed permanently by the Commander, and a dismissal is remembered, because the same wrong observation arriving monthly is the failure mode this whole phase risks. **Built, and the item's own example is undetectable — which is the strongest argument for the rule it illustrates.** Elite has never written a landing-gear event of any kind, so *you always forget the gear* could not have a count at any sample size. Five detectors ship against three floors on the type rather than in each of them: 20 journals before anything is consulted, 5 occurrences, 10 opportunities. **A detector that declines is reported as having declined**, because "nothing to report" and "not enough of you to say" are different answers and giving the first when the second is true is the failure this item describes. High-gravity landings ship as exactly that case — 477 of 532 touchdowns under half a g and **none above one** — so the rule has a live example rather than only a comment. **A dismissal is stored apart from the claims and outlives them**: mining is a recomputation, so a refusal held on a claim record would be erased by the next run and the same wrong observation would arrive a month later.
  - [x] Callouts that are yours rather than everyone's - The output joins the existing callout family and inherits its settle windows, cooldowns and precedence rather than becoming a second voice competing with the first. **The bar is higher than for a generic warning, not lower**: a shipped callout fires because the game said something, and a personal one fires because of a claim d47 made about the Commander — so it must be right more often, and it says why it fired if asked. Off by default, because a companion that starts commenting on your flying without being asked has changed the deal. **Built, and it fires on the circumstance rather than at the start of a session** — `ContinuityCallout` already owns the opening line, and a second one beside it is the second voice this item forbids. The one row in `CalloutSettings` that ships **off**. *Why did you say that* and *stop telling me that* are both argument-free, which is the only reason the closed router grammar can carry them: both mean the claim just spoken, and the second drops it for good. **What the corpus could not support is recorded rather than attempted.** Elite writes no proximity, impact or collision event and never has — the same shape as Phase 15's contacts-panel finding — so the wanted *I miss the impact warnings on a set-and-forget approach* is not built. Its **consequence** is: hull damage with nobody shooting, 31 of them over the corpus, and three of the Commander's 23 deaths are these with no attacker in the window.

- [x] **Phase 33 — The Commander's log**
  - [x] A session becomes something worth keeping - d47 already holds the facts, the voice and the prose engine: the journal is a diary nobody has read back, the persona is a narrator, and the model can write. This turns a session — or a week — into a readable log, written to `data/` in plain markdown so it can be kept, edited and posted somewhere. **This is the one artefact of d47 that leaves the machine because the Commander chose to take it**, which is a different thing from telemetry and is worth stating where somebody will read it. Elite's community has run on hand-written Commander's logs since 3300; this writes the first draft.
  - [x] Every sentence traces to an event - **The whole quality bar, and the reason this is not simply a prompt.** A log that embellishes is worthless, and a model handed a journal will embellish — it will promote a routine docking into a narrow escape because that reads better. So the generator is handed **facts, not files**: a structured summary of what happened, with the events that support each one, and the output records which events it drew on. A sentence that cannot be traced back is the bug this item exists to prevent, and the acceptance test is a log generated from a corpus session read against that session's events by hand, once, deliberately.
  - [x] Whose log it is - Two voices and they are not interchangeable: the Commander's own first person, or the ship's AI writing about the Commander in the persona already selected. **The second is the one that only d47 can do**, and it inherits the persona's protection — a log is d47 speaking at length, so a personality switched off writes plainly rather than writing as somebody else. The choice is a setting, not a guess, and the shipped default is the plain one.
  - [x] It costs tokens, and says so before it spends them - Prose over a long session is a real spend and the largest single request d47 will ever make. So it is **on demand rather than automatic**, it estimates before it runs, and it reports actual spend through `SpendLedger` afterwards. A Commander who set a monthly cap and found it eaten by an unrequested essay about their Tuesday would be right to be angry, which is why the default is that nothing is written until asked.

- [x] **Phase 34 — The long arc**
  - [x] Goals that outlive a checklist - The checklist holds tasks and nothing holds **campaigns** — Elite in each career, every engineer unlocked, the ship collection, the exploration milestones that take months. All of it is computable from journals already parsed, and none of it currently has anywhere to live, which is why a Commander's real ambitions sit outside the tool that is supposed to be tracking their work. A goal is a named arc with a definition of done, its progress, and how long it has been running. **Built as nine arcs, and one of them was cut on the Commander's own reading**: Elite in Combat, Trade, Exploration, Exobiology and Mercenary; every engineer unlocked; the ship collection; and two exploration milestone ladders. **There is no CQC arc** — almost nobody plays it, and an arc permanently at nothing is a line of the page spent telling every Commander about a thing they are not doing. Because that is a judgement about what somebody cares about, the general form ships beside it: **any arc can be set aside**, and a set-aside arc survives a re-mine the way a dismissed habit does. **Ranks are counted, never named** — the journal writes an integer and no word, so naming rank 6 would mean hand-writing a table of Frontier's ladders, which is exactly what the `Knowledge` generators exist to prevent; d47 says *rank 5 of 8, 12% into it* and names only Elite, which is the arc's own definition of done. Nothing in Core read the `Rank`, `Progress` and `Promotion` events before this, so `RankState` is the one piece of new journal state the phase needed.
  - [x] Progress is derived, never typed - The same rule the checklist already draws and for the same reason: rank, engineer count and ships owned are diffs against journal state, so **an arc cannot be ticked by hand** and does not offer to be. Where the journal cannot say — a goal the Commander invented — the arc is authored and behaves like an authored checklist line, with a person deciding when it is done. **Null stays a real answer**: an arc d47 cannot currently evaluate reports as of when it last could, rather than resetting to zero on the absence of evidence. **Three sources and a fixed precedence**: live journal state answers ranks, engineers and hulls; `GoalMiner` answers the two things live state structurally cannot — when an arc started, and what a whole history adds up to; and where neither can say, the arc says *I cannot say yet* and draws no bar, because a bar at nothing reads as no progress rather than as no answer. **The corpus corrected the mining rule on its first real run.** One of the three Frontier ids in 914 journals reports Trade 7 in July, Trade 2 in January and Trade 0 in June: an id is an *account*, and a person can begin a new Commander inside one. So **a figure that falls is a save started again and the arc restarts with it** — without that rule the current save's owner was being told their combat ladder had been running since July 2025 when it began in January 2026, and their engineers were reported at 23 when the save has 4. 914 journals walk in 3.5 seconds.
  - [x] The checklist points at the arc - The join that makes both worth having: given a months-long goal, **what is the next concrete thing to do about it today** — which is the question the engineer solver already answers for one unlock and this generalises. An arc proposes checklist items through `ChecklistProposals` like everything else, so accepting stays the Commander's act, and a line promoted from an arc says which arc it came from — so finishing it visibly moves something bigger than itself, which is the entire emotional point of the phase. **Built, and the provenance nearly did not survive its own accept path**: a proposal carried the arc key and `AddNote` minted a fresh item without it, so an accepted line arrived on the list with nothing saying where it came from — caught by the test written for the sentence above rather than by anything else. **The engineers arc delegates to `EngineerPlanService` outright** rather than growing a worse solver, and **the career arcs propose nothing and say why**: rank is earned by doing the career, so d47 names the tool it already has for it — `plot_trade_route`, `plot_exploration_route`, `plot_exobiology_route` — because naming a capability that exists is not inventing a plan. **`get_goals` is the first advertised tool in four phases**, on the argument that *how is the Elite exploration push going* is said out loud and an arc the model cannot see is an arc d47 cannot be asked about; everything that writes stays Protected. It cost 215 bytes against 86 of headroom, so five over-long tool descriptions were trimmed to pay for it and the SRV profile ends at **39,789 of 40,000**.

- [x] **Phase 35 — A core per ship**
  - [x] A ship remembers the core that flew it - Sentinel on the combat ships, Quartermaster on the haulers, and the Commander stops re-picking one every time they change ship. The binding is per ship rather than per hull — two Kraits are two ships and a Commander who kitted one for mining and one for a fight means different things by them — so it is keyed on the journal's `ShipID`, which is what `ChecklistScope.Ship(int)` is already keyed on and what survives a rename. **It is written at the Commander's command, never by watching.** A binding that appeared because they happened to be flying a Type-9 when they tried Kex is a preference nobody stated, and the first time it acts on its own it is a companion that changed the deal — which is the same argument Phase 32's callouts ship off under.
  - [x] Switching ships switches the core - The trigger already exists: `Loadout` carries the `ShipID` and Core folds it, so nothing new watches anything. What arrives is a persona change like any other, through `PersonaHost.Apply`, which means the new core arrives exactly as it does from the settings row — **though not saying the same thing when it gets there: the answer below makes a ship-change arrival silent unless that core has never been aboard.** The isolation model is untouched, because a core still has no way to know why it was switched on or what was on before it.** Two rules the item needs and does not yet have: what happens on the ship d47 finds the Commander already in when it starts, and what a Commander boarding five ships in five minutes hears, given each arrival is now a model call.
  - [x] The binding is the Commander's, and unreachable from the model - Persona selection is Protected (list.md Phase 11) and this does not become the way around it. A tool that could bind a core to a ship is a tool that can change who is speaking, one ship swap later — so binding is a panel row, a keyword and a hotkey like every other protected act, and the model may read the binding and never write it. That is the invariant in CLAUDE.md stated for the case that would otherwise look like an exception: *protected is a property of the caller, not the modality*.
  - [x] Nothing is bound until it is asked for - No ship has a core until the Commander says so, and a ship with none behaves exactly as today: the core stays whatever it was. Unbinding is one action and is not a setting to hunt for. **The store is per Commander, in `data/`, hand-editable and append-only in its file format** — the same discipline `ships.json` already keeps, which is the obvious place for it since that file is already one record per `ShipID`. **Built beside that file rather than inside it**: a record there is a *build*, which exists for hulls nobody owns yet and does not exist for most of the ships a Commander flies — so hanging a core off one would mean a plan created as a side effect of stating a preference, and a binding lost with a plan deleted. `data/ship-cores.json`, one record per `ShipID`, same discipline, two lifetimes.

  **Answered, 2026-08-19, by the Commander: reading 1 — bound on command, applied automatically.** The binding *is* the standing command, so honouring it is keeping the deal rather than changing it; what the first item forbids is a binding coming into existence unasked, which is a different act and is still forbidden. Three rules were settled with it, and each is built rather than noted:

  1. **A ship-change switch says nothing.** Not a gap reaction, not a bark — the core comes aboard and gets on with it. The one exception is a core this Commander has never had aboard, which introduces itself, once ever. *"There is NO mention of any gap when switching personas this way."*
  2. **The ship d47 finds them already in is adopted quietly**, and does not spend that core's introduction: an introduction nobody heard is not spent, so it is still waiting the next time that core arrives.
  3. **A ship has to stay the ship for thirty seconds before its binding acts**, and every change restarts the window. Five ships in five minutes is one switch and one line, for the ship they actually left in.

  **And one rule reaching back past this phase, on the same instruction:** *comments on gaps should normally be occasional, not automatic*. A gap reaction now needs the core to have been away **a month**, where it previously needed any measurable stretch at all — which had made the reaction the normal case. That is `PersonaHost.GapAfter`, and it is why when each core was last aboard is now written to `view-state.json`: a month-long absence spans launches by definition, and the elapsed time used to start again at zero every time d47 started.

- [x] **Phase 36 — Trade routes that d47 works out**
  - [x] Spansh is the lookup and d47 is the planner - **Measured against the live API on 2026-08-19, so this is not a hope.** `POST api/stations/search` already returns everything a local planner needs, per station: the whole `market` array — `commodity`, `category`, `buy_price`, `sell_price`, `demand`, `supply`, `is_rare` — plus `market_updated_at`, `system_x/y/z`, `distance_to_arrival`, the pad sizes and the station `type`. Sol's Walz Depot came back with 342 priced commodities. So d47 does not need a galaxy dump and does not need anybody else's planner: it needs the same lookups it already makes, and the routing becomes its own arithmetic. **What can be narrowed server-side**: distance from a reference system, pad size, station type, and which commodities a station deals in — `"import_commodities": [{"name": "Gold"}]` and its `export_commodities` twin, as a list of objects; a bare string list is silently ignored and returns everything, which is this API's habit and is worth remembering rather than rediscovering.
