A commodity-filtered query also **trims the returned market to the commodity asked about**, which is what keeps the pull affordable: 200 candidate stations came back in 865 KiB across four pages of fifty. **Built exactly that way.** `SpanshRequest.Markets` is one more body against the endpoint the station search already uses, and `SpanshResponse.ReadMarkets` reads the array into `MarketSnapshot`. **The pull is deliberately unfiltered by commodity**, which the bullet above shows is the affordable option and is not the one taken: cargo retention has to choose what to carry, and a market trimmed to one commodity cannot answer "what else is on this board". Three pages of fifty, about four seconds, and a station with no coordinates is dropped rather than silently treated as being at Sol. **And no pad filter is sent** — it would narrow the pull and it would also drop an outpost the Commander is docked at, leaving the planner unable to price the market it is planning from.
  - [x] What the lookup will not do, and therefore what d47 must - **Price and demand cannot be filtered server-side and cannot be sorted on.** Bounds written inside the commodity object are accepted and ignored — 203 stations with `demand >= 1`, 203 with `demand >= 50000`, 203 with no bound at all — and every sort shape tried against a commodity's price answered HTTP 400. So the shortlist arrives unranked and d47 ranks it, which is the arrangement this phase wanted anyway. Two things the shortlist is not: **the commodity filter is coarse**, matching what a station deals in rather than what it currently pays for — 49 of 200 "Gold importers" near Sol had any Gold demand at all, and the rest read 0/0 — and **fleet carriers dominate the extremes**. The best Gold price within 50 ly was 4,760,900 credits at a carrier, against 52,282 at the best station; carriers set their own prices and then move, so `type` and `market_updated_at` are load-bearing rather than decoration, and a route built on a price nobody checked the age of is a route to an empty pad. **Both are built and both are visible in the answer.** Carriers are dropped by `TradePlanner` and *counted*, so a plan says how many were left out and why; `market_updated_at` is checked against the Commander's own `max_price_age` before a market is planned with, and every stop reports the date it was reported on. Nothing is sorted or bounded server-side, exactly as measured.
  - [x] A planner of d47's own, beside the one it borrows - `plot_trade_route` ships today and computes nothing: it hands the question to Spansh's `api/trade/route` and reads back the answer. That is the right trade for a plain chain of runs and it cannot answer any of the three below, because they are not parameters of somebody else's planner — they are a different problem. So this is d47's own arithmetic over market data — which the bullet above establishes is possible with the lookups d47 already makes — and the phase has to say plainly whether it replaces the borrowed one or stands beside it, because two tools that both plan trade routes and disagree is worse than either alone. **It replaces it, decided by the Commander on 2026-08-19, and the borrowed call is gone rather than kept as a fallback** — a fallback is that same failure with a delay on it: two profit models behind one tool name and no way to tell which one answered. `plot_trade_route` keeps its name because it answers the same question, `api/trade/route` is no longer called from anywhere, and the trade plot left `IRouteService` for `ITradePlanService` because it stopped being the submit-and-poll protocol that interface exists to describe.
  - [x] The hold does not have to be emptied - A leg that sells everything is the assumption every planner makes and it is not always the best move: holding a commodity past a station that pays poorly for it, to a later one that pays well, can beat taking the money now. **That makes it an optimisation over what is in the hold rather than a chain of independent legs** — the state carried between hops stops being "credits" and becomes "credits and cargo", which is a different algorithm and not a flag on the existing one. It is also the twist a Commander will notice, because it is the one no other tool does. **Built as two successors per leg rather than as a cleverness**: sell everything this station pays for, or keep any lot the destination pays more for, both carried into the beam so the search decides downstream. Buying is ranked against the better of what the destination pays and the best price on the whole board, which is what makes a two-hop carry buyable in the first place. **The first version deleted the feature it was written for**: a leg that neither sold nor bought was dropped as an empty ship moving for no reason, which is exactly what a pure retention leg looks like — caught by the test written for this line, and the guard is now on the hold rather than on the trading.
  - [x] Round trips - A route that comes back to where it started, so an evening's trading ends at the Commander's own base rather than four systems away. The neighbouring `plot_riches` tool already takes a `loop` parameter and Spansh's planner already understands one, so this is the least novel of the three — worth stating so it is not built twice. **Built, and it was not free after all**: Spansh's planner silently *drops* `loop` on a trade plot, which this repository had already recorded and which was one more reason the borrowed one could not answer the phase. Here the closing leg is restricted to the origin and, having no "later", buys only what the origin itself pays for. **A shorter loop that pays better than the ten hops asked for is taken**, because it is a better answer rather than a shortfall.
  - [x] Selling into a market moves its price, and the plan has to know - Dumping far more of a commodity than a station has demand for drops what the rest of it fetches, so a plan that ignores it reports a profit the Commander will not get. **The figure is not known and must not be invented.** The Commander's recollection is roughly ten times demand and explicitly uncertain, and a constant guessed here would make every profit in every plan wrong in a way that reads exactly like the feature working — which is the failure `EliteSpecifications.tsv` and every generator in `tools/` exist to prevent. Two honest routes: derive it from the Commander's own `MarketSell` events, where the quantity, the demand at the time and the credits actually received are all in journals d47 already parses — 915 of them on this machine — or ship the planner saying it does not model saturation and cap a leg at demand. **Not to be worked out now**, and recorded here so that whoever does it knows a measured answer exists on disk. **Shipped as the second of the two honest routes, on the Commander's call**: no leg ever sells more than a station asked for, and every plan says out loud that d47 does not model what dumping past demand does to the price. A stop also reports when a lot was *cut short* by demand, so the cap is visible rather than merely promised. The `MarketSell` derivation remains where this line left it.
  - [x] Ten destinations in a time somebody will wait for - **A target rather than a holy number, and negotiable** — but it wants stating, because the naive formulation of this problem does not finish. Cargo retention makes the carried state *credits and hold* rather than credits, so a plain search over ten hops is the reachable set raised to the tenth power, and that is the version that runs for hours. The version that does not is a bounded search: a beam of the best states carried hop to hop, each leg's buy-and-sell decision solved inside it. **The anchor is what the borrowed planner costs today** — Spansh answered a *four*-hop trade route in forty-eight seconds, measured on 2026-08-14 and recorded in `SpanshRouteService` — so ten hops in appreciably less than that is a good result and anything in minutes is not. **Ten hops is the ceiling and five is the default**, and the measured shape — 150 markets, 30 commodities, a beam of 200 — finishes in tens of milliseconds, asserted by a test that fails if it ever takes ten seconds. The parameter went from "1 to 8" to "1 to 10", and paying for that and for `loop` leaves the tool surface at **39,918 of 40,000**.
  - [x] The arithmetic is not the problem, and the round trips are - **Measured on 2026-08-19, both halves.** A proxy with the real shape — ten hops, a beam of 200, 150 reachable stations, 30 commodities — is 300,000 leg evaluations, and Python does it in **0.79 seconds**; the C# this would be written in is some tens of milliseconds. There is no need to be clever about the search. What costs is asking Spansh: a station search answers in **1.1 to 1.3 seconds whatever it returns** — 25 stations at 125 KiB took 1.07s, 100 stations at 519 KiB took 1.27s — so the bill is the number of requests and hardly the size of them. Two consequences the design should start from rather than discover: **ask for large pages**, since four times the stations cost a fifth more time; and **cache markets**, because `market_updated_at` says exactly how stale a price is and a market does not move between two plans made a minute apart. A planner that re-fetches what it already holds is the only way this gets slow. **Both consequences are in the code and neither was discovered.** The sweep asks for the service's largest page and stops early on a short one; a gathered sweep is cached for fifteen minutes, and a *wider* cached sweep answers a narrower question because the planner measures every leg itself. Two plans a minute apart cost one pull.
  - [x] Where the prices come from, and what that costs - Elite writes a `Market` event and a `Market.json` for the station the Commander is docked at, which is local, exact and covers almost nothing of the galaxy. Everything beyond it is a network source, which is an egress claim with a settings row and a place in architecture.md's provider-traffic table — and third-party market data is **untrusted input** on the same footing as the journal, in-game comms and web search. The phase has to pick: plan only over what the Commander has seen, or reach out and say so. The existing capability's other rule carries over unchanged — **the Commander's balance is never inferred**; a plan is made against a figure they said out loud. **It reaches out and says so**, decided by the Commander on 2026-08-19 — but it plans over what they have seen *first*: `Market.json` is read on the tick into `data/markets.json`, the last twenty-five markets they have stood in, and **the newer of the two prices wins** rather than theirs automatically. It is the galaxy search's host, its one setting and its one disclosure, because it is the same decision. **The disclosure got smaller rather than larger**: the borrowed planner was sent the hold size and the figure they gave to trade with, and this one sends the system and a radius, because the arithmetic happens here.

- [x] **Phase 37 — Routing you can see**
  - [x] A Routing tab, and the strip grows to seven - The three routing capabilities d47 already has can only speak their answers, and a route is the thing a voice is worst at. **Plan, Progress and Course as three modes of one tab**, in the idiom Transcript already uses for Conversation, Technical and the log file — three readings of one journey rather than three destinations, so they cost one tab and a mode control rather than three tabs. **Second in the strip**, beside the transcript rather than out among the ledgers, because a route is read while flying and the tab a hand reaches for in flight should not be the sixth one along. Enum order is strip order and nothing serialises a `PanelTab`, so the placement was free — checked rather than assumed. **The spoken route cost nothing**: `PanelPhrases` enumerates the tabs and their roots, so "routing", "plan", "progress" and "course" all work without the router grammar being touched, and `Named` is strict enough that "plot a course to Shinrarta" does not collide with the Course root.
  - [x] Desktop only, and the roots are flags - `VrPanelSurface` makes no `EnableRouting` call, which is the whole implementation: a tab appears only where a host furnishes it and `PanelView.Tab` declines to select one nobody did. **`EnableRouting` takes its roots as flags rather than a fixed three**, so if the headset ever gets this it gets *Progress* and only Progress — the mode that needs no keyboard and is the one worth reading at a metre — and that is a different set of flags on one call rather than a second page. Building it that way cost nothing now. The headset's own answer on this was **view-only if ever**, which is exactly what Progress is.
  - [x] Progress: the route being flown, all of it - **The mode that justifies the tab.** A Sol-to-Colonia plot is 131 waypoints, and `routes.md` records that reading that aloud is not an answer — so the spoken form gives totals and the next handful and says *ask again from further along*. That cap is correct for a voice and pointless for a screen. Every hop, its class, its hazards, and where the Commander is in it, read from the same `NavRoute` the callout reads: no service, no network, nothing on the tick loop but a reference comparison. **`RouteProgress` lives in Core** because it is arithmetic over records and arithmetic is worth testing without a window. Two rules it carries: **an unknown leg length blanks the total rather than shortening it**, because a sum that quietly omits a leg reads as a shorter trip; and **an unrecognised star class draws as "scoop unknown" and never as "no"**, because a Commander told a star is unscoopable routes around one that would have refuelled them.
  - [x] Plan: three planners, one form each - One page of three cards — jump route, Road to Riches, trade run — rather than a second strip, which `PanelTabs.axaml` records as the thing the tab design exists to avoid. A finished plot opens as a drill *level*, so the breadcrumb carries it and Back returns to the forms, and **the crumb is levelled** so a second result replaces the first rather than reading as a route through two plans at once. Gated on the same `Knowledge.GalaxySearch` flag the tool reads, and off draws the sentence the tool speaks plus a way to the row — a capability that is off rather than an error. **A result is drawn whole**: the service returns every waypoint and the capability speaks five, so the cap belongs to speech. **Capital is typed every time and never written to `data/`** — cargo capacity comes from the journal because it is a property of the hull, and what a Commander is worth is nobody's business but theirs; a form field is exactly where that rule would be lost by accident. **Two things the panel needed that the spoken path never did**: a plot is a submitted job, so a card has a pending line and a cancel, where a voice answer is awaited by definition.
  - [x] One last plan, shared by the voice and the panel - **The real design risk of the phase.** `RouteCapability` computed a plan, described it and kept nothing, which is right for speech and leaves a surface with nothing to draw — so a tab that plotted its own would hold a different route from the one the Commander had just been told about, which is the settings-row-and-speaking-path disagreement arriving somewhere new. `RoutePlanBook` holds the last plan of each kind in `data/route-plans.json`, written by whoever plotted and read by both. **The panel's buttons go through `CapabilityRegistry.InvokeAsync`** rather than round it to the service: the model-free keyword router already invokes tools without a model, so a second caller of one path is the established shape and two paths to one answer is not. **A cache of answers rather than a preference**, which is what separates it from `ShipCoreStore`: nothing in it is the Commander's work, so a file that will not parse is dropped and rebuilt on the next plot rather than reported as a problem to go and fix. **A plot that found nothing writes nothing**, because a stored "no route" would draw as a plan.
  - [x] Course: the clipboard, and the drive that says whether it worked - `NavigationCapability`'s two tools as controls, in `navigation.md`'s own order, because the order **is** the feature: the clipboard first and always, then the best-effort map drive, then the after-the-fact check against the route file. It opens on the destination of the route being flown, which is the system a Commander opening it usually wants. **Every system name drawn anywhere on the tab is a copy target** — a waypoint in Progress, a stop in a trade plan — through the same tool rather than straight at the clipboard, so the one place that knows how to put a name somewhere useful stays the one place. That join is what makes three pages worth more than three pages.
  - [x] A schema fence has to be the schema - `routes.md` carried a pasted `DocumentationGateTests` failure inside the `plot_trade_route` fence, shipped with Phase 36 and published to the docs site since. The gate did not see it because it asserted the page *contains* the schema, and it did — with a stack trace underneath it. Tightened to require a fenced block that **is** the schema. **All 44 capability pages passed unchanged**, so the worry that some legitimately mix prose into a schema fence was unfounded and this cost no rewriting; and **the fault was reintroduced once** to confirm the new assertion names the page and the tool where the old one said nothing at all.
  - [x] What the tab does not do - **No new tool, no new service, no network call of its own.** The tool surface stood at 39,918 of 40,000 before this phase and stands there after it: a panel is not a tool, and everything here is a surface for capabilities d47 already had. The galaxy plotter stays unwired for the reason `routes.md` gives — `api/generic/route` wants the drive's physics and belongs behind the module specification table — market saturation stays unmodelled with the plan repeating its own caveat rather than inventing a figure, and there are still no mining routes because `api/mining/route` is a 404.

- [ ] **Phase 38 — A build you can watch**
  - [x] Two gauges at the head of the slot list - **Power and jump range, live while the build is edited**, because they are the two numbers a build is designed against and neither is visible until after the credits are spent. Today a Commander answers both by alt-tabbing to Coriolis. Panel-side and spending none of the tool surface: a gauge is not a tool. Plan of record in [docs/plans/phase-38-a-build-you-can-watch.md](docs/plans/phase-38-a-build-you-can-watch.md), and what the corpus said about all of it in [docs/spikes/build-gauges.md](docs/spikes/build-gauges.md). **Horizontal bars rather than dials** (the Commander's call, 2026-08-20): they read at overlay resolution, survive a narrow panel, and need no drawing code of their own. **The priority ladder is out of this phase** by the same ruling — <i>"just give me the horizontal bar chart with both a percent and MW overage (if any) and I'll deal with it in-game"</i> — though `ModulesInfo.json`'s `Priority` is read and kept, so it is a small later addition.
  - [x] Jump range, computed at any mass - `(max_fuel / fuel_multiplier) ^ (1 / fuel_power) × optimal_mass / mass`, plus the Guardian booster's bonus. **Every constant already ships** per drive in `EliteSpecifications.tsv`. Validated against 2,862 real `Loadout` events: **median error 0.000%**, p95 0.000%, 99.3% within half a percent — and it settles what Frontier's own `MaxJumpRange` means, which is unladen mass plus **one jump's fuel**, not a full tank. Accepted when a replay across the corpus reproduces that, and the 0.7% where Frontier's figure omits the booster bonus is asserted as known rather than silently tolerated.
  - [x] Three needles: best, middle, worst - **Best** is unladen plus one jump's fuel, which is the game's own figure and anchors the gauge to something checkable in-game; **middle** is a full tank; **worst** is full tank and full cargo. "Empty fuel" is not a state you can jump in, so the honest top of the range is one jump's worth. Accepted when the best needle equals what Elite reports for the ship being flown, the gauge redraws as slots are planned, and a ship d47 cannot see into says so rather than drawing a figure it cannot stand behind.
  - [x] Power, retracted and deployed, without new data - The split falls out of `mtype`'s first letter: `h*` is a hardpoint weapon and draws only when deployed, `u*`, `c*` and `i*` draw always. That is the distinction hardest to keep in your head by hand — a shield booster sits in a `TinyHardpoint` slot and is `usb`, so it draws all the time. Accepted when both totals are shown separately and a shield booster counts as always-on while a multi-cannon does not.
  - [x] The generator stops dropping three columns - `powercapacity` from coriolis for the 42 power plants, because a plant's `power` column is empty and there is otherwise a numerator with no denominator; `jumpboost` for the five Guardian FSD Boosters, supplied by hand for the validation above; and `entitlement` from FDevIDs for the badge below. Accepted when the header counts are unchanged but for the new columns, `SlotLayoutTests` still passes the four irregular hulls, and a test asserts a Prismatic Shield Generator carries a Powerplay entitlement while a stock one carries none. **Built, with two corrections.** coriolis spells the plant's output `pgen` and has no field called `powercapacity`; and it was **five** columns rather than three, because a cargo rack's `cargo` and a fuel tank's `fuel` were unmapped too — the laden and full-tank needles are computed from those on a build d47 has to model, and they also tell a 5E Cargo Rack from a 6E, which was the complaint the figures bag was built to answer.
  - [x] Planned rolls modelled from the effects already shipped - **No new source.** `Blueprints.tsv`'s `effects` column carries attribute, change and a good-or-bad flag **per grade**, on all 786 modification rows and all 154 experimental ones — including Power Draw on 280 and Optimal Mass on 30 — and the base figure each percentage applies to is the module's own row in the specification table. What is missing is applying them rather than only printing them as a sentence. Accepted when a test applies a blueprint's own grade row to a stock module and lands on the `Modifiers` Elite actually wrote; that test is also what settles whether a blueprint and an experimental on one module compound or add, which the table does not state.
  - [x] Modelled and measured stay distinguishable - **An amendment to a stated rule, and it goes in as one.** `ShipsMode.Parted` says the effects shown are the fitted module's own and never a plan's, because showing modelled figures beside measured ones is the failure the specification table is built to avoid. That is right about a slot row and wrong about a design gauge: a power budget you cannot see until after the materials are spent is not a budget. Narrow to these two gauges. Accepted when a modelled figure is visually distinct from a measured one everywhere the two meet, and switching a slot from planned to fitted moves the number between kinds without changing its meaning.
  - [x] `ModulesInfo.json` as the measured cross-check - Elite writes per-module `Power` **and** `Priority` for the ship being flown, engineering included — 693 occurrences against 2,862 `Loadout` events in the corpus, so roughly a quarter as often. Same shape as the roll figures the tab already treats as measured: authoritative when present, absent often, never invented. It also makes the priority ladder answerable — what browns out at half plant health — which no spreadsheet answers. Accepted when its figures win where the file is present and any disagreement with the table is reported rather than averaged away. **Built, and the 693 is wrong.** All 694 `ModuleInfo` events in the corpus are a bare marker — a timestamp and an event name and nothing else — so there is no history to replay this against: the figures live only in the file Elite overwrites in place, for the ship being flown, as of the last write. A **live** cross-check, then. And because the file names no ship, it is used only where every slot it lists holds the module the `Loadout` says is there — a Commander who swaps ships without re-outfitting leaves the previous ship's figures on disk, and those would put one hull's draw under another hull's plant.
  - [x] A badge on modules a pledge is needed to buy - FDevIDs' `entitlement` column names the gate exactly: **19** modules carry `ELITE_SPECIFIC_V_POWER_<id>`, and the id says *which* Power — Prismatic Shields, Pacifier, Imperial Hammer, Cytoscrambler, Pack-Hounds, Enforcer, Retributor, Concord, Advanced Plasma Accelerator, Mining Lance, Pulse Disruptor, Rocket Propelled FSD Disruptor. Not a hand-maintained list. d47 already tracks the pledge, so what the Commander can actually buy can read differently from what they cannot. Accepted when those 19 carry it and nothing else does, and the glyph renders in a headless capture rather than as tofu — a missing glyph is the class of thing only an eye catches. **Built, and it does not name the Power.** The gate carries Frontier's numeric id and **nothing d47 reads maps that id to a name** — FDevIDs has no powers file, coriolis carries none, EDSY names no Power at all, and Frontier's own module description does not mention one. Twelve hand-written id-to-Power pairs is the confidently-invented game data the guardrails exist to prevent, and getting one wrong sends a Commander to defect to the wrong side. So the badge says what is certain: a pledge is needed, and an **unpledged** Commander cannot buy it at all — which needs no id and is the half that is actionable.
  - [ ] Recipes for what Frontier engineers and EDEngineer does not carry - Reported against a Guardian Gauss Cannon the page said had **no engineering**: it has **Anti-Guardian Zone Resistance**, and Rapid Fire besides. The two shipped tables already name the gap exactly — EDSY's offer list says `hexgg` takes `Weapon_RapidFire` and `GuardianModule_Sturdy`, and `Blueprints.tsv` has a row for neither because **EDEngineer carries no Guardian weapon recipes at all**. Every Guardian hardpoint is in that state, and `misc_agzr` is offered to the power plant, power distributor, Guardian FSD Booster and the hull, shield and module reinforcements too. EDSY holds the missing half — name, grade 1, materials and a Frontier symbol it marks `// TODO` itself, so it is a lead and not an authority. Accepted when a Guardian Gauss Cannon offers Anti-Guardian Zone Resistance with a costed recipe, `OfferedButNotCostedTests` is inverted to assert the gap is closed, EDSY's material keys resolve to `Materials.tsv` symbols rather than being written through unmapped, and a symbol EDSY guesses at is recorded as a guess rather than shipped as a fact. **Until then the surface tells the truth about itself rather than about Elite** — *"Frontier engineers this and I have no recipe for it"* — which is shipped and is not a substitute. **Half of this shipped in v0.43.0 and the half named here did not, because they are different problems.** Eighteen of the twenty-three uncosted offers were never missing data: EDSY splits a module type EDEngineer does not, and the largest is the **SCO drive**, whose eight blueprints were all offered with no recipe behind them — on the drive nearly every Commander flies. That is a join, it is settled by the corpus rather than by a resemblance (SCO drives rolling `FSD_LongRange` report `Modifiers` the ordinary drive's grade rows reproduce exactly), and it is closed. Anti-Guardian Zone Resistance is the other kind: EDEngineer costs it nowhere, and **FDevIDs has no row for any of its three materials**, so EDSY's keys cannot resolve to `Materials.tsv` symbols at all — the criterion above cannot be met from the sources d47 has, and a recipe whose ingredients cannot be keyed cannot be costed, gathered or put on a checklist. The generator now names every remaining gap on each run. See [docs/spikes/build-gauges.md](docs/spikes/build-gauges.md) §4.
  - [x] Ask before the plan and the checklist drift apart - Boarding a ship whose build carries engineering the checklist has not got is the moment to say so, yes or no. Accepted when it fires on a ship swap and only where the two actually disagree; **no** leaves both untouched and does not ask again for that same difference; **yes** revises rather than rebuilds, so the ordering the Commander spent an evening on survives — which `ChecklistDocument.Revise` already guarantees and this must not route around. The prompt is the act of accepting, exactly as pressing *Put this build on my checklist* is; it promotes nothing on its own. **Both routes** (the Commander's call, 2026-08-20): spoken as the ship is boarded, because that is the moment and the Commander is in the cockpit rather than at a window; and left on the Ships tab as a banner with the two answers, for one who was busy flying. The no is remembered **on the build** rather than in memory, so a restart does not re-ask — and a difference that *changes* is a new question and is asked.
  - [x] What this phase does not do - **No damage, shields or DPS**, which are a build's other axes and are not what was asked for; **not an outfitting simulator**, because a build needing more than two numbers belongs in Coriolis and d47 should not pretend otherwise; and **no tool surface spent** — whether the model may ask for a power budget is a separate and cheaper decision to make later.

- [x] **Phase 39 — The checklist in the headset**
  - [x] The Checklist tab is furnished on the VR panel again - What the Commander is working on, readable in a headset, where it cannot be read any other way. Phase 25 made it reachable there — which a `Window` cannot be — and it was **withdrawn on the Commander's instruction** during the panel redesign. This reverses half of that: **Loadout stays withdrawn** unless asked for separately, and bringing one tab back is not a step toward parity, which remains a someday-maybe. Plan of record in [docs/plans/phase-39-the-checklist-in-the-headset.md](docs/plans/phase-39-the-checklist-in-the-headset.md).
  - [x] The code change is one call, and that is by design - The withdrawal was done by **not calling** `EnableChecklist` rather than by hiding anything: a tab nobody furnishes has no builder, registers no root, and `PanelView.Tab` already declines to select it, so the spoken route and the drawn one agree with no special case. `VrPanelSurface` still takes `checklists`, `goals` and `backfillGoals` from `AppHost` and leaves them unused against exactly this day. Accepted when the tab appears in the headset, the spoken route reaches it, Loadout still does not, and the comment recording the withdrawal is updated rather than deleted — it explains a real decision. **The one call was one call, and it was not the work.** Furnishing it took the line the constructor had been kept ready for since the withdrawal, and everything after that was what a headset does differently from a monitor — including two defects nobody could have found any other way, both recorded below.
  - [x] It reads at overlay resolution, and a ray can hit it - The panel renders once and is sampled by the compositor, so a secondary line that reads at 100% on a monitor can be unreadable through a lens; and rows sized for a pointer at a desk are a different target at arm's length. The checklist is a long list of short rows, which is the worst case, and it is the first VR tab needing much scrolling. Accepted when a capture at the overlay's own size is legible and the Commander confirms on the headset, every interactive target meets the size the VR pages already settled on, and a long list can be scrolled to its end without the ray leaving the panel. **Measured rather than eyeballed:** the big panel is 1024 pixels across a 1.1 m quad at 1.1 m, which is 53° and 19 pixels to the degree, so the muted second line stood 29 arcminutes tall against a floor of about 20 — over it, and not by enough to also be the lowest-contrast text on the tab, so it went up one step to Body and kept its colour. Shared, not conditioned on the surface. Four controls — *Edit*, *Delete* and the two movers — turned out to be about twenty pixels tall, below the floor every other button on the page already stood on, and now carry it by name. **And the list could not be scrolled to its end at all:** the host window's arrange hands a scroll viewer a viewport 128 pixels taller than its real one, an offset is clamped down to fit a viewport and never let back out, so every frame dragged the document back up — the last three lines of a long checklist were unreachable, and the transcript's *newest* had been landing just short of the newest on every surface `OffscreenSurface` draws. Held across the pass and re-arranged, with the bar told where the document actually went.
  - [x] Ticking works from the headset, refusals included - A derived item cannot be ticked by hand and the reason has to be readable there too. Accepted when an authored item ticks, a derived one refuses with its sentence, and neither needs the desktop window. **No VR-only behaviour**: one view definition renders to both surfaces, and a tab that grew its own behaviour in the headset would be the second UI codebase the invariant exists to prevent. **The tick was drawing and doing nothing.** A press through the panel toggled the box and never raised `Click`, which is the event a checklist line's handler hangs off — so a line ticked in the headset showed a tick and stayed open until the next rebuild rubbed it out. No checkbox on any other VR page carries behaviour on `Click`, which is how it survived Phase 25 unnoticed. A real release raises both; this one now does too. The refusal needed no new mechanism: a derived line has no checkbox at all and carries its sentence on the row, which is the same row in both surfaces.

- [x] **Phase 40 — Emissions worth dropping for**
  - [x] Say when a system might be running High Grade Emissions - Asked for 2026-08-21: *"Notifies me when I am in a system that has a chance of having High Grade Emissions available, and what the material(s) is/are."* Said on arrival, because that is the only moment it can be acted on — a Commander who has jumped out cannot, and one who is about to has a decision in front of them. Accepted when arriving somewhere qualifying names the materials, arriving somewhere ordinary says nothing, and the same system is not said twice. **Priming says nothing**: the backlog replays a whole session of jumps and only the last could still be acted on.
  - [x] The trigger table is the Commander's - **Two derived readings shipped wrong before it.** The wiki's *"individual Faction States and the allegiance of the controlling faction"* was first read as both-per-faction, which announced Federal composites in **Oppi** — Independent, one Federal faction of seven, none controlling; then as allegiance-per-system with states still per faction, which was still half wrong. The table given on 2026-08-21 supersedes both: every row is **allegiance + population over a million**, with a **controlling faction** state on four of them, and the four state rows are **Independent** — so **Alliance yields nothing**, and **Expansion** counts beside Boom, reversing that morning's Boom-only ruling. **Several materials at once survives**, from one controlling faction wearing two states — the corpus has CivilUnrest+Expansion, Expansion+War and Boom+Expansion among them. Accepted when every one of the ten rows is asserted by name. **Superseded prose kept nowhere:** the old reading is gone from the rules, the tests and the docs rather than left standing beside code that no longer obeys it. The wiki says contents depend on *"individual Faction States **and the allegiance of the controlling faction**"* — states individual, allegiance the system's — and the following sentence about signals being assigned to a faction governs the state half only. Making both per-faction had d47 announce Federal composites in **Oppi**, an Independent system holding one Federal faction out of seven, none of them controlling anything; reported 2026-08-21. The **84 of 400** figure quoted in support of the per-faction reading is real and argues the opposite: a minority superpower faction sits in roughly a fifth of populated systems, so the wrong reading mislabelled about a fifth of the galaxy. **Several materials at once never depended on it** — that comes from two factions in different *states*, which is untouched, and is the case the Commander asked for by name. Accepted when Oppi says nothing and a system with one faction in Boom and another in Outbreak names both groups.
  - [x] Sourced, corroborated, and checked against a table that already ships - **The blocker was never the mechanism, it was the table**, and hand-writing it is the one thing `CLAUDE.md` forbids about game data. Three sources: the Elite Dangerous Wiki's *High Grade Emissions* page, which alone states the rank-order mechanic; the Frontier Forums guide *Unidentified Signal Sources: A Complete Guide* (2017, edited 2018), which is the primary community research the rest descends from; and `edgalaxy.net/hge`, whose six filter groups over live EDDN detections are behavioural corroboration rather than a second copy of the prose. And then a fourth that was already here: **`Materials.tsv` carries these conditions in its own origins column**, generated by `tools/gen-materials.py`, for all ten materials — so `EmissionRules` is asserted against the shipped table material for material, and a regenerated table that disagrees fails rather than drifting. Accepted when that assertion passes both ways: no rule without a table row, and no table row without a rule.
  - [x] Four readings the sources disagree on, settled and recorded - The two prose sources contradict each other in four places, and a table that hides that is one nobody can check later. Settled by the Commander, 2026-08-21: Proprietary Composites **is** included beside Core Dynamics Composites; the Proto materials are **Boom only**, not Expansion or Investment; the million-population floor applies to **every** group rather than to Outbreak alone; and **superpower beats state** — a Federal or Imperial faction yields composites or shielding and nothing else, whatever state it is in. That last is the load-bearing one: the 2017 guide says the opposite in as many words, and the wiki's reading was taken because it is newer and because it is the one consistent with the rank-order mechanic the same page states. Accepted when each ruling is pinned by a test naming it as a ruling rather than as behaviour — the fault was reintroduced on the fourth, and three tests went red.
  - [x] Nothing said about a material there is no room for - *"Skips if I'm already full of that material."* A material at capacity is dropped and a group entirely at capacity says nothing at all, so a Commander who has finished gathering stops hearing it without switching anything off — which is what earns the row its default of on. Capacity is injected exactly as `MaterialMilestoneCallout` takes it, from the same derived grade table. **An unknown capacity means say it**, which is the opposite of the milestone callout's choice and deliberate: there an unknown means a percentage that would have to be invented, here it means only that d47 cannot prove the Commander is full, and silence on that basis withholds something true.
  - [x] A row of its own in settings - Like every other callout, separately switchable and reachable by voice, because finding one chatty is not a reason to lose another. Protected, like the rest of them.

- [x] **Phase 41 — Limpets before you leave**
  - [x] Remind me to buy limpets when I dock somewhere that sells them - Asked for 2026-08-21: *"When I land at a station that offers limpets, and I have more than X cargo capacity, and I am under Y% limpets, remind me to grab limpets — where X and Y (and the feature itself) are settings in the settings page."* Accepted when a big hold with no limpets is reminded at a station that sells them, and a small hold, a full rack, a station that does not, and the priming backlog are all silent.
  - [x] Limpets are not a commodity, and that is the fact everything else rests on - They are bought through **Advanced Maintenance** — the Commander's correction, 2026-08-21, and confirmed at the pad: Reiter City's commodity market, open on screen, listed **356 items and not one limpet**. It is also why Elite writes `BuyDrones` rather than `MarketBuy`, and why there is not one `MarketBuy` for drones in 920 journals. So `Market.json` cannot answer whether a station sells them, however fresh it is — and a first design built on reading it was wrong for a reason no amount of care about staleness would have fixed.
  - [x] The `rearm` service is the signal, measured against real purchases - For each of the **136** `BuyDrones` in the corpus with a known docking before it, the station's `StationServices` were read. `rearm` is present at **133 of 136**, and all three misses are the same fleet carrier. `repair` is not the flag — absent at two `CraterOutpost` purchases where `rearm` is present. `commodities` is present at all 136 and is useless as a filter, being present at 3,741 of 3,779 dockings anyway. **An earlier reading rejected `rearm` because it appears at 95% of dockings**; that mistook a true observation for a false positive rate. Most stations really do sell limpets, and the 188 without it are on-foot settlements, small outposts and carriers. What makes the reminder rare is the other two conditions, which is where it always belonged.
  - [x] The carrier miss is accepted rather than special-cased - The Commander's ruling, 2026-08-21, choosing the simplest of three offered: use `rearm` everywhere and accept being silent at their own carrier. A known three-in-136, written into the callout and into the docs so it is a recorded gap rather than a bug report waiting to happen.
  - [x] The hold comes from a file, because the journal cannot say - **Measured rather than assumed:** not one of the 3,781 dockings in the corpus is followed within fifteen seconds by a `Cargo` event carrying an `Inventory`, and only 4% are followed by any `Cargo` event at all. `Cargo.json` is the only source that knows what is aboard at the pad. **It was already here** — `CargoHold` and `CargoManifestReader` have read that file since Phase 18 for colonisation, `JournalSpine` already polls it, and `CommanderGameState.Hold` is already kept current. A duplicate reader was written before that was noticed and is not in the tree; the lesson is the cheaper one, that this repo's answer to a new need is usually already in it.
  - [x] Y is a percentage of cargo capacity, and the row says so - The Commander's ruling, 2026-08-21. X defaults to **64** tonnes and Y to **5%**, so twelve limpets in a 256 tonne hold is low and thirteen is not. The denominator is in the row's help text in as many words, because a percentage whose denominator is not written down is a number nobody can set confidently — and it is the thing the tests assert by name rather than merely exercise. Compared as a multiplication rather than a division, so no rounding is invented.
  - [x] No price is quoted - Reported as *"always the same price"*, and it very nearly is: across 148 purchases it is 101 (×105), 95 (×36), 82 (×5) and one each of 201, 97 and 91. It does not affect the condition, and it is the reason the line names no cost — d47 has no reading of *this* station's limpet price at the pad, since `BuyDrones` only fires after buying.
  - [x] Off by default - Unlike most callouts. It is for Commanders who fly limpets, and one who never does should not have to switch it off to stop hearing it. Phase 32's habit callout is the precedent for defaulting off with a reason.

- [x] **Phase 42 — The checklist in the order you care about**
  - [x] The order today is the order things arrived - Asked for 2026-08-21: *"When you have 600+ items in your checklist, you really only care about the next few items at the top — not the random order that D47 has put them in (it seems random, I know it's not totally)."* **It is insertion order and nothing else.** `ChecklistDocument.In(scope)` returns everything live in a scope *in the order it was added*; `ChecklistPage` filters `document.Items` and sorts nothing; and there is no `OrderBy` over lines anywhere in Core or the panel — the ones in `ChecklistService` order material ingredients and filter words, which are not the list. `ChecklistItem` carries no timestamp, so even *recently added* is unavailable: position in the file **is** the order. At `MaxLiveItemsPerScope` of 200 across four or more scopes that is 600-odd lines of chronological sediment, where a build promotion drops forty contiguous items wherever the Commander happened to be that week and everything since layers on top. Not random — worse, arbitrary in a way that looks like it might mean something.
  - [x] Projects are ordered by the Commander, and that order is stored - **A project needs no new vocabulary, because it is already modelled twice.** `ChecklistScope.Ship(id)` is a ship being outfitted and `ShipBuild` is one being acquired — `IsOwned` is exactly the difference between *finish outfitting the Python* and *buy the Cutter and kit it out*, which are the two the Commander named. Systems, suits and weapons are scopes already, and a Phase 34 arc is the fifth kind. What is new is a rank **between** them, authored the way item text is authored and surviving a restart. **Deliberately not a priority field on every item**: six hundred items is six hundred decisions and the maintenance stops in week two, whereas the ordering actually wanted is over a handful of projects. Get the project order from the Commander, get the line order from the game, and the six hundred sort themselves.
  - [x] Order within a project is derived on read, never stored - The rule `ChecklistItem` already states — **"No number is ever stored here"** — decides this: a stored priority score is precisely the figure that gets recited in October with nothing left to say where it came from. So the Commander's ranking persists and everything below it is recomputed. **The first derivation is *what can be done now, in the ship you are in, where you are standing*,** and every signal it needs exists: `ChecklistEvaluator.IsActive(scope, loadout)` already knows whether a ship-scoped line belongs to the ship being flown, `ContinuityCallout.Short()` already computes the nearest thing a plan is short of so actionable-versus-blocked is answerable, and scope against the current system is what `Here()` already does for the opening line. Open, and to be decided rather than drifted into: whether a project the Commander has never ranked sorts among the ranked ones by the same derivation or falls below all of them. **Decided in the build, 2026-08-21: below all of them.** Ranking one project has to mean *above everything I have not spoken for*, or ranking means nothing at this size — and a rank the derivation could overrule whenever the Commander flies somewhere is a control that lies. Among the unranked the derivation decides, here-and-now first; the first rank stores the whole sequence being looked at, so only a project arriving later is unranked, and it lands at the bottom where one press promotes it.
  - [x] The opening line becomes the top of the list, said out loud - `ContinuityCallout.Compose` assembles Gap, then Short, then `Here()` **or** `Unlock()` — a precedence invented independently of the checklist **because the checklist had no order to read from**. Once it has one the callout stops needing an opinion of its own and says the next few things. Asked for 2026-08-21: *"I don't always care about the next engineer to unlock. I do want to remember my checklist and how I've prioritized it."* The unlock clause then appears when an unlock genuinely is near the top of *that Commander's* list and not otherwise, which answers the complaint structurally instead of by deleting a clause somebody will want back. **The Here-over-Unlock ruling of 2026-08-20 survives intact**: what is under the Commander's feet is an errand and still outranks a project one unlock step away.
  - [x] What this phase does not do - **No general priority system, no scoring, no weights, no due dates.** Each is a knob that has to be maintained by hand for a list this size, and a knob nobody turns is a knob that lies. It also does not reorder the file: the stored document keeps its own order and the ordering is a reading of it, so a hand edit still lands where the Commander put it.

- [x] **Phase 43 — A Commander with a past**
  - [x] About Me reaches the conversation and nothing else - Asked for 2026-08-21, of the *About Me* box: is it treated *"like a backstory rather than three things not to forget"*? **Neither, today.** It reaches exactly two places — `PromptAssembly` position 4, rendered as *"About the Commander, in their own words:"* above the cache breakpoint, and `LogPrompt` for the Commander's log. It reaches **no flavour line at all**: `FlavourTurn.AskAsync` takes provider, model, persona, instruction and game state, and there is no parameter for it and no call site that could pass one. So every ambient remark, the session's opening line, the carrier's lines and a core's introduction are written by a model that has never heard of the person flying — which is the real reason those remarks feel generic, and why no number of stock lines fixes them. Ten ways to say *docked at a station* are still ten ways to say nothing. **Built 2026-08-21**: `AskAsync` takes `aboutMe` beside `persona`, and every one of its six call sites states what it passes — null for the carrier, the two lore lookups and the voice casting question, which are not the ship's AI speaking to the Commander. The log gets both halves, because a log is written once about a whole evening and the rule below has nothing to save there.
  - [x] A third context flag on the brief, not a new mechanism - `FlavourBrief` already declares `NeedsPersona` and `NeedsGameState` per brief, so this is the same shape a third time. **The carrier's tower does not get it**, for the reason it already gets `NeedsPersona: false` — a stranger on a comms channel does not know the Commander's history, and putting one of them in two places at once is the thing the isolation model cannot survive. The two flags will correlate and must not be merged: they answer different questions. **Built**: `NeedsAboutMe`, with `NeedsStory` beside it for the depth; the carrier's brief declares both false and a test names that by role. The ambient remarks, the opening line and a core's first words all carry the sheet — a greeting that knows the Commander's name is the line where it matters most.
  - [x] The sheet always, the story sometimes - A real biography has two halves that want opposite handling. A character sheet — name, origin, year, build, accent — is some forty tokens, stable, and relevant to nearly anything said. A backstory runs to thirteen hundred and is relevant *occasionally*: when the Commander docks somewhere that means something to them. In a turn the distinction barely matters because position 4 is cached; **in a flavour call there is no such shelter**, and thirteen hundred tokens carried to produce one sentence about a docking bay would make ambient remarks the most expensive thing d47 does. So the sheet goes every time and the story goes sometimes — chosen by the same caller-supplied index `AmbientLines` already uses, **deliberately not random**, because no Core component reads a clock or a seed and a recorded session has to replay to the line it produced live. The added per-call cost is measured and recorded rather than assumed. **Built and measured 2026-08-21.** The field is split into two rows: `llm.characterSheet` is new, and `llm.aboutMe` keeps its key and becomes the story — the settings file is append-only, so nothing is renamed and whatever a Commander had already written is their story. The index is `Announcement.Variant`, the count `AmbientCallout` picked the stock line with, carried on the announcement for the reason the situation travels on the key; `CommanderStory.StoryEvery` is **four**, which at the default interval is about once an hour, and only the ambient brief ever asks for the story because only it is written often enough for *sometimes* to mean anything. **The measurement**, against a 164-character sheet and a 5,193-character story, at the chars/4 rule the log quote uses — no key was reachable from the build session, so these are bytes converted and not a tokenizer's count: the label is ~76 tokens and the sheet ~41, so a sheet-only remark carries **~118 tokens more than before — 0.06¢** at claude-opus-5 list price — and a story call **~1,417 more, 0.71¢**. Over the cadence that is 0.22¢ per remark and under a cent an hour. The base prompt is ~1,055 tokens of guardrails and preamble before the core's own block, so the sheet is about an eleventh of a remark and a story call more than doubles it, which is the figure that justifies *sometimes* rather than *always*. The label costs more than the sheet, and that is accepted: it is the part that tells the model what the sheet is.
  - [x] Position 4 is relabelled to commit, not to hedge - The label is neutral today and the model is told nothing about what kind of thing it is reading. A Commander's biography asserts things about named in-game figures that are their own invention rather than Frontier's lore, and **the answer is not a disclaimer**: it is their character in their game and the field takes whatever they write. The instruction should say this is the Commander's own story and is true of the world they share, so the ship's AI inhabits it — an AI that speaks of a Power with suspicion because its Commander has reason to is the feature, not a leak. Operational answers are unaffected: those come from tools and tables rather than from a prompt block. **Built**: `PromptAssembly.AboutMeLabel` — *true of the world you share … never question it, hedge it or call it fiction, and do not recite it back*. One label for the turn, the log and every flavour line, so the ship's AI is the same AI in all three; a test asserts the old neutral wording is gone.
  - [x] Independent of Phase 44 - About Me is the most per-Commander thing in the settings file and is currently in the least per-Commander place, which Phase 44 fixes. **Neither phase blocks the other**: the flavour wiring here is orthogonal to where the field is stored, and doing this one first means the story reaches the AI for whichever Commander is flying today. The new `CharacterSheet` field is per Commander in exactly the way `AboutMe` is, and joins it in Phase 44's overlay with the same rule — empty is meaningful.

- [x] **Phase 44 — Several Commanders, one installation**
  - [x] Two ship stores collide across Commanders, and that is a defect rather than a feature request - Elite's `ShipID` is **per Commander and starts small**, so one Commander's ship 7 and another's ship 7 are the same row in two stores that carry no Commander at all: `ShipCoreBinding(int ShipId, …)` and `ShipBuild(Id, Hull, int? ShipId, …)`. Bind a core under one Commander and the other's ship 7 answers to it; `ShipDriftWatch` then compares one Commander's build against the other's actual ship and asks about a drift that is not real. **The correct precedent is next door and was followed everywhere else**: `ChecklistScope.Ship(int)` is ship-id keyed too, but `ChecklistDocument` carries `CommanderFid`, so a checklist is per Commander *and* per ship. These two missed it. Fixable on its own and worth doing ahead of the rest of the phase, since every other item here assumes those two stores tell two Commanders apart. **Built 2026-08-21 in v0.47.1**, ahead of the rest: `ShipCoreBinding` and `ShipBuild` carry `CommanderFid` inside the document, rows from before the key are adopted whole by the first Commander seen, and build ids stay unique across the file. `OnFootBuildStore` was checked against the corpus and left fid-free on purpose — `SuitID` comes from one global counter, eighteen ids across three FIDs with no collision. The live half — `_aboard` as a bare int — waited for the switch signal below and shipped with it.
  - [x] The split is per row and per store, declared rather than inferred - Already right and not to be touched: `SamplingStore`, `MemoryStore`, `GoalStore`, `HabitStore`, `ChecklistStore` and `LoreStore` are keyed per Commander **with the key inside the document**, because a Frontier id comes out of the journal and turning untrusted input into a filename buys a path-traversal surface for an organisational convenience. **`SpendLedger` is the counter-example and is deliberately installation-wide** — `SpendEntry` carries no Commander and one ledger is constructed for the process, because the running cost of the AI is the person's and not the character's. The Commander's ruling, 2026-08-21. It is exactly the store a per-Commander sweep would convert without thinking, which is why the scope is **declared on each row and each store the way `Protected` already is**, never assumed by default. `D47Settings` is one record in one file: keys, devices, theme, zoom and hotkeys are the installation's; `llm.aboutMe` is the Commander describing themselves and `Persona.ShipCoreShip` is a ship id, and both are per Commander in a per-installation file. Layering an overlay over installation defaults has to keep *unset* and *deliberately blank* apart — for About Me, empty is meaningful. **Built 2026-08-21**: `SettingRow.Scope` (`Install` | `Commander`), declared on four rows — `llm.aboutMe`, `llm.characterSheet`, `persona.shipCoreShip`, and `persona.shipCore`, whose value is in the store keyed per Commander — and rendered as a *per Commander* pill beside *protected*. The overlay is `D47Settings.Commanders`, one `CommanderSettings` per Frontier id with the id inside the document; `CommanderScope.Project` is the read, `Persist` the write, and `SettingsService.Current` is the projection, so no reader learns there are two layers. Null in the overlay is unset and reads through to the installation's; empty is deliberately blank and reads as nothing. Nobody flying means the installation's, and nothing migrates out of that — the `MemoryStore.NoCommander` rule. `CommanderScopeTests` holds the declared rows against the fields the overlay reaches, in both directions. `SpendLedger` untouched.
  - [x] One switch signal, and it says whether it happened during priming - **The detection point already exists**: `GameStateStore` keeps a bucket per Frontier id and assigns `_activeFrontierId` on `Commander` and `LoadGame`, per Commander since Phase 2 and with no path from one bucket's events into another's. That assignment is silent; making it raise is the whole of the plumbing. **The trap is priming.** The journal backlog is folded through the same `Apply`, so at startup the active id flips every time the replay crosses into an older journal belonging to somebody else — and a naive event fires repeatedly at launch, discarding transcripts for logins that happened last month. `ContinuityCallout` is already immune through its own `IsPriming` gate, but that is its gate and not the signal's, so **the event carries the flag and every subscriber honours it**. Reading the id per call rather than capturing it, the way `GoalBook` does, is correct for a pure reader and does not generalise: a transcript cannot be discarded by a function that reads an id. **Built 2026-08-21**: `GameStateStore.CommanderChanged` carries `CommanderSwitch(Previous, Current, Priming)`, and `JournalSpine.Poll(priming)` passes the tick's first-tick flag through. Raised with the new Commander already active and before their identity event is folded, so a reset subscriber sees their events arrive after it. The settings projection subscribes before the priming tick and follows every reassignment — it is the pure reader — and the host's handler honours the flag and does nothing for a replayed switch.
  - [x] What a new Commander logging in actually changes - The Commander's ruling, 2026-08-21: *"The old transcript goes away, a new one is created. New ship, new AI."* So the working transcript is discarded — `ShipCoreService` already discards one when the core aboard changes, which is the precedent — and the core is re-resolved. **The live half needs more than the store fix**: `ShipCoreService` keeps `_aboard`, the ship it has already acted on, as a bare int, so two Commanders both flying ship 7 read as *no change* and the wrong core stays aboard even once the store is keyed correctly; and `_started` left set makes the new Commander's first ship read as a swap, spending a gap reaction and a model round trip on a boarding that never happened. **`null` to A is adoption and discards nothing** — d47 runs before Elite has said who is flying, which is why `MemoryStore.NoCommander` is a real key — and A to B is a switch. Nothing migrates out of `NoCommander`: entries typed before anyone was identified are not retroactively attributed to whoever logged in first. **Built 2026-08-21**: `AppHost.OnCommanderChanged` — `PersonaHost.ForgetTranscripts` (every core's, as new lists, then `TurnLoop.UseTranscript` pointed at the fresh one), `ShipCoreService.Reset` and `ShipDriftWatch.Reset` so the next tick adopts their ship silently through the store keyed per Commander, and a mark on the panel. `ShipDriftWatch._asked` turned out to be a third bare-id site and is dropped by the same reset — without it the old Commander's outstanding question settles against the new one's build and records a *no* they never gave. Adoption and replayed switches log and return.
  - [x] Welcome back, Commander - The Commander's observation, 2026-08-21: logging in is an event the journal reports, so the switch never has to be inferred from ship state. **The line already exists.** `ContinuityCallout` is Phase 31's opening line, assembled in Core with no model in the path and then put through `FlavourBriefs`, so the core says it in its own words and personality-off says it plainly — which makes the greeting and the new ship AI's first words the same sentence. Its gate is `_said`, *"said once per run of d47, whatever happens next"*, and this phase moves that from **once per process to once per session, where a login starts a session**. It already handles a Commander d47 has never seen by saying nothing rather than manufacturing continuity out of an empty store. **One amendment to that rule, stated rather than slipped in:** a switch is answering *who is speaking now* rather than *what were we doing*, so it names the Commander even when there is nothing else to say. **Built 2026-08-21**: `ContinuityCallout.Rearm` clears `_said`, restarts the settle window from the next live tick, and the line after a switch is *Good evening, Commander Jameson. Ready to go.* — the launch greeting is unchanged. A relog by the same Commander raises no switch and so no second greeting: only a login by somebody else starts a session here.
  - [x] The prompt cache dies on a switch, and that is correct - About Me sits above the breakpoint, so changing Commander invalidates the whole cached prefix by construction. Recorded here as a known cost rather than left to be found as a mystery latency spike, and **not to be optimised around**: it happens once per switch and a switch is rare. **Recorded 2026-08-21**: `SettingsService.UseCommander` announces each Commander row that moved under its own key, so About Me reaches position 4 through `ApplyLlmSettings` exactly as an edit does, and that rebuild is the cache dying. Nothing defers or batches it.

- [x] **Phase 45 — One transcript, both surfaces**
  - [x] The transcript view is shared, and the way it is drawn is not - Asked for 2026-08-21: *"I want the Windows UI transcript selection to be echoed in VR and vice-versa."* This **reverses a stated decision** rather than filling a gap: `TranscriptPage` is per-surface today, *"a property of the surface, exactly like `PanelMode` and for the same reason"*, on the grounds that a shared page would send the headset to the log file the moment the window went there. The Commander's ruling is that this is the point. The principle that keeps the reversal from being arbitrary: **what you are reading is shared, how it is drawn is per-surface** — so `PanelMode` and zoom stay exactly as they are. The precedent already leans this way: `AppHost.RouteNavigation` moves **every** navigator for a spoken phrase, because a phrase has no surface attached to it, and the two surfaces are described as agreeing about where they are for as long as neither is driven separately. This makes the agreement unconditional. **Built 2026-08-22**: `TranscriptMirror` in Core, over the navigators rather than as a new piece of state — the page is still read from each surface's own navigator, and the mirror keeps the Transcript root agreeing across them, either direction, no preferred surface. `PanelMode` and zoom are untouched. `TranscriptPage`'s doc records the reversal in the Commander's words rather than quietly rewriting the reason it used to give.
  - [x] The mirror is total, because both surfaces furnish all three roots - `PanelView.Nav` is a `new()` per view, but `AppHost` already collects every navigator for the spoken route, so the fan-out point exists rather than needing inventing. VR builds its copy through the same constructor, so Conversation, Technical and Log file are registered on both and there is no destination one surface has and the other lacks — **no *except* case**. Two things to get right: **guard re-entrancy explicitly**, because A changing sets B, which raises `Changed`, which sets A, and today that terminates only by luck; and **do not stack two mechanisms**, since `AppHost.Navigate`'s loop over every navigator would otherwise be maintaining the same invariant as the mirror, and two mechanisms holding one invariant eventually disagree about it. **Built 2026-08-22**: `AppHost.RouteNavigation` adds each navigator to the mirror as it routes it, so the fan-out point is the one that already existed, and a surface routed after the other has moved arrives on the shared root rather than a step behind. The echo is stopped by a flag the mirror holds while a move of its own making raises `Changed`; direction is decided by what each navigator was last known to be on rather than by comparing the two, so a surface whose chooser refused the move is *behind* — caught up on its next change — and never a second source dragging the other back. One mechanism: `Navigate` and `Show` still reach every navigator, because tabs are per surface and a phrase or a switch has no surface, but they *initiate* the way a press on the mode control does — the first navigator they reach raises `Changed`, the mirror carries the root to the rest, and the loop is declined by each of those as already there. Neither loop propagates anything, and the Phase 46 reconcile code did not change. The mirror moves navigators in the handler rather than posting: every surface is on the window's thread (architecture.md D1), and two posted moves in flight could cross and each read the other's arrival as a fresh change.
  - [x] Scoped to the transcript, and not to the whole of navigation - Mirroring tabs and drill trails as well would be more consistent with *the two surfaces agree about where they are* — and it acquires the *except* the transcript does not have, because Settings is desktop-only and Loadout is withdrawn from VR. It degrades quietly rather than breaking (`PanelView.Tab` already declines to select a tab nobody furnished) but a rule that holds *except sometimes* is the kind people misremember. Ship the total one and let real use say whether the headset lagging a tab behind is annoying enough to pay for the exception. **Built 2026-08-22**: the mirror reads and writes `RootKeyOf(PanelTab.Transcript)` and nothing else. A test holds the window three levels into a checklist while the headset reads the conversation and asserts the transcript root still follows and the tab and trail do not.

- [x] **Phase 46 — Switches that reach d47 itself**
  - [x] A switch position may name a panel destination - Asked for 2026-08-21: a spare three-position toggle on the throttle base, nothing bound to it, flipping the Transcript between Conversation, Technical and the log file. **The fit is exact** — three detents, three roots — and the phase is one field rather than a subsystem, because Phase 21 built the whole capture and reconcile path and Phase 45 makes one navigator move both surfaces. **Both reasons Phase 21 closed its vocabulary are artefacts of the target being Elite.** `SwitchValidation.Assignable` admits only the ten actions with a `Reports` flag, because an action that cannot be asked *are you already there* could only press — and d47 knows what page it is on with certainty rather than inferring it from `GameStatus`, so the *position means a state* rule works better here than where it was invented. To be decided rather than inherited: whether spring-return controls and hats, declined by the walk today because a momentary control desyncs, are admitted for d47-side targets where there is nothing to desync with. **Built 2026-08-22**: `SwitchPosition.Destination`, the key of a root some surface registered, and `SwitchReconciler.ReconcilePanel`, which follows the game path's rule exactly — the flip is the question, *are you already there* is asked of `SwitchTick.Showing` and answered exactly, and between flips nothing is touched — with no contest watch, because nothing drives the panel behind d47's back. `Showing` is what every surface agrees it is showing and null when they differ, and **null is *unknown*, never *unchanged***: the flip goes out and each surface declines for itself if it is already there. Built ahead of Phase 45, so `AppHost.Show` moves every surface the way `Navigate` does for a phrase; the mirror collapses both loops to one and this code does not change. **Spring-return controls and hats stay declined, for a page as much as for an action** — decided rather than inherited, for three reasons: the walk happens before the target is known, so admitting them would put a momentary/maintained classification into the capture and a second rule into the validator to keep it d47-only; a momentary control can only mean a press, and a press is the spoken route, which already reaches every page; and the value of the phase is the fit the Commander described — three detents, three roots — which a maintained switch gives and a button does not.
  - [x] The target is declared, never a prefixed string - `NavCrumb.Level` already records the reasoning: a statement by the caller rather than a rule about string prefixes that a rename would silently break. The destination vocabulary is derived from the roots each surface registered, so there is no second list to keep in step with the spoken route — the invariant `PanelNavigation` opens with — and it inherits `PanelView.Tab` declining to select a tab nobody furnished. **Built 2026-08-22**: its own field in `switches.json` — `{ "button": 5, "destination": "transcript.technical" }` — and a position that names both an action and a destination is refused as meaning two things. `PanelNavigator.Destinations` is every root of every tab in bar order, `PanelDestination` is the root with its tab, and `PanelNavigator.Show(key)` arrives on the tab and the mode together, refusing a root nobody furnished and a chooser holding the panel. `AppHost` unions the surfaces' lists for the editor's page control and for the tick's vocabulary; a key in the file that no surface answers to is reported on the row by name, the way an action bound to no key is, and never moved to.
  - [x] Not behind the key-injection gate, and still not on the tool surface - `Automation.Switches` is off by default and gated by `Automation.Keyboard` because **a switch that reconciles the ship reaches the keyboard**. One that changes which page is drawn presses nothing, reads no binds and checks no foreground, so it does not belong behind that row and must not silently acquire it. Assignment stays reachable from the panel only — Phase 21's rule, unchanged, because a hostile in-game message must not be able to remap a switch. `PendingReconcile` already documents `Steps` as *empty when this is only something to say*, so a non-pressing outcome is expressible without widening the type. **Built 2026-08-22**: `ReconcilePanel` runs before the `Enabled` check rather than behind it, and a test holds a page flip to a queued move with switches and key injection both off. `PendingReconcile.Destination` carries the key with `Steps` empty; `AppHost.CarryOutReconciles` sends those to each surface on its own thread, outside `_acting`, because a page move holds no keys for a honk to collide with, and only then takes the semaphore for whatever is left. Each surface registers a poster captured on its own thread alongside its navigator — `RouteNavigation(nav, post)`, the headless-dispatcher rule — and the tick reads a snapshot the navigators' own thread retakes on every move, never the navigators themselves. Assignment is still `SwitchWindow` and nothing else; `report_switches` is unchanged and still reports only.

- [x] **Phase 47 — Adventures**
  - [x] A story the Commander progresses through, tracked from their own journals - Asked for 2026-08-21: *"Lay out a story-based adventure that the commander progresses through. Progress is tracked via events in the commander's journals."* **The furthest-reaching item on the list and the one to attempt last.** The scaffolding is mostly built: Phase 34's `GoalStore` and `GoalBook` hold named arcs with a definition of done and how long they have been running, `ChecklistProposals` is the accept path so agreeing stays the Commander's act, and three backfills already exist. An adventure is an arc with a narrative and an authored definition rather than a computed one. **Built 2026-08-22**: `Adventure` — a name, a source, a spine, an opening and an ordered list of beats — in `data/adventures.json`, the sixth copy of the `GoalStore` shape, keyed per Commander and hand-editable. `AdventureBook` reads it, folds the journal after each story's acceptance, and is the one thing the tick, the prompt and the tab all read; `AdventureCallout` is the path the live journal reaches it by, and the Adventures tab — its own tab, desktop-first — is where one is written, asked for, read, begun, abandoned and removed. **The governing framing arrived after the plan and reshaped it**: *story, not a checklist*, in the sense of *Story Genius*, *The Emotional Craft of Fiction* and *Save the Cat!* — so every adventure carries a spine (premise, want, stake, turn, ending), a beat is a dramatic function with a title anchored to a place rather than a stop, length is a structure rather than a count, and no count reaches the Commander anywhere they read.
  - [x] The trigger vocabulary is closed and the prose is free - The rule macros and switches already follow, for the same reason: the file is hand-editable, so what it can say is checked rather than trusted, and **a step naming an event that does not exist is refused by name with the reason** rather than becoming an adventure that silently can never finish. **Triggers match structured fields and never free text** — a ship name and an in-game message are attacker-controlled, and the journal is untrusted input; a step firing on a system address is a field comparison and carries none of that exposure. **Built 2026-08-22**: five kinds and nothing else — arrive (`FSDJump`/`Location`/`CarrierJump` on `SystemAddress`), dock (`Docked` on `MarketID`, never the name, because a carrier's is player-chosen), land and scan (`Touchdown`/`Scan` on `SystemAddress` and `BodyID`) and rank (`Promotion` on a career and an integer). **The vocabulary is the event catalogue**: Core has none and a catalogue of Frontier's schema written by hand would be a table of theirs. Every match is an integer comparison on a structured field; a file naming a sixth kind is refused by name with the five, and the editor's chooser *is* the vocabulary, so the form cannot compose what the validator refuses — `MacroWindow`'s guarantee from the panel side.
  - [x] Progress is derived, and an adventure counts forward only - Phase 34's rule, with one wrinkle its arcs do not have: an arc is cumulative and order-free, an adventure is sequential. **Adventures start when accepted and mine no history.** It sidesteps the backfill problem entirely, keeps progress honest, and makes the first beat a beginning rather than an audit — and Phase 34 has already been bitten here, where one Frontier id in the corpus reports Trade 7 in July, 2 in January and 0 in June because an id is an *account* and a person can begin a new Commander inside one. **Built 2026-08-22**: one stamp, `AcceptedAt`, and one pure fold, `AdventureFold.Apply`, for the live tick and the startup catch-up alike; only the current beat can match, a later place reached early is not banked, and nothing before the stamp counts. **Commanders play with d47 closed**, which is the reason progress is derived rather than stored: a stored beat pointer would miss every beat that fired while it was not running, permanently, where a walk over the files since the earliest acceptance — bounded to those files and the one before — finds them at the next launch. The catch-up leaves a high-water mark per Commander so the priming replay cannot count a beat twice. **Proved on the corpus**: five real places one Commander went on 21–26 June 2026 fire in order at their recorded timestamps, and the same five flown before the stamp fire nothing. Abandon stamps `AbandonedAt` and stops the fold; *Begin again* resets the stamp and starts from the opening — no resumable dead span, which would be two kinds of time in one record.
  - [x] Written, generated or imported, and each records how it arrived - Three provenances and three risk profiles. **Commander-authored** follows the checklist, macro and lore templates outright. **Generated by d47** is safe in the shape the Commander proposed — propose, accept, save, execute — which is `ChecklistProposals`' own shape and turns *the model authors triggers at runtime* into *the model authored this once and a person agreed*. It needs one thing validation cannot give: **a dry run before it is offered**, because an adventure can name only real event types and still be unsatisfiable — a system that is not there, a beat gated on a rank that cannot be reached second — and d47 has galaxy search, `RoutePlan` and `EliteSpecifications` to check that the places are real and in a sane order. **Downloaded from a site** is the sharp one and is deferred: it is stranger-authored prose spoken in the persona's voice and carried as standing context, it opens a new `EgressDisclosure` destination where Phase 23's web search opened none, and it raises a licensing question a derived data table does not. **Importing a file the Commander already has costs none of that** and is the version to build if the appetite is there. **Built 2026-08-22**: `AdventureSource.Commander` and `Generated`, with `WrittenBy` carrying the persona id; **Imported is deferred on the Commander's call and Downloaded on the list's**.
