Generation is a panel action and not a tool — 86 bytes of surface left, and nothing about an adventure is model-callable — running **two dedicated turns** with `FlavourTurn`'s bookkeeping: the spine first, asked for a story and not a route, then the beats against it, because a model asked for places and prose together writes an itinerary with adjectives. Then the dry run: `AdventureResolver` resolves every system, station and body to its id through the galaxy search (spansh carries `id64`, `market_id` and `body_id` on its ordinary searches, now read into the summaries), bounds every hop to the reach, checks pad size against the fleet, and holds a rank beat to the next one or two. A refused beat goes back through the turn once with the refusal as a remark, so the common case is the Commander never sees one. The Commander decides three things with defaults — reach, length as structure, and *this ship only* or *anything I own*, shown only when there is a choice — and may speak a brief; the fleet, the carrier, the ranks and the position are read, never asked. **Revision before acceptance**: a draft's *Change something* runs another turn with the brief, the draft, every remark so far and the position, the revised draft replaces the pending one with the previous kept for *Put it back*, and the core's reply is spoken. The stops may come from the Galactic Exploration Catalog on edastro.com behind its own row and its own egress entry, off by default — EDSM's own endpoint answers any non-browser client with a bot challenge, measured 2026-08-22 — fetched whole so nothing about the Commander goes with the request, and every suggested place is still held against spansh's id: two sources, and a disagreement refuses it.
  - [x] The ship's AI tells it, and the authored beat is the floor - `AmbientLines`' pattern exactly: the authored text plays when there is no model and personality-off says it plainly, and with a model the core says it in its own voice and the authored line is never heard. **The Commander's framing is the design and not a decoration** — *"treating the story as the commander's companion would throughout, rather than a sequence of events to be completed."* That makes adventure state **standing prompt context rather than an event that fires at gates**, so the core can notice the Commander is two jumps from where the story wants them while they are going somewhere else. It costs nothing in caching: game state already sits below the breakpoint and this sits with it. Beats take the callout family's discipline — a settle window so three gates in a minute are not three model calls, and a beat that arrives while the Commander is being interdicted is dropped rather than spoken late. **Built 2026-08-22**: `AdventureCallout` says a beat twenty seconds after it fires, drops one that comes due mid-danger rather than saying it late, and says the opening at once; `FlavourBriefs` gives it a rewording brief so the core says the line in its own voice keeping every fact and adding none, and the authored line plays with no model or personality off. **Standing context** sits below the breakpoint beside the game state: who wrote it, the premise, the want and the stake, the beats reached so far with the last line, and the current beat — **and the persona knows what the Commander knows, plus the stake**: the turn and the ending enter the block only once their beats have fired and the beats ahead never do, because a storyteller who knows the ending leaks it and models especially. Foreshadowing is authored into the earlier lines by the beats turn, which does know. Between beats the persona wonders and only beats state; a persona change mid-adventure changes the teller and not the tale.
  - [x] Fiction is a register, and the persona is what makes it one - d47's tiering exists so an invention is never spoken as fact, and an adventure names real systems and stations. **Having the ship's AI tell the story is the mitigation rather than the risk**: a companion saying *there is meant to be something out past the third moon* is audibly a character telling a story, where the danger was always the knowledge table's flat voice asserting it. The test that decides whether the seam holds: mid-adventure, asked what is actually at a system the story has been talking about, the answer comes from the tables and d47 can say which is which. Adventures are a tier the persona may speak from and the factual tools never read from, and no row is ever written into a shipped table from one. **Built 2026-08-22**: the context block's own label — a story the Commander agreed to hear; the places are real and the people may not be; asked what is actually at a place, answer from the tools and say which is which — rather than a line in `Guardrails.Text`, because position 2 is a constant whose bytes every cached prefix depends on and the label rides below the breakpoint where a story's presence costs nothing. Invented people are allowed and invented places are refused by the dry run; no `LoreTier` is ever written from an adventure and there is no code that could. The register test for a live model sits with the scenario corpus for the next pass; what is asserted today is structural — `AdventureContextTests` holds that the turn, the ending and the beats ahead are absent from the block until their time.

- [x] **Phase 48 — The mini panel without a headset**
  - [x] A third instantiation, not a second design - Asked for 2026-08-22: *a 2D overlay to mimic the VR mini-panel*. **The mechanism is already the whole answer.** `PanelView` is a `UserControl` instantiated once per surface against one `PanelViewModel`, and mini is `PanelMode.Mini` — a reduced content set rather than a smaller copy — so this is a third host calling the same constructor with the same model and the same mode. No second view definition, no screenshot of the window, nothing to keep in step: the overlay cannot show something stale because there is nothing for it to be stale about. **The Phase 9 trap does not reach here.** A `UserControl` detached from a logical tree rasterises as an empty quad with no error, which is why `VrPanelSurface` hosts its copy in a `Window` that is constructed and never shown; this one is a window that *is* shown, which is the ordinary path and the easy half. What does reach here is the rule that amendment protects — the VR path never depends on the state of the window the Commander can see — and a third surface makes that **three independent facts rather than two**. The acceptance is blunt: the transcript's tail readable over Elite with d47's own window minimised, and the headset untouched by closing the overlay. **Built 2026-08-24**: `OverlayPanel` is a `Window` whose content is `new PanelView { DataContext = model, Mode = PanelMode.Mini }` — the same constructor the window and the headset call, against `AppHost.Panel`. No second view definition and no screenshot; the only thing it adds to the headset's arrangement is a `LayoutTransformControl`, which the window's zoom already uses. The acceptance is held twice: `TheOverlayWithoutAHeadsetTests` renders the surface and asserts an appended line changes the pixels, once with no window at all and once with the window minimised, and `MinimiseSafetyTests` is **not touched by this phase** — which is how the VR path's independence stays three facts rather than becoming two and a shared helper.
  - [x] Borderless is the condition, and d47 reads it rather than guessing - A topmost window composites over a borderless or windowed game and is **simply not there** over an exclusive-fullscreen one: no error, no log line, nothing to diagnose. That is the failure shape this project has already paid for twice — the VR overlay that ran with sound and no picture, and the microphone whose silent default was indistinguishable from not hearing — so it is worth a check even though the feature is small. Elite writes the answer beside a file d47 already opens: `Options/Graphics/DisplaySettings.xml`, root element `DisplayConfig`, element `FullScreen` — **2 is borderless, read off the Commander's own machine 2026-08-22**, with 0 windowed and 1 exclusive on the community's reading, which the row must not state in Frontier's voice until somebody flips the setting and looks. `ElitePalette` reads `GraphicsConfigurationOverride.xml` out of that same folder under the rule this inherits outright: **read-only and fail-soft, because d47 is a guest in the Commander's game configuration**. Missing, hand-edited or written by a mod resolves to *cannot tell*, and cannot-tell draws the overlay rather than refusing to. When it can tell and the answer is exclusive, **the row says so by name** — that sentence is worth more than the feature it guards. **Built 2026-08-24**: `EliteDisplay` in Core, beside `ElitePalette` and on its terms — read-only, fail-soft, and the same `Options/Graphics` folder. 2 is borderless on the Commander's own file, 0 windowed and 1 exclusive on the community's reading, and **an undocumented number is reported by number** rather than folded into cannot-tell, so a 3 arriving one day says 3. Cannot-tell draws the overlay. The sentence lives on an `Info` row read fresh on every draw, because the fix for it is to go and change the setting in Elite and a Commander who has just done that should see it say something else.
  - [x] Output-only, and the pointer goes straight through it - The mini panel in VR is reachable by a ray; **this one is not touchable at all, and that is the design rather than a limitation.** A click the overlay eats is a click Elite did not get, and a focus steal mid-combat is worse than anything the overlay could have been showing. Three window styles carry it and each is its own claim: `WS_EX_TRANSPARENT` so the pointer passes through, `WS_EX_NOACTIVATE` so it never takes the foreground, `WS_EX_TOOLWINDOW` so it is not something to Alt-Tab into. Every way of changing what it shows is therefore somewhere else — the window, the spoken route, or a switch — which is **the captions' shape rather than the panel's**. Placement is the one exception and has to be explicit: the headset's answer is the reanchor gesture, and the flat one is a place mode where the overlay briefly takes clicks so it can be dragged and gives them back the moment it is done. Where it ends up is a **view preference and not a setting** — it joins the VR anchors and the window's own placement in `ViewState`, because a monitor coordinate is not something a Commander typed, and `settings.json` is append-only for anything that ever is. **Built 2026-08-24**: `WS_EX_TRANSPARENT`, `WS_EX_NOACTIVATE` and `WS_EX_TOOLWINDOW`, with `WS_EX_LAYERED` beside them — **which is the opacity row rather than a fourth claim**: Avalonia's own `Opacity` blends the content against the window's own background, which here is the colour it is already drawing, so a Commander who asked for half would have got a washed-out strip hiding exactly as much cockpit as before. Place mode takes off the pass-through and nothing else, so even a drag cannot take the foreground from Elite; it ends on release or on a second press. Placement is `ViewState.Overlay`, position only — the size falls out of the scale row, so there is nothing else to remember. **What Win32 does with those styles is the one part no test here reaches**: the headless platform has no handle, so the flags are asserted by reading the code rather than by running it.
  - [x] It shows itself when the game does - A strip pinned over a browser is a strip the Commander turns off within a day. `IEliteWindow.IsForeground` is the existing answer and is already cheap enough to be asked before every injected key, so the rule is **visible when Elite is in front and hidden otherwise**, polled on the tick like everything else that reads the world. Two calls here are decisions rather than inheritances. It hides when **d47's own window** is in front, because the panel is right there showing strictly more. And there is **no interlock with the headset**: a Commander in VR has no use for it, but wanting both is a real case — a second monitor somebody else is watching — and an overlay that silently declines to appear because SteamVR is running would be exactly the undiagnosable nothing the item above exists to prevent. Off by default, which settles it either way. **Built 2026-08-24**: `OverlayPanel.ShouldShow(enabled, eliteForeground, placing)`, polled through `IEliteWindow.IsForeground` on the tick — the same instance the injector asks before every scancode, now on `AppHost.Elite`. Hiding when d47's own window is in front needed no term of its own: Elite does not hold the foreground while d47 does, so the decision recorded is the decision **not** to carve out an exception. No interlock with the headset, and off by default. One term was added rather than inherited — **placing wins over the foreground rule**, because a Commander sets the strip up before launching the game as often as after, and a place mode reachable only with Elite already running is one they cannot reach on the day they need it.
  - [x] It reads the shared transcript and is never a second place to navigate - Phase 45's mirror is over **navigators, not surfaces**, so a third one joins through the same `AppHost.RouteNavigation(nav, post)` that already routes both — with its poster captured on the overlay's own thread, the headless-dispatcher rule, which is not optional on a surface whose whole job is drawing what another thread changed. Mini draws the transcript's tail and the provenance line, plus the Adventures reading `AdventureMini` added on 2026-08-22, and **those two roots are all the overlay furnishes**: `PanelView.Tab` already declines to select a tab nobody furnished, so being sparse costs no special case anywhere — the same *not calling `Furnish`* that withdrew Loadout from the headset. What must not follow is the overlay's tab dragging the window's, and the mirror already rules that out, since the transcript root is shared while tabs and trails stay per-surface. Phase 46's destination vocabulary is the union of what the surfaces registered, so a spare detent could name the overlay's page with nothing new built, and a key no surface answers to is already reported by name. **Built 2026-08-24**: a third `AppHost.RouteNavigation(nav, post)` with `leads` left false, its poster captured on the overlay's own thread. `Furnish` is called exactly once — for Adventures — so the transcript and `AdventureMini` are the whole of it and the other six tabs are declined by the navigator with no special case anywhere. Prompts are deliberately **not** routed: a chooser on a surface the pointer goes through is a question nobody can answer.
  - [x] Interface, not VR, and one page to write - The rows belong on `InterfaceCapability`: there is no headset in this, and a Commander goes looking where the theme, the zoom and the hotkeys already are rather than under a card about a device they are not wearing. **Scale is the lever, because there are no metres.** Mini is fixed at 512x280 in the headset for a stated reason — apparent size is the pixel count and the quad's width in metres together, so the width is the lever and the height is a floor under a reduced content set — and on a monitor half of that product is missing. So the overlay's pixel size falls out of `ZoomLadder`, which is a `LayoutTransform` that re-measures and rewraps rather than a scale that blurs. On/off, scale, opacity, and a show/hide hotkey beside the ones already on that page; every one of them a new key in an append-only file. The docs gate takes it from there — rows hang off a registered capability whose page must quote its current surface, so `docs/capabilities/interface.md` grows a section, and that section is where the exclusive-fullscreen answer gets written down in full. **Built 2026-08-24**: four rows and two gestures on `InterfaceCapability` — on/off, size off `ZoomLadder`, opacity floored at 0.2, and the display-mode `Info` row — with `Ctrl+Alt+O` to show or hide and **`Ctrl+Alt+M` to move**. That second key is the one deviation from this item's list, and it is forced by the item above: placement has to be explicit, and a settings page cannot be where it is reached from, because the overlay is hidden whenever d47's own window is the thing in front. `docs/capabilities/interface.md` grew six anchored sections, which is where the exclusive-fullscreen answer is written down in full. **And one thing that was nobody's item**: `ShutdownMode` was the default, so d47 quit on the *last* window closing and had only ever had one — a second window would have left a Commander who closed the panel with d47 still running and nothing on screen to close it with. It is `OnMainWindowClose` now, said out loud.

- [x] **Phase 49 — Where to buy it, and what it costs there**
  - [x] The question `find_nearest_station` does not answer - Asked for 2026-08-22: *closest or cheapest commodity*. The tool already means **where can I buy a thing, nearest first** — it just only knows about modules and ships, which are the two things a Commander buys least often. So this is a `commodity` parameter on the tool that already exists rather than a new one: the advertised surface was last measured inside a hundred bytes of `ToolProfiles.ComfortableBytes` at 40,000, a fresh tool description costs hundreds, and a parameter on a tool whose sentence already covers this costs tens. **Both sides of the board, in one answer.** Buying is supply and buy price; selling is demand and sell price; *where do I dump 700 t of tritium* is the same query read the other way round, and a tool that answers only the buy half will be asked the sell half anyway — by a model that will then reach for the trade planner and return a circuit nobody wanted.
  - [x] The data is already pulled and the ranking is already local - **Nothing new is fetched for this.** `SpanshRequest.Markets` asks the station search for everything in a radius and takes the whole `market` array each result carries — 342 priced commodities for one station, measured 2026-08-19 — and answers in 1.1 to 1.3 seconds *whatever* it returns, so the bill is the number of requests and hardly their size. **Spansh will not do this server-side and that is settled rather than worth retrying**: every sort shape tried against a commodity's price answered HTTP 400, and the demand bounds are accepted and ignored — 203 stations for `demand >= 1`, 203 for `demand >= 50000`, 203 for no bound at all. So d47 ranks locally, which is not a workaround here but exactly the arrangement a *cheapest* answer needs. `MarketBook` is the free half: 25 markets the Commander stood in themselves, **exact rather than reported**, with `PriceSource` already deciding which wins where both have a price and the newer one taking it.
  - [x] Cheapest and nearest are two answers, and which one is right depends on the tonnage - A station 40 ly further out that is 200 credits cheaper is the wrong answer for 8 tonnes and the right one for 780, so **the quantity is part of the question rather than a refinement of it**. The tool takes one and, given it, ranks by what the whole load actually costs to go and get; without one it ranks by price and says the distance, because a Commander who did not say how much is asking what a thing is worth. Supply is a filter and not a footnote — a station listing the cheapest steel in the bubble and holding nine tonnes of it is not an answer — and the same for demand on the sell side. `find_nearest_station`'s existing shape carries the rest: `near`, `max_distance`, `large_pad` and `limit` mean here exactly what they already mean, which is most of why this is a parameter and not a tool.
  - [x] A price is a report with a date on it, and the answer says the date - **This is the way this feature is wrong while looking right.** A quoted supply figure ages badly — a colonisation rush strips a station in hours — and an answer that sounds current is worse than one that admits it is a month old. The parts already exist: `MarketSnapshot.UpdatedAt`, `PriceSource`, and `max_price_age` on `plot_trade_route`, whose bound this inherits rather than inventing a second one. A market the Commander docked at themselves is said to be **theirs**, because that is the one figure with no caveat on it. And a **fleet carrier's market is excluded unless it is asked for**: `MarketSnapshot.IsCarrier` already tells them apart, the prices are player-set and can be a joke, and the station itself may be a hundred light years away by the time the Commander arrives.
  - [x] Asked by voice, and drawn on the Routing tab - The spoken route is most of the value — this is a question a Commander asks with their hands on the stick — but the Routing tab is where an answer with six rows and four columns per row belongs, and **a panel page costs no tool-surface bytes at all**, which is the lever Phase 47 used for adventure generation and is worth using again while the ceiling is this close. The tab already holds the plan, the course, the result and the progress pages, so this is a fifth of the same kind rather than a new surface. The docs gate takes the rest: `find_nearest_station` lives on `GalaxyCapability`, so [docs/capabilities/galaxy.md](docs/capabilities/galaxy.md) — which today says *where to buy a named module or ship, nearest first* — has to quote the widened schema, and CI fails until it does.

- [x] **Phase 50 — Colonisation trade routes**
  - [x] Everything one build still needs, and where to buy it - Asked for 2026-08-22, and **flagged by the Commander as not thought through yet**, so the items below are what is already settled by earlier phases rather than a design. Phase 17 stops at what you owe: `get_construction_needs` names every outstanding commodity, how much is left, how much is already in the hold, and how many trips the ship's capacity implies. **This phase starts there and answers the next question**, which is where a Commander goes to get it. Phase 49 answers that one commodity at a time; a build asks it about twenty at once, which is a different question and not twenty of the same one. **Built 2026-08-25**: `ColonisationSourcing` is the arithmetic, `ITradePlanService.SourceConstructionAsync` the seam, a `where_to_buy` parameter on `get_construction_needs` the spoken half, and the Checklist tab's Sourcing root the drawn one. The unit is *this station covers six of your twenty*, which is the sentence a Commander acts on.
  - [x] The shopping list is exact, and it is the one thing here that needs no source - `ColonisationConstructionDepot` is **a snapshot rather than a delta** — measured over 6,330 events and 120,208 resource rows, with `RequiredAmount` never moving mid-build and `ProvidedAmount` never decreasing across 119,887 consecutive comparisons — so `ConstructionSite.Outstanding` is a fact off the Commander's own disk rather than anything predicted. That is the trap which caught `EngineerProgressState` and `ModuleStore`, silently both times, and colonisation does not have it. **Nothing in this phase should ever recompute that list**; every part of it that is uncertain is on the market side. **Built 2026-08-25**: nothing recomputes it. `ConstructionSite.Outstanding` is handed to the sourcing search as given, and the one thing that changes what gets *bought* — the carrier figure — is applied to the shopping list and never to the site's own figures. `WithoutTheParameterNothingIsSearched` holds the old answer unchanged.
  - [x] It only works for a site that already exists, and that boundary is a licensing one - The 2026-08-16 spike settled it: every machine-readable rendering of Frontier's facility figures sits inside GPL-3.0 source, EDSC publishes nothing, EDCD has no colonisation repository, and Frontier's own guide states every mechanic and publishes not one number. So **d47 cannot cost a facility that has not been placed**, cannot say what to build first, and must not learn to here by the back door of a sourcing plan. `ColonisationPlan.WhatThisCannotBe` is already the sentence and it is the refusal this phase reuses verbatim — *asked to source a build that is still an intention, say what cannot be known and offer what can*, which is the plan and the count. **Built 2026-08-25**: by construction. The only site this can source for is one the Commander's own journal has reported a depot event from, so there is no path here that costs a plan and no figure that could come from one.
  - [x] The join is symbol to display name, and it has already bitten this capability once - The depot's rows carry a **folded internal symbol**; both market sources are keyed by the **display name** — `Market.json` by `Name_Localised`, spansh by its `commodity` field — so *Low Temperature Diamonds* has to meet `$lowtemperaturediamond_name;` and every outstanding row has to survive the trip. Phase 17 measured the same seam going wrong: `ColonisationContribution` is mixed case on 30 of 30 symbols against 0 of 31 for the depot, and a fold that strips `$` and `_name;` without lowercasing joined the depot to the hold perfectly and matched no contribution at all — right-looking and wrong. **A sourcing plan that quietly omits a commodity because its name did not join is that failure with a Commander's week on it**, so the acceptance is blunt: every outstanding row either resolves to a market row or is named in the answer as one d47 could not price, and nothing is dropped in silence. **Built 2026-08-25**, and written wrong first: the acceptance test caught it on the first run, which is the whole reason that test exists. The join is name to name — `Name_Localised` is on every row of all 120,208 measured — and `FoldingTheDisplayNameDoesNotProduceElitesSymbol` asserts the fold's real output so nobody puts it back.
  - [x] It is a covering problem and not the trade planner - `TradePlanner` maximises credits over hops and carries **credits and cargo** between them, because holding a commodity past a poor buyer is sometimes the move. None of that is this. **The cargo is decided before the Commander leaves** — it is the depot's list — the objective is trips and time rather than profit, and the binding constraint is **supply where you buy** rather than demand where you sell. Same market sweep, same `MarketSnapshot`, different arithmetic, so it belongs beside the planner in Core under the rule that made Phase 36 testable: handed a list of markets, answers with a plan, reads no clock, opens no socket, knows nothing about Spansh. **Built 2026-08-25**: greedy set cover, ties broken on cost and then on distance, `maxStops` bounding the answer to one a Commander can act on. Pure, beside the planner, reading no clock and opening no socket.
  - [x] What d47 cannot see, said out loud rather than estimated - Three limits, all already established and all load-bearing here. **What is on the carrier is not derivable**: accumulating `CargoTransfer` was reconciled against `CarrierStats` and came out wrong 679 times against right 347, driving 11 commodities negative, so a plan cannot subtract what is already stashed — and the carrier as a mobile depot is exactly what a real colonisation Commander is doing. **The site is only as fresh as the last docking**, since 6,307 of 6,330 events fired while docked at that very site. And **a network supply figure ages fastest precisely during a rush**, which is Phase 49's caveat arriving here with more tonnage behind it. A plan therefore says what it counted, what it assumed, and what it could not see. **Built 2026-08-25, and amended by the Commander the same day.** The three limits are all said — the site is as fresh as the last docking, prices carry their age and stale markets are counted rather than swallowed, and nothing is ever dropped in silence. The carrier is the amendment: it is still never *derived*, and it is now **subtracted where the Commander types it**, dated on every answer that used it. `CarrierManifest` is the store and the reasoning.
  - [x] Open, and to be settled in a plan before anything is built - Named here so they are not decided by accident. **What shape is the answer** — a shopping list per station, an ordered run, a course plotted, or a checklist project on Phase 17's substrate so it survives the session? **How many sites at once**, given three were open simultaneously in the corpus and `ConstructionSite` is deliberately a collection with a selection? **Where does the carrier fit**, when it is the obvious staging post and the one container d47 is blind inside? And **where is it asked from** — the surface ceiling makes a panel page on the Routing or Checklist tab cheap and a new tool expensive, and the same trade Phase 49 makes. This item is a placeholder for `docs/plans/`, and it should be the first thing struck out. **Struck out 2026-08-25**, which is what it existed for. All four are answered in [docs/plans/phase-50-colonisation-trade-routes.md](docs/plans/phase-50-colonisation-trade-routes.md) and three of the four shipped as recommended: a shopping list per station, one site at a time, and a panel page plus a widened tool. The fourth was overturned — the carrier is entered by the Commander and subtracted, rather than named and left out.

- [x] **Phase 51 — The window goes mini too**
  - [x] The desktop window shows what the headset already can - Asked for 2026-08-22: put the main window into mini mode and back. **The content half is nearly free and that is the point.** `PanelMode` is a property of the view rather than of a surface — `Mode` is a `StyledProperty` on `PanelView` and `ApplyChrome` computes every region from `Mode` and `Tab` together — so the desktop is simply a host that has never set it. `VrPanelSurface.ApplyMode` is the whole pattern to copy: read the setting, compare against what the view is on, assign only if it moved. What this phase actually builds is the **window** half — the shape, the way in, the way back, and the two places the existing code will get it wrong — and everything in the list below is one of those. **Built 2026-08-24**: `MainWindow.ApplyWindowMode` is `VrPanelSurface.ApplyMode` copied — read the setting, compare against what the view is on, assign only if it moved — and the content half really was nearly free. **What was not free is the order**: the rectangle moves before the content does, because changing the content raises a resize of its own and the placement memory has to have sampled the shape being left before that arrives.
  - [x] The way back must not live in the thing that disappears - Mini takes the tab strip, the mode control, the breadcrumb, the search box, the banners and the header, all of it by design. **A Commander who lands there with no way out has bricked their window**, and a first run that ends in a force-quit is the only way this feature is remembered. Three ways back, none of them drawn by the panel: the **hotkey**, which is the one that works when nothing at all is on the surface and which joins the four already on the Interface page under Phase 4's press-the-key-to-bind rule; the **spoken phrase**; and the **title bar**, because mini keeps its decorations. That last one is a ruling rather than an oversight — a chromeless click-through strip pinned over the game is Phase 48 and a different animal, and keeping the frame here means the window can still be moved, resized and closed by the means the Commander already knows, which holds this phase to *a content change plus a resize*. **Built 2026-08-24**: all three. `Ctrl+M` window-scoped rather than system-wide — `Reanchor` and `ShowOverlay` are system-wide because they are wanted when Elite fills the screen, and this one is pressed by somebody looking at the window it acts on. It writes the setting through `SettingsCaller.Hotkey` rather than going at the view, the same road `ZoomHost.Set` takes, so the row cannot show a state that is no longer true. The title bar is untouched, which cost nothing because it was never going to be taken away. **Amended 2026-08-24, the same day, on the Commander's instruction**: *“in the mini window there does need to be a physical UI control to switch it between modes.”* So there are four ways back and one of them is drawn — an **Expand** button in the panel's bottom corner, **Shrink** in the full window. The reasoning above is not withdrawn and none of the three survivors is: the argument was that a drawn control must not be the *only* way out, and it was read one step too far into *there must not be one*. A way out nobody can see is a way out somebody has to be told about. It is furnished rather than branched, so the headset's mini and the click-through overlay draw no button; it sits in its own row rather than in `StatusRow`, which is the transcript's and vanishes on the story; and it survives a chooser, because a chooser is exactly the state a Commander can feel stuck in.
  - [x] Its own row and its own words, because mode is per-surface - Phase 45 already settled the principle in one line: what you are reading is shared, **mini/full and zoom are how a surface draws it and stay where they were**. So the window's mode is a new key in `Ui` beside Theme and Zoom — machine-wide rather than per-Commander, because a window is a property of the desk and not of whoever is flying today — read out of `SettingsService.Changed` like every other row, since Phase 4 requires it to take effect with no restart. **And the spoken phrases must not collide with the headset's.** `VrCapability.ModeKey` already owns *mini panel* and *full panel*; a Commander in a headset who says those must not shrink a window they cannot see, and one at a desk must not resize a quad they are not wearing. *Mini window* and *full window*, and neither phrase reaches the other surface. **Built 2026-08-24**: `ui.mode` beside Theme and Zoom, Install-scoped, read from `SettingsService.Changed`. The collision was already guarded and nobody had noticed: `SettingsByVoiceTests.EveryDeclaredCommandPhraseActuallyRoutes` asserts every declared phrase routes to **its own** row, so *mini window* landing on `vr.mode` would have failed a test that has been green since Phase 19. `MiniWindowPhrasesTests` names the four anyway, because the claim is specific enough to be worth reading.
  - [x] The size is measured, and the placement memory has to be told about it - Mini is fixed at 512x280 in the headset for a stated reason — apparent size is the pixel count and the quad's width in metres together — and on a desk half of that product does not exist. So the window's mini size is **the mini content's own desired size at the current zoom, plus the frame**, measured rather than typed, which also means `ZoomHost` keeps meaning what it means: a `LayoutTransform` re-measures, so a mini window at 150% is a bigger mini window rather than a clipped one. **The trap is `WindowPlacementMemory`.** It samples the window on every resize and move and writes the result to `ViewState` as a size the Commander chose, so an untold mini toggle overwrites the full-window rectangle and the way back arrives 512 pixels wide — permanently, and across a restart. It is the same shape as the maximised case that record already guards, and it wants the same treatment: the full rectangle survives the toggle, and **the mini rectangle is remembered separately** so a Commander who widens their mini window keeps it. Acceptance is exact: full to mini to full lands on the pixel it started on, twice running, with a restart in the middle. **Built 2026-08-24**: `MiniSize` is `PanelResolution.Mini` plus `PanelView.AskLineHeight`, taken at 100% and then scaled — **the ask line is the whole of what is measured**, because it is the one thing the window's mini keeps that the headset's does not, and a surface that never furnished one lands back on 512x280 exactly. The frame needed no arithmetic: `Window.Width` and the placement record are already the same numbers, and the title bar sits above them. The trap was as described and is now two records — `ViewState.MainWindowMini` beside `MainWindow`, with `WindowPlacementMemory` holding which shape it is in so a sample lands in the right one. The acceptance is a test verbatim, and **reintroducing the single-record version fails it**.
  - [x] Mini on a tab that is not the transcript is a hole, and it is already open - `ApplyChrome` hides the tab strip in mini but leaves `PagePane` visible whenever the tab is not the transcript, so a surface put into mini while it is on Settings draws the settings page — whose nav collapses below `NavCollapsesBelow` of 900 and whose body wants 700 — into a 512-wide surface with **no tab strip to leave by**. The headset can be driven into this today by setting `vr.mode` to mini on the wrong tab; the desktop will find it on day one, because the desktop is where Settings lives. **The ruling: going mini selects a reading mini actually has** — the transcript, or the Adventures mini where a host furnished one — and coming back restores the tab that was showing. It belongs in `ApplyChrome` and `ApplyNavigation`, where both surfaces get it at once, rather than in the window, because fixing it only for the desktop leaves the headset bug exactly where it was. **Built 2026-08-24**: `MiniShows` and `SettleMini` in `PanelView`, so both surfaces got it at once and the headset bug closed with the desktop one. **One ruling the item did not make had to be made**: a move to a tab mini lacks *while already in mini* is declined and **not remembered**. Only what mini took away is given back — a phrase naming Settings that visibly did nothing must not queue up a jump to Settings for whenever the Commander next goes full, which would be a move they asked for minutes ago and had every reason to think had not happened. The first cut got this wrong and a test caught it. **Narrowed 2026-08-24, the same day, on the Commander's instruction**: *“it should have the same tabs as the VR mini panel (including Checklist).”* Mini now shows **everything the surface was furnished with except Settings**. That is a narrowing rather than a reversal, and it is only available because half of what this item was defending has since been built: the two grounds were that the settings page does not fit *and* that there was no tab strip to leave by, and the second is gone — the drawn way out added the same evening is on every page mini has. The first was never general. It is one page's measured minimum width, and the checklist, the engineer pages and the clocks have no such number; a capture at 512x280 is how that was checked rather than assumed. **The exclusion is named rather than the inclusions listed**, because a list of what mini may show is a list somebody has to remember to add to. The capture also found the checklist's own toolbar overlapping itself — a `DockPanel` whose filling child cannot shrink, wrong below about 700 pixels and so wrong in a narrow desktop window all along, now a `WrapPanel`.
  - [x] The ask line stays, and it is the one line of shared code this phase changes - `AskRow.IsVisible = full && transcript` is right for a headset with no keyboard and wrong for the one surface whose entire point is a keyboard. **A mini window you cannot type into is worse than the full window in every respect** and would be switched off inside a day, which makes this the difference between the feature landing and the feature being built. It is **furnished rather than branched**: the host that wants it says so, the way `EnableSettings` and `EnableChecklist` already work, so no code anywhere tests which surface it is on and the headset's mini is untouched. Everything else mini shows is unchanged — the transcript's tail, the provenance line and the microphone indicator, which is the whole content set and is already what it draws. **Built 2026-08-24**: `EnableAskInMini`, called from `MainWindow` and nowhere else — so the headset's mini is untouched and the flat overlay of Phase 48 stays output-only by simply not making the call, which is the same *not calling `Furnish`* that withdrew Loadout from the headset. One line of `ApplyChrome` changed, exactly as forecast.
  - [x] What it is not, and what it is worth - **This is not Phase 48** and neither blocks the other. That one is a second window, chromeless and click-through and pinned over a game running borderless; this one is the window the Commander already has, still interactive, for the very ordinary case of one monitor and wanting d47 out of the way without losing it. This is also **much the cheaper of the two** — a setting, a hotkey, a resize and two fixes to code that already exists — so if only one gets built it should be this one. The rows hang off `InterfaceCapability`, which means [docs/capabilities/interface.md](docs/capabilities/interface.md) grows a section or CI fails. Three tests carry it: a headless capture of the window in mini showing the tail, the round-trip rectangle above, and the existing minimise-safety test still passing untouched — because nothing in this phase is allowed to change what the headset draws. **Built 2026-08-24**, and it was the cheaper of the two as predicted. Two rows and a gesture on `InterfaceCapability`, two sections in [docs/capabilities/interface.md](docs/capabilities/interface.md). All three named tests exist: a headless capture of the window in mini showing the tail *and* an assertion that an appended line changes the pixels, the round-trip rectangle twice running with a restart in the middle, and `MinimiseSafetyTests` **not edited by this phase** — which is the only way that claim stays worth anything.

- [x] **Phase 52 — Say it and the ship does it**
  - [x] Five phrases, and four of them are words rather than work - Asked for 2026-08-23: *Engage*, *Supercruise*, *Take us out*, *Separate and Engage*, *Separate and Supercruise*. **Two of the five are already built and are one line each.** `GameActions` has `hyperspace` (bind `Hyperspace`) and `supercruise` (bind `Supercruise`) with phrases on both, so *Engage* and *Supercruise* are entries on actions that exist rather than anything new — which is the finding that should shape this phase, because it means the effort is all in the other three. **The bare word is the risk and it has bitten this router before.** `engage` already appears inside *engage supercruise*, *engage boost* and *engage the frame shift drive*; a keyword that short hijacks every sentence containing it, which is remediation.md 16 exactly — *"my fleet carrier"* answered by the position tool. So *Engage* is a **whole phrase and never a keyword**, matched against the whole utterance the way that fix established, and the same for *Supercruise* against the three phrases that already contain it. Accepted when saying *engage* jumps, saying *engage supercruise* still supercruises, and a test asserts both against the router rather than against a running game.
  - [x] "Take us out" cannot be a key, and the phase has to say so rather than fake it - **Elite has no autolaunch binding.** Verified against Frontier's shipped presets rather than guessed — the rule Phase 10 set after finding three other things with no binding — and `KeyboardMouseOnly.binds` carries no `Autolaunch`, no `Undock` and nothing launch-shaped in either the Horizons or the Odyssey product folder. Launching is a panel button, not a control. So this item is a different animal from the four around it and has two honest endings: a **UI macro** of the galaxy-map kind — a fixed key sequence through the left panel, which is the Commander's own sequence, gated on `GuiFocus`, and as fragile as that one is — or **a refusal that names the reason**, which is what the fuel scoop and the discovery scanner already get. Accepted either way, but **not accepted as a `GameAction` with an invented bind name**: a variant naming a control Elite does not have would resolve to nothing and fail silently, which is the one outcome worse than saying no. **Shipped as the macro** (the Commander's call, 2026-08-25), walking left panel → leftmost tab → top item → select, with the press counts as one named constant because they are the only part that is a guess about a menu rather than a fact about the game. The absence was re-verified rather than inherited: every `.binds` under both product folders' `ControlSchemes` carries nothing launch-shaped, the only match for "launch" being `FireChaffLauncher`, and a test asserts no catalogue entry ever grows such a variant. Everything around the walk is checked against the game — docked before starting, `GuiFocus` confirmed on the panel before a direction key is sent, and the `Docked` flag clearing is what decides whether it worked, so "I walked the panel and we are still docked" is a distinct answer from "it launched".
  - [x] Separate: full throttle, boost until the mass lock breaks, then engage - The two compound commands, and the only genuinely new mechanism here. **The pieces exist and the wait does not.** `UseBoostJuice` is already the `boost` action, `SetSpeed100` is a real bind in Frontier's presets but not yet a `GameAction`, and the engage at the end is item 1's. What is new is that this **waits on the game's own state rather than on a clock**: `GameStatus.FsdMassLocked` is already parsed (`1u << 16`), so the loop boosts, watches the flag, and engages the moment it clears. That is strictly better than the galaxy-map macro's fixed waits and is the shape every later macro should copy. **Bounded, because an unbounded boost loop is a fuel leak with a heat signature.** A ceiling on attempts and a wall-clock ceiling, both saying what happened when they are reached — *"still mass locked after four boosts; you may be too close to the station"* — rather than stopping quietly. Accepted when the sequence is asserted against a replayed status stream that clears the flag on the third sample, and against one that never clears it.
  - [x] The second one ends in supercruise, whatever the ask said - **Recorded because the ask says otherwise.** *Separate and Supercruise* was written as *"boost until we can break mass lock and then engage hyperspace"*, identical to the line above it; the name says supercruise and two commands with different names and identical behaviour is not what was wanted. Built as supercruise, and noted here rather than silently corrected so that the Commander can overrule it in one word. **The difference matters in the game as well as on paper:** hyperspace needs a destination locked in the nav panel and refuses without one, where supercruise needs nothing, so the two fail in different ways and the failure is what a Commander would notice first.
  - [x] A switch per command, and Elite in front for every one of them - Nothing here is exempt from what Phase 42 settled. Each is its own row rather than one row for the group, because *"acting on its own"* draws why there is a switch per action and not one for the category — and a Commander who trusts d47 to supercruise may well not trust it to boost. The foreground check is not optional and is already cheap enough to be asked before every injected key; `release_all()` stays unconditional, and a compound command that is interrupted half way through releases everything it was holding rather than leaving a throttle up. **These reach the ship, so they are reachable from the keyword router and the panel and are not tool surface**: a spoken command that has to wait for a model round trip is a command given at the wrong moment, which is the whole reason the model-free router exists.

- [x] **Phase 53 — Bind it with the stick, not the keyboard**
  - [x] Most of this is already built, and the phase is mostly the join - Asked for 2026-08-23 as a sizing question: extend *press the thing you want and d47 binds it* — Phase 4's rule, and what push-to-talk uses — to accept a **joystick button**. **The expensive parts are paid for.** Phase 21 already reads controllers through `Windows.Gaming.Input` (`HotasControllers : IHotasReader`), already carries the **+6.4 MB TFM cost** that projection needs, already has a `--selftest` check that the projection activates in a published build, and already runs a **capture walk** — the Commander operates a control and d47 works out which one it was, which is precisely the gesture this phase wants a second caller for. `PushToTalkKey` is the template for the other half: a polled edge detector on the tick raising `Pressed` and `Released`, which is exactly the shape a held button needs. So this is a join between two things that exist rather than new hardware work, and it should be scoped as such rather than as "add joystick support".
  - [x] It gives the spring-return buttons a job - **Phase 21 declined momentary buttons on purpose**, and that decline is the reason this phase is worth doing rather than a problem for it. A switch needs a *position* to mean something, so a button that springs home has nothing for the reconciler to reconcile — the spike could not even tell "the control went home by itself" from "the Commander walked past without pausing", since the durations overlap (407-1611 ms against 206-1751 ms). **Push-to-talk is the opposite case**: momentary is not a limitation there, it is the whole mechanism. So every button Phase 21 had to turn away is exactly what this phase accepts, and the two features partition the hardware between them rather than competing for it.
  - [x] A button is not a key, and the store has to say so in a new word - `settings.json` is **append-only**: never rename or remove a property, and a repair needs a revision and a replacement. So a joystick binding is a **new key beside** the existing push-to-talk key rather than that key learning a second meaning — which also settles what happens when both are set, because a Commander who bound a key and later bound a button has said something and neither answer should be inferred. **The identity is device plus button, and the device is the hard half**: several HOTAS controllers is the ordinary case, so *button 7* alone is ambiguous, and whatever names the device has to survive a replug and a reboot. Phase 21 already had to reason about arrival and removal — the settle window is 1500 ms and the give-up 6 seconds — so the question has been faced once and its answer should be reused rather than invented again.
  - [x] The clash check must survive the move - Keyboard push-to-talk warns when Elite is bound to the same key, and it is the single most useful thing that path does: a key bound in both places has **no symptom other than not working**, in one direction or the other, and nothing tells you. **Elite's `.binds` file carries joystick bindings too**, so the same warning is buildable here — and losing it silently would be a regression dressed as a feature, since a HOTAS is far more likely to have every button already spoken for than a keyboard is. Accepted when binding a button Elite already uses says so by name, the way the keyboard path does. **Read-only, always** — d47 never writes the Commander's bindings. **Built, and it is weaker than the keyboard check by one fact that was not known when this was written**: Elite records a joystick binding against its own device hash — `Device="4098BD65" Key="Joy_24"` in the Commander's own file — and that hash is *not* the `NonRoamableId` `Windows.Gaming.Input` reports, with nothing published joining the two. So d47 can say a button of that number is spoken for somewhere and cannot say it is the same stick, and the warning is hedged in exactly those words rather than being dropped: a false warning costs a sentence, a missed one costs an evening of a microphone that will not open. The off-by-one is the real hazard here — Elite counts buttons from one, `HotasReading` from zero — and it is a test rather than a comment.
  - [x] What it must not become - **Not a remapper**, which is the line Phase 21 already drew and the reason its switch actions are limited to the ten Elite reports the state of. This phase binds **d47's own** commands to a button — push-to-talk first, and the hotkeys on the Interface page after it. It does not sit between the stick and the game, it does not rebind anything in Elite, and a button it is listening to is one Elite is free to be listening to as well. That is also why the clash warning above is advice rather than a refusal.
  - [x] Scope, honestly - Push-to-talk is the one that matters and should be the whole of the first cut: it is the control a Commander reaches for with their hands already on the stick, which is the entire argument for the feature. The other bindable gestures — the hotkeys on the Interface page — are the same mechanism pointed at a different list and should follow only once the capture, the storage and the clash check have been through a headset session. **The polling rate is not a risk**: the keyboard path already detects push-to-talk on the same tick, so a button read there is no less responsive than the key it replaces. **Shipped as exactly that first cut**: push-to-talk and nothing else, with the Interface page's hotkeys left for a later pass as this item asks. Two rulings from the Commander, 2026-08-25 — a bound controller that is not present **warns and falls back to the key**, since a silent stick otherwise means no voice at all until somebody notices; and with a key *and* a button bound **either opens the microphone**, the gate closing on the last release rather than the first, so letting go of one while the other is held does not cut a Commander off mid-sentence. The walk captures on **release** rather than on press, which is what tells a button from a maintained switch without guessing at durations — Phase 21 measured those overlapping at 407-1611 ms against 206-1751 ms, and *did it come back at all* is a different question that is not close. Buttons already held when the walk opens are ignored, because sixteen were held at rest on the bench and that is what a switch looks like from here.

- [x] **Phase 54 — A floor and a ceiling**
  - [x] The cheap model cannot answer a turn, and that is item one - Asked for 2026-08-23 as **Min and Max *Model* and *Thinking Level***, and exploration turned up a live defect that has to be fixed before any of it is reachable: **`claude-haiku-4-5` is in the picker and cannot answer a turn.** `AnthropicLlmProvider` sets `Thinking = ThinkingConfigAdaptive` and `OutputConfig.Effort` unconditionally; Haiku 4.5 is pre-4.6 and rejects both. `FlavourTurn` swallows the failure at `LogDebug`, so the first thing anybody does with a cheap-model setting would **silently kill every ambient line with nothing on screen**. **Amended 2026-08-25 on the Commander's instruction: the bar is Haiku as a *viable option*, not merely a safe one to point a floor at** — and that wants two mechanisms rather than one. **A deny-list in front**, mirroring `BasicWebSearchOnly`, whose doc comment already argues this exact case for the same 4.6-or-later family rule, so the known case never costs even one failed turn; kept as a **separate** field even though membership is identical today, because two fields are two failure modes. **And a model-keyed demotion behind it**, which is the half that makes the allow-list-versus-deny-list argument stop mattering: the list handles what is known and the demotion handles what is not, so neither has to be right about the future. **The real gap is not that Haiku is unusual — it is that one provider learns from refusals and the other cannot.** Both OpenAI providers already wrap every optional field in `EndpointDemotions`, Phase 29's *advertise, then demote*: send it, and on a refusal naming the field, record it and retry the turn once without it. The Anthropic provider has no demotion at all. Ported, it must key on **endpoint and model** rather than endpoint alone — Anthropic serves five models from one address, and a Haiku refusal must never switch effort off for Opus 5. **Two capability differences are named rather than discovered**, and neither blocks it: Haiku wants a **4096**-token cacheable prefix against Opus 5's 512, which d47's prompt clears but which fails *silently* when it does not; and Haiku is absent from `OperatorSystemMessageModels`, so live game state arrives as a `<system-reminder>` convention rather than a boundary journal content cannot spoof — already every ChatCompletions endpoint's normal path, and a reason to say so where the model is chosen rather than a reason to refuse the model. Both fields are omitted rather than substituted: inventing a `budget_tokens` per rung on the model that exists to be cheap is the wrong trade, and the comment should say so. **Reported rather than dropped in silence**: `TurnEvent.Routed.Effort` and `TurnResult.Effort` are already nullable and already render with no effort clause, so a model that took no effort says so — which finally gives `SupportsThinkingEffort` its first reader in `src/`, having been assigned in three providers and read nowhere. **Built 2026-08-25, and both mechanisms are in.** Two things the plan left open were settled by measurement rather than by argument. The demotion type was **lifted** rather than twinned, and the refactor reached nothing at all: `EndpointDemotions.cs` moved up one namespace and every one of the fifteen OpenAI call sites resolved unchanged, because `D47.Llm.OpenAi` is a child namespace of `D47.Llm` — the model is a trailing defaulted parameter, which is what keeps them byte-identical. And both fields turned out to be nullable and **omitted** from the JSON on null, so no two-branch construction was needed; the one trap is that the `Thinking` union converts implicitly from its config but not from null, so the null branch wants a cast or the build fails. Proven in both directions against the unmodified tree, which is the only step that matters: with the capability forced back to `true` six tests fail, and with the obvious wrong fix — omit the two fields for everyone — nine fail instead, of which four are the partner theory that exists for exactly that. **The manual half is still owed**: no test can prove Haiku's real endpoint accepts the result, and the 4096-token cache floor fails silently, so it wants a real conversation with the spend ledger's warmth column in view.
  - [x] Five rungs, because the fourth was never the top - `Xhigh` joins the ladder between `High` and `Max`, and **declaration order is the ladder** — the clamp depends on it. Ordinal churn is safe: settings serialise enums as camelCase strings, the spend ledger records no effort, and there is no `(int)` cast on it anywhere in `src/`. **The doc comment on `ThinkingEffort` is wrong and is corrected with it**: its claim that the C# SDK lacks `xhigh` is false for the pinned `Anthropic 12.40.0`, where `Effort` is `{ Low, Medium, High, Xhigh, Max }`. Anthropic translates it straight; both OpenAI providers map it down to `"high"` beside `Max`, which is the safe default and can be raised against a real 200. **No `EffortRouter` case moves onto it** — the router keeps its four outputs and `Xhigh` is reachable only by setting a bound, which is what makes `EffortRouterTests` untouched and the change provably additive. **Built 2026-08-25 and carried quietly by v0.71.2**, ahead of everything else in the phase; the line here simply went unticked until the phase closed, which is the one thing to notice about it. Nothing about the item changed on the way: no `EffortRouter` case moved onto the new rung, `EffortRouterTests` is untouched, and `TheEffortLadderTests` holds the order the clamp depends on.
  - [x] A floor is a call class, not a rung the router picks - **The two dials do not behave alike, and that is what reshaped the ask.** `output_config.effort` is a top-level request field sitting outside the cached prefix, so routing effort per turn is free — which is why `EffortRouter` already gets away with it. Model is the opposite: caches are model-scoped and `TurnLoop` already treats a switch as a sanctioned cold prefix. Measured against a ~6k-token prefix: a warm Opus 5 turn is ~1.05¢, a warm Haiku turn ~0.21¢, and **returning to Opus after a detour costs ~3.5¢ in cache writes — roughly 23× what the cheap turn saved.** Alternation is strictly worse than never routing and only pays across runs of five-plus cheap turns, so **per-turn model routing is refused**: the model dial is a call-class split instead. **The free money is in the background calls.** The seven `FlavourTurn` sites — ambient remarks, opening brief, gap reaction, two lore lookups, voice casting, adventure generation — carry no conversation history and already declare `coldPrefixExpected: true`, so pointing them at a cheap model costs **zero cache** and is a straight ~5× saving on that traffic. It needs a floor setting, not a router. **Built 2026-08-26, and the counts in the plan were right: eight callers take the floor and two keep the ceiling.** `Turns.BackgroundModel` is resolved once in `ApplyLlmSettings` and falls back to `Model`, so each of the eight is a one-token edit and a fresh install is unchanged — asserted rather than assumed, at the loop and at the store. **The negative half is a gate over `AppHost.cs` rather than a behavioural test**, and the reason is what the property is: both models are ids on one loop, so a call site reading the wrong one returns a perfectly good answer from the wrong model and no observation catches it. What can go wrong is a ninth caller reaching for `Turns.Model` because that is what its neighbours used to say, and that is a fact about the text. The plan asked for a grep; a grep nobody runs again is a grep that was true once.
  - [x] The ceiling is a cost dial and a guard against the router's own false positives - The clamp goes **at the `TurnLoop` call site, from properties on `TurnLoop`** rather than inside `EffortRouter.ChooseFor`, following how `Model`, `Persona` and `WebSearchEnabled` already arrive from `AppHost.ApplyLlmSettings`. That keeps two decisions with two owners apart — *what did the Commander ask for*, a pure heuristic, against *what will they pay for* — and it is why the router's own tests stay untouched. `ThinkingEffortRange` is a small static beside the enum holding the ladder, `Clamp`, and the parsing the rows bind through, so the clamp and the rows cannot disagree about spelling or order. **`Clamp` orders the bounds before applying them**: `Math.Clamp` throws when min exceeds max, a hand-edited settings file can produce exactly that, and Core must never throw on a settings file. The ceiling earns its keep twice over, because `EffortRouter` matches substrings with no word boundaries — *"what do you think about"* hits `"think about"` and routes to Max. **Background efforts are deliberately not clamped**: they are call-site decisions with stated reasons, and a floor of High would turn every ambient remark into a reasoning call, which is the exact blow-up the floor *model* exists to prevent. **Built 2026-08-26, and `Math.Clamp` turned out to be unusable twice over.** The plan already knew it throws when the minimum exceeds the maximum, which a hand-edited settings file can say and Core must never throw on; what it did not know is that the generic overload constrains to `IComparable<T>`, which an enum does not implement — so it binds to the numeric overloads and will not take a rung at all. The comparison is written out, and the comment gives both reasons so nobody delegates it back.
  - [x] Three rows, and the one that fails in silence - `BackgroundModel`, `EffortFloor` and `EffortCeiling` on `LlmSettings`, all nullable so that **null means behaviour identical to today** — asserted rather than assumed. **`BackgroundModel`, not `MinModel`**: `Model` can never be renamed to `MaxModel` because the settings file is append-only, and *min* implies a range something picks within, which is the reading this phase rejects. The name should say the call class, because that is what the value selects. Three rows go in after `llm.model` with **no `Group`**, because the renderer resets on exit with no divider and a group of three would leave the ungrouped rows beneath it sitting under a heading that does not cover them. The effort rows set **both `Choices` and `ChoiceSource`** — open-vocabulary is `ChoiceSource != null && Choices.Count == 0`, so a source alone would turn a five-rung ladder into a search window — and each truncates against the other bound. **The highest-risk detail in the phase**: `BackgroundModel` is a model id and belongs to the endpoint's namespace, so it must be cleared by **both** the provider row's `Write` and the endpoint row's. A stale background model after a provider switch is a 400 that `FlavourTurn` logs at Debug and returns null from, and every ambient line then falls back to its authored text with nothing on screen. **Built 2026-08-26, with two corrections to the plan.** The anchor gate does **not** fail before the docs are written: `SettingsSurfaceShapeTests` asserts that every row *declares* a `DocsAnchor` and nothing anywhere asserts that one resolves to a heading, so the docs step is owed by the rule rather than caught by a test — worth knowing, because the plan predicted a red suite that never came. And the endpoint row **does not apply on Anthropic at all**: `AcceptsCustomEndpoint` is false there because Anthropic has one address, so the test proving an endpoint change clears the background model has to run on OpenAI. The amendment itself is in both writes and was proven by reverting each and watching the matching test fail.
  - [x] What keeps the ceiling, and why - The floor reaches the background calls **and only them**, and the negative half is the half worth testing. Three sites keep the conversation model with a comment saying why, because *"it uses `FlavourTurn`"* is exactly the reasoning that would later move them by accident. **Adventure generation** already asks Medium against token budgets an order of magnitude above every other caller: the Commander pressed a button and is waiting, the output must name real systems exactly, and it is validated and re-asked on refusal. **The Commander's log** is shown as a price quote the Commander agrees to, and the book refuses to write if the model changed between quote and write. **Key verification** is proving the key for the conversation model, so it must use it. One thing is **flagged rather than fixed**: the web-search capability check asks about `Turns.Model` while now gating a lookup that runs on the background one — correct today by accident, since web search is endpoint-gated in all three providers, and the contract says it is model-gated in principle. Note it; leave the line alone. **No tool schema changes and no tool-surface bytes are spent** — worth stating, because "add a settings row" and "grow the tool surface" are usually the same act and here they are not. **Built 2026-08-26.** Both keepers carry the comment, and the adventure one says out loud that it is reached through a lowercase `turns.Model` lambda, which is the sentence that stops a future grep from missing it. The web-search capability check is flagged in place and left alone, exactly as the plan asked: it asks about the conversation model while now gating a lookup that runs on the background one, and it is correct today only because web search is endpoint-gated in all three providers.

- [ ] **Phase 55 — The panes move where you want them**
  - [ ] The rule between two panes becomes something you can take hold of - Asked for 2026-08-25, against the Loadout tab and then widened the same minute: *"I would like that to be available anywhere there is a separator like that — not just the Loadout tab."* **One mechanism covers all of it.** Every vertical bar the Commander can see between two panes is drawn by `DrillView`, which `PanelView.BuildPageOnce` constructs once per furnished tab — so Loadout, Engineers, Checklist, Routing and Adventures all get it from one change, and there is no second place that draws a separator. Today it is not a control at all: it is `BorderThickness(1, 0, 0, 0)` on the host `Border` of every pane but the first, deliberately *"a rule between panes rather than a gap, because a gap at a metre reads as two surfaces and a rule reads as two columns of one."* **That sentence is about the headset and survives untouched** — what this phase adds is a handle on the window's copy, which is a different surface with a different pointing device.
  - [ ] The mouse and nothing else, which is why this is the window's alone - The Commander's words: *"handles that the mouse (and only the mouse) can drag"*. **So it is enabled by not enabling it anywhere else**, which is the parity rule working as written rather than an exception to it: `PanelView.EnableSearch` and `EnableTurnDetails` are already desktop-only by being called from `MainWindow` and nowhere else, and this follows them. That matters more here than for a search box, because the same `PanelView` is instantiated on three surfaces and the headset drives it through a geometric hit test — a splitter that existed there would be draggable by the ray, which is the one outcome the ask rules out. The flat overlay is output-only and the pointer goes straight through it, so it is covered by the same silence. **A handle is not a hover target either**: the headset and the overlay must not grow a cursor change, a hit region or a widened rule, because none of them can be acted on there.
  - [ ] A drag that the next click undoes is not a feature - **The trap, and the reason this is not a two-line change.** `Draw` clears the column definitions on every navigation and re-adds `1*` per pane, so a width the Commander dragged is discarded the moment they open a ship. The panes themselves are already kept across redraws for exactly this class of reason — *"so a level does not lose its scroll position to the strip getting one column wider"* — and the widths now need the same care. What is remembered is a **proportion rather than a pixel count**, because the window is resizable and a remembered 640 px means something different at 1024 and at 2048; and it is remembered **per pane count**, because a two-pane split and a three-pane split are different arrangements the Commander will want set differently. `WindowPlacementMemory` is the neighbour to follow — it already keeps this window's geometry, on this surface, out of the append-only settings file.
  - [ ] The reflow still decides how many panes there are - A drag changes the proportions **within** a pane count and never the count itself. `ArrangeOverride` computes `clamp(floor(width / MinimumPaneWidth), 1, MostPanes)` and that stays the authority, so widening the window still opens a third pane and narrowing it still closes one. **`MinimumPaneWidth` is the clamp on the drag as well as on the reflow**, and it has to be, or a Commander can drag a pane down to a sliver the reflow believes is 380 wide — two mechanisms disagreeing about how much room a pane has, which is the shape of defect this repository keeps finding. A drag that would take either side below the minimum stops at the minimum rather than refusing, because a handle that stops moving says what the limit is and a handle that snaps back says nothing.
  - [ ] Where it must not creep - **Not a resizable headset panel.** The VR panel's size is settled by apparent size in arcminutes and is a different question with a different answer, and nothing here reopens it. **Not keyboard-resizable and not voice-resizable**, because the ask names the mouse and only the mouse — and a spoken *"make that pane wider"* would need a unit nobody has agreed. **Not a saved layout per ship or per crumb**: the unit is the surface's split at a given pane count, not a remembered arrangement per subject, which is a much larger promise and one nobody has asked for. **And the rule stays a rule when nothing is being dragged** — the handle is the same hairline it is today until the pointer is on it, so a page at rest looks exactly as it does now.

- [ ] **Phase 56 — Making an adventure together, out loud**
  - [ ] Research first, and the first finding is that half of it is already built - Asked for 2026-08-25 as **collaboration with the ship's AI to create an Adventure**, and filed as a phase to be researched rather than designed, because the shape of the work depends on facts nobody has checked yet. **The one fact already checked changes the size of it.** `AdventureGenerator.ReviseAsync` exists and does exactly this reasoning: its own summary is *"reasoning with the AI about a draft before it is accepted"*, it takes an `AdventureRemark` and the whole `exchange` so far, and it re-runs the turn with the brief, the current draft, its spine and every previous remark in context. `AdventureRemark(Remark, Reply)` is already the record of a back-and-forth. **So this phase is not "build a conversation about a story" — it is "let the Commander have the one that exists, where they actually are."**
  - [ ] The gap is the surface, and it is one caller wide - `ReviseAsync` has **exactly one caller in the repository**: `AdventuresPage.cs`, on the desktop panel. `AdventureCapability` has no remark, revise or exchange surface of any kind, so **there is no spoken route to it at all.** A Commander flying with their hands on the stick — the premise of the whole product — can have d47 generate a story and can accept or reject it, and cannot say a word about it. That asymmetry is the phase. Whether the answer is a spoken route, a headset surface, or both, is the first thing the research has to settle, and the parity rule says it need not be both: a tab may live on one surface, and VR reaching parity is a someday-maybe rather than a constraint to bend a design around.
  - [ ] What it must cost, and the ceiling that decides the mechanism - **The tool surface is the binding constraint and it must be measured rather than assumed.** It has been at the ceiling for several phases, and Phase 50's widening spent 103 bytes of what was left, so *"add a `revise_adventure` tool"* may simply not be affordable — and the answer is not to find out by breaking prompt caching. Two mechanisms cost nothing and both already have precedent here: **a panel page costs no tool-surface bytes at all**, which is the lever Phase 47 used for generation itself and Phase 50 used again; and **a dynamic command is matched first, whole-utterance, carries its arguments and costs no surface either**, which is what a free-text remark actually is. A remark is prose — *"make the second beat further out"* — so it is the dynamic-command shape rather than a schema with fields. Measure the surface with the probe before designing anything that assumes a tool.
  - [ ] The turn is not free and the Commander is waiting - Generation already asks Medium effort against token budgets an order of magnitude above every other caller, and Phase 54 records why it must keep the **conversation** model rather than a cheap floor: the Commander pressed a button and is waiting, the output must name real systems exactly, and it is validated and re-asked on refusal. **Every one of those reasons applies to a remark, and one applies harder** — a revision runs the whole turn again with the draft, the spine and the entire exchange in context, so an exchange that runs long is a turn that costs more each time it goes round. What the research has to establish is what a Commander will actually pay for a conversation about a story they have not accepted yet, and whether the exchange needs a ceiling, a summary, or nothing at all. **Say what it cost**, the way the rest of the product does, rather than discovering it in the ledger.
  - [ ] Speaking a draft is not the same as drawing one - The Adventures tab shows a draft as a page: a name, a spine, and a beat per row with its trigger. **Spoken, none of that structure exists**, and reading six beats aloud is not a conversation, it is a recital the Commander cannot hold in their head. Change request 25 already settled the neighbouring question when it put *Step X of Y* on the feed and overturned a Phase 47 rule to do it, so the precedent is that the spoken form is allowed its own shape rather than being the drawn one read out. What that shape is — a summary and an offer to hear more, one beat at a time, or something else — is a research question and probably the hardest one here.
  - [ ] The trigger vocabulary is still closed, and collaboration must not open it - Phase 47's safety property, unchanged and load-bearing: **a step naming an event that does not exist is refused by name with the reason**, triggers match structured journal fields, and a generated adventure is only ever *proposed* — propose, accept, save, execute — which is `ChecklistProposals`' shape. A conversation that talks the model into a trigger nobody validated would take that apart quietly, and the refusal-and-re-ask loop `GenerateAsync` already runs is where the answer should live rather than in a new check. **A remark changes the draft, never the rules the draft is checked against.**
  - [ ] Where it must not creep - **Not a chat about anything else.** This is a conversation about a draft that exists, with the draft in context; a general story-writing mode is a different product. **Not editing an accepted adventure** — progress counts forward from acceptance and mines no history, and a story that can be rewritten under a Commander who is three beats into it is a promise Phase 47 deliberately did not make. **And not a second generator**: whatever this becomes, it calls `ReviseAsync`, because two paths that both build adventures are two paths that will disagree about what an adventure may contain.

- [ ] **Phase 57 — Every voice can come from somewhere different**
  - [x] Six slots, and they come from the roles that already exist - Asked for 2026-08-25. Today one provider speaks for everybody. It becomes a choice per group: **Aboard** (the ship's AI and the crew), **Carrier** (the captain and the tower), **NPCs**, **people you know**, **direct messages**, and **anyone in range**. **The groups are derived rather than invented**: `RadioVoice.IsOverTheAir` already separates aboard from over-the-air, `VoiceCast` already holds five roles, and `ForSender`'s `isPlayer` flag already separates a Commander from an NPC — and `IncomingMessages` already carries the channel in the announcement key, which is what separates a squadron mate from a stranger in local. A `VoiceGroup` sits above `VoiceRole` and no existing role changes meaning. **The human channels sort by consent rather than by humanity**, settled with the Commander on 2026-08-25: `friend`, `wing` and `squadron` are people they chose to be in contact with, `local` and `starsystem` are anyone in range with no consent anywhere in it, and `player` — a direct message — gets its own slot, because whether that implies contact is neither obvious nor the code's to decide. Accepted when each slot can name a different provider and every voice still comes out of the arbiter in the order it was written. **Built 2026-08-25.** Two things the plan left implicit turned out to matter. The channel is **carried on the announcement** rather than parsed back out of its key: the key is an identity for cooldown purposes and nothing promises to keep its shape, and routing a bill off it is a coupling that would break quietly. And `IncomingMessages` now reads its player-channel list *from* `VoiceGroups` rather than holding a second copy — two lists that have to agree about `starsystem` are two lists that will not. **The one consequence nobody had written down is the speaking rate**: it is stored per provider, `ElevenLabs` *rejects* a rate outside its range rather than clamping it, and a figure chosen for Edge applied to a carrier that has moved is not a fast carrier but a silent one — so `SpeechCapability.RateFor` gained a per-provider overload.
