That is not change-requests.md 43, which is still out of scope; it is the existing per-provider row read for the provider actually speaking.
  - [x] In the room keeps the provider; over the air goes to Edge - Everything that reaches the Commander through a radio — the carrier, NPCs, people you know, direct messages, anyone in range — defaults to **Edge**, which costs nothing, so nothing another Commander types can spend the Commander's money. Only the two voices actually in the cockpit, the ship's AI and the crew, keep whatever provider is selected. **The line is `RadioVoice.IsOverTheAir` and it already exists**, so this is an existing division being given a second job rather than a new one being invented. **One consequence is named rather than left to be discovered: this moves the Carrier to Edge.** The captain and the tower are d47's own fictions rather than other people's text and carry no cost risk; they go to Edge because they come over a radio, and if they should follow the cockpit instead that is a one-word change. The warning goes on the change rather than on the state: choosing a paid provider for one of those is choosing to be billed per character for text somebody else writes and can write as much of as they like. **Edge is free and is not local** — it sends the line to `speech.platform.bing.com` — so the row must not let "free" read as "private", which is the mistake a Commander would make unaided and the reason the item below is a separate item. Accepted when a fresh install speaks every stranger through Edge without being asked, when moving one of those slots to a paid provider says plainly what that means, and when an existing file is **migrated** rather than left paying quietly — which is safe here because the install base is two, and is recorded as being true on 2026-08-25 rather than for ever. **Built 2026-08-25**, with one addition the ruling implies and does not say: **nothing is migrated while nothing is speaking.** With `none` selected the Commander has asked for silence, and writing Edge into five slots would answer that by starting to talk — so the map stays unwritten until a provider that speaks is chosen, and the ruling applies at the moment it means something.
  - [ ] No other player's text has to leave the machine - The reason this phase exists rather than a nicety it enables. `speech.md {#egress}` already says re-voiced messages are *"written by other players"*, which makes local and system chat an **untrusted, attacker-influenced, unbounded-volume** path pointed at a per-character paid API — someone spamming local chat spends the Commander's money. **The item above settles the cost half and not this one**: Edge is free and still sends every line to Microsoft, so this half waits on a provider that runs on the machine — which is Phase 59, and is why Phase 59 exists. Accepted when the ship's core can be on a paid provider **while no other player's words leave the machine at all**, and when the settings surface states which of those two things is currently true rather than leaving it to be inferred. **Half built 2026-08-25, and the item says which half.** The cost half is settled outright: every slot carrying another player's words defaults to Edge, which is free, so nobody can spend the Commander's money by typing. The egress half is not, and cannot be here — Edge is free and *not* local, so those words still reach `speech.platform.bing.com`. What shipped is the second clause: the disclosure states which of the two is true rather than leaving it to be inferred, and says plainly that free is not private. **The first clause waits on Phase 59**, which is why Phase 59 exists, and this item stays open until it lands rather than being ticked on the half that could be done.
  - [x] One client per provider, never one per slot - `ElevenLabsTtsProvider.MaxConcurrent` justifies itself with *"Callouts, crew lines and re-voiced comms all share the same account, so the gate has to be here rather than in any one pipeline."* **That reasoning only survives if two slots choosing the same provider share one instance.** Six clients would each believe they owned the account's whole concurrency budget, and the failure is the one Phase 11 already fixed: a red banner and a sentence the Commander never heard. Accepted when a test pins that selecting one provider for two slots constructs it once. **Built 2026-08-25.** Pinned by `SlotsSharingAProviderShareOneClient` and by its harder half, `OneSlotLeavingASharedProviderDisposesNothing` — a provider is released only when the last slot leaves it, and disposing on the first would arrive as five silent slots rather than one. `MeteredTtsProvider.Dispose` stopped forwarding for the same reason: several decorators now wrap one shared client, and the first slot rewired would otherwise close the connection the other five are still speaking through.
  - [x] The wiring plan becomes a diff and stays a pure function - `SpeechWiring.Plan` returns one `RebuildClient` bool today. It becomes per-slot: which changed, which clients to build, which to dispose, which to share. **It builds nothing, disposes nothing and reaches no network**, which is the property it was extracted for and the reason both faults found in one afternoon's hand-testing became reachable by a test. Its harness grows with it, keeping the key-arrival edge it was written for — selecting a provider before pasting the key, then pasting it. **Built 2026-08-25.** Still pure, still builds nothing: `Plan` answers which providers to construct, which to release, which slots moved and whose list to ask for again.
  - [x] A file written before this still loads, and sounds the same - `ProviderVoices` already holds one field per role inside `VoiceChoices`, so the store gains no dimension; what changes is which fields move on a switch, and `VoiceMemory.Switched` becomes per-group. `Speech.Provider` keeps its meaning as the Aboard group's, and the other five read a new nullable map where **absent means "the same as Provider"**. Phase 19's rule is unchanged and applies twice over: a choice that cannot be filed under a known provider is dropped rather than filed under a guess. Accepted when a settings file from before this change loads, and every voice is the one it was. **Built 2026-08-25.** One field the plan did not anticipate: `CarrierVoicesProvider`, a second stamp beside `VoicesProvider`. Two slots own a stored voice — the ship's AI with its per-core pairings, and the carrier — and once they can be on different providers, one record of *whose voices these are* cannot answer for both. A map keyed by slot was the alternative and was rejected: the other four slots draw from the provider's pool at the moment somebody speaks, so four of its six keys could never be written.
  - [x] The disclosure becomes a table, because one sentence can no longer be true - Phase 4 requires stating exactly what leaves for the selected provider, and there is no longer one. Accepted when the row states what leaves **per slot**, when the Privacy list agrees with it rather than restating it, and when the state the item above describes — the core paid for, no other player's words leaving — reads as one legible sentence rather than six rows a Commander has to combine. **Built 2026-08-25**, leading with the legible sentence rather than the rows.
  - [x] Which slot is costing money is a question worth being able to ask - `SpeechSpend` records per provider and that survives untouched. Per-slot is what the refactor makes askable for the first time, and it comes free without weakening the metering seam: `MeteredTtsProvider` stays the single place counting, and one thin decorator per slot wraps the **shared** client, so the count knows the slot without `ITtsProvider` learning about roles and without a second client. Accepted when the cost row breaks down per slot and the per-provider total still agrees with itself. **Built 2026-08-25.** Auditions bill to the slot being cast rather than to a category of their own, which is what makes the per-slot breakdown and the per-provider total sum the same rows instead of keeping two tallies.
  - [x] What this phase does not do - **No per-role speaking rate**, which was raised while this was being proposed and deliberately kept out: it is a second dimension on a row that already has a doc anchor, it overturns `VoiceCast.Rate`'s own stated reasoning, and it carries an open question about which range the row narrows to when two categories disagree — so it is [change-requests.md](docs/plans/change-requests.md) **43** rather than a line here. **No new provider**, which is Phases 58 and 59; this one is the seam they both arrive through. The rulings behind every item above, and the seams they cut, are in [per-role-voice-providers.md](docs/plans/per-role-voice-providers.md), signed off 2026-08-25.

- [x] **Phase 58 — A voice from OpenAI**
  - [x] A third provider, and the seam it arrives through is already built - Asked for 2026-08-25, written 2026-08-26 once the spike behind it came back. **Phase 57 did the structural work**, so this is a catalogue entry, a client and a key row rather than a redesign: a slot names a provider, one client is built per provider however many slots name it, and a metering decorator per slot counts what it spends. Nothing above `ITtsProvider` learns that a third provider exists — which is what the seam was for (architecture.md §2). **Whatever concurrency gate the account needs goes in the client**, beside `ElevenLabsTtsProvider.MaxConcurrent` and for its stated reason: the gate is a property of the account rather than of any one pipeline, and Phase 57's shared client is what makes that true again. Accepted when a slot can name OpenAI, when every voice still comes out of the one arbiter in the order it was written, and when selecting it for two slots constructs it once — which is already a test and should stay one. **Built 2026-08-26, and it found a defect Phase 57 had left behind.** The key row's `AppliesWhen` still read `speech.provider` alone, so putting the carrier on ElevenLabs while the cockpit stayed on Edge configured the slot that needed a key and took the box to put one in off the page. It now applies while **any** slot names that provider. Nothing else about the seam had to move: the client map, the per-slot decorator and the shared-client rule all took the third provider without a change.
  - [x] The model is a dated snapshot, and the pin has its reason beside it - **`gpt-4o-mini-tts-2025-12-15`**, enumerated beside the floating `gpt-4o-mini-tts`, and pinned for the reason `ElevenLabsTtsProvider.DefaultModel` already gives: a floating alias is a voice that can change under a Commander who chose it. The date is not decoration — `instructions` is documented to work on this model and **not** on `tts-1` or `tts-1-hd`, so the pin is also what keeps [#49](https://github.com/dseelinger/d47/issues/49) reachable later without moving it. Accepted when the model is a constant with the snapshot date in the comment, and when no code path can send the floating name. **Built 2026-08-26.** Asserted as the literal id rather than against the constant, following `TheModelOnTheWireTests`' rule that changing a pin should be a decision somebody makes rather than a change a test follows silently.
  - [x] A static catalogue is honest, and the contract should say so - OpenAI has **no voices endpoint**: the thirteen built-ins are enumerated in the request schema's own `voice` description — `alloy`, `ash`, `ballad`, `coral`, `echo`, `fable`, `onyx`, `nova`, `sage`, `shimmer`, `verse`, `marin`, `cedar`. So `VoiceListing.Unreachable` is unreachable here and `NoKey` is wrong: the voices are known without a key. **Answer `Listed` always, and do not invent a fifth state** — `VoiceCatalogue.WhyEmpty` is only consulted when the list is empty, which for this provider it never is, so a fifth state would need a sentence describing a situation that cannot arise. `VoiceIdsAreOpaque` is **false**: `onyx` says who it is in a way `JBFqnCBsd6RMkjVDRZzb` never did, which is the property that flag was added to record. Accepted when the picker fills with no key stored, and when a test pins that `WhyEmpty` is never reached. **Built 2026-08-26**, with two decisions the item did not anticipate. **No gender is claimed** — OpenAI publishes none, and a voice tagged as a woman's decides who is given it in `VoiceCast.ForSender`, which is too much to build on a hunch; the sex-matching widens to the whole pool, which is the case that code already handles for a provider tagging nothing. And the accent field holds **"multilingual"** rather than a locale, which is both true and the value `VoicePool` is careful to read as a word — asserted, because reading it as a locale once discarded 472 voices of a 473-voice account.
  - [x] The Check button needs a probe that is not a voice list - It proves a key by listing today, and there is nothing here to list. **The cheapest honest probe is a one-character synthesis, discarded** — a fraction of a cent, and it exercises the exact call that has to work rather than a proxy for it, which is the argument the ElevenLabs check already makes for itself. Whatever it is, **a failure must still tell "refused the key" from "could not be reached"**, which `docs/spikes/elevenlabs-voice-sources.md` §3 establishes as load-bearing and which the two `SecretCheck` outcomes already express. Accepted when a wrong key says so in words an account holder can act on, and when an unreachable host says something different. **Built 2026-08-26, and the obvious classifier was wrong.** A refused voice had to be told from a refused key, and the first attempt looked for the word *voice* in the message — OpenAI's is *"Invalid value: 'george'. Supported values are: …"*, which does not contain it anywhere. The structured `error.param` field names the field exactly, which the spike had already shown on a refused `speed` and nobody had thought to read. Two new `TtsFault` members carry the distinction the answer depends on.
  - [x] It cannot be told a language, and that is a rule rather than a caveat - **Measured 2026-08-26, and the mechanism is the opposite of what the proposal predicted** — see `docs/spikes/openai-tts-language-and-speed.md`. The published schema is `additionalProperties: false`, but the live endpoint does not enforce it: `language: "fr"` is accepted with `200` and changes nothing, and so is a field that cannot mean anything, while a *declared* field with a bad value is refused with a precise `400`. So a language is **ignored, not rejected** — the worse of the two, and the exact failure that moved the ElevenLabs pin off Multilingual 2: a rejection is a contract `EndpointDemotions` can see and adapt to, and an accepted-and-ignored parameter is invisible to every caller. **The consequence is a rule at the settings surface**: a provider that cannot be told what language to speak must not be offered for a slot carrying **other people's words**, because that text can be in any language at all and this one would follow it. That is a declared property on `TtsProviderInfo` rather than a name checked in a condition — the same shape as `Billed` and `VoiceIdsAreOpaque`, and for the same reason. Accepted when the four comms slots do not offer OpenAI at all, when the Aboard and Carrier slots do, and when a hand-edited settings file naming it for `range` is treated the way every unusable value is rather than spoken. **Built 2026-08-26**, and enforced in **two** places rather than one: the picker does not offer it, and `VoiceGroups.ProviderFor` does not obey a settings file that names it anyway. A rule living only in a dropdown is one a text editor walks straight past, and `settings.json` is a file a Commander reads. The fallback is **Edge rather than the ship's provider**, because the ship could be on the very provider being refused and a fallback that can loop is not one — proven by reintroducing the fault, which fails four assertions.
  - [x] The speaking rate ships with real bounds, because the warning did not hold - The brief reports `speed` as documented but historically ignored on this model. **Measured 2026-08-26 it is honoured**, monotonically across the whole `0.25`–`4.0` range: 40.82 seconds at the bottom against 10.75 at `1.0`. So the honest outcome is *not* `MinimumRate = MaximumRate = 1.0`; the row moves and the bounds are the documented ones. **Two qualifications, both measured and both load-bearing.** It **saturates at the top** — `4.0` buys 3.30× rather than 4× — so the row's help must not promise a figure the provider does not deliver. And **synthesis is not deterministic**: the same string at `1.0` came back between 9.95 and 11.45 seconds across four calls, about ±7%, so any test that asserts on duration needs that tolerance or it is a flake waiting to be written. Accepted when the rate row narrows to `0.25`–`4.0` while a slot on OpenAI is being set, and when the phase adds no test that could fail on a re-run of the same input. **Built 2026-08-26.** The clamp is the whole conversion, both ends being multipliers, and no test in this phase asserts on a duration — which is the item's own instruction, taken seriously: the spike measured ±7% between identical calls.
  - [x] What it costs cannot be converted, so d47 does not pretend it can - **The unit does not line up and the gap is not a rounding error.** `SpeechSpend` counts characters because ElevenLabs bills by the character; OpenAI publishes per minute of audio. Measured on the spike's own clips, a line of plain prose runs at **951 characters a minute** and a line full of system names and numerals at **671** — a spread of about 40% with *content*, not with settings. So a published per-minute price cannot be turned into the dollars-per-thousand-characters this row holds, and any figure d47 derived would be wrong by a different amount on every line. **The mechanism to say so already exists and needs nothing**: `ListDollarsPerThousandCharacters` is nullable, the row already renders *(not published — no price will be quoted)*, and `SpeechSpend` already reports "no rate set for OpenAI" beside a character count rather than inventing a total. `Billed` stays **true**, because "free" and "billed at a rate d47 does not know" have to read differently. Accepted when the session's speech line quotes OpenAI's characters and no dollars until the Commander sets a rate themselves, and when setting one is possible. **Built 2026-08-26, and a test fired exactly where it said it would.** `EveryProviderThatChargesPublishesARateOrTheWordingSaysSo` asserted that every billed provider published a rate, and named its own trigger in its summary: *"a provider added with `Billed = true` and no list price … this is the test that says so at the moment it is added"*. This is that provider. The guard **moved rather than went** — it now asserts that such a provider says *"no rate set"* and never quotes `$0.00`, which is what it was really protecting: free, a figure, and a count with no rate behind it are three different readings and must not collapse into one.
  - [x] `instructions` is left alone, and the seam is left where it can take it - The reason to want OpenAI at all — steering accent, tone and delivery from prose, which would let a Guardian core be *cast* rather than merely assigned a voice — and deliberately not in this phase. It is [#49](https://github.com/dseelinger/d47/issues/49). What this phase owes it is one decision made correctly now rather than unpicked later: **`instructions` is per-provider construction, never per utterance.** `VoiceSelection` is `(VoiceId, Rate, Name)` and is constructed all over; the persona is known where the client is built and not where a sentence is pushed, so accepting this later changes nothing about that record's shape **provided it is not made per-sentence**. Accepted when nothing sends `instructions`, and when the client's constructor is the place that would. **Built 2026-08-26**: nothing sends it, and the client's constructor is where it would go.
  - [x] What this phase does not do - **No per-role speaking rate** — that is `docs/plans/change-requests.md` 43, unchanged by anything here. **No local voice**, which is Phase 59 and is the half of Phase 57 that Edge cannot finish. **No tool surface spent**: a provider is a settings row and the model has never been able to choose one, which is a safety property rather than an omission. And **Cartesia does not arrive** — it was argued as the better Aboard provider *if* OpenAI drifted on system names, and the spike says it does not, so that case stays argued in [per-role-voice-providers.md](docs/plans/per-role-voice-providers.md) rather than queued.

- [ ] **Phase 59 — A voice that never leaves the machine**
  - [ ] It is the half of Phase 57 that Edge cannot finish - Phase 57 settles the cost half outright, because Edge is free and nothing another Commander types can spend the Commander's money. **The egress half it does not settle**: Edge is free and is *not* local — it sends every line to `speech.platform.bing.com` — so *"no other player's text leaves the machine"* waits on a provider that runs on the machine. That is this phase, and it is why this phase exists rather than being a nicety Phase 57 enables. Intended for the over-the-air slots, which is where free, offline, unlimited and nobody else's text leaving is worth most.
  - [ ] Unblocked on 2026-08-25, and still unwritten on purpose - The licence question was the block and it is cleared: `Microsoft.ML.OnnxRuntime` was downloaded and read rather than its metadata, its declared licence file is MIT, and its `ThirdPartyNotices.txt` carries **Eigen under MPL-2.0** — which `LicenceGate.FromFile` never sees, because it reads only the declared licence file. The gate would pass and be wrong to, exactly as it already records for `OpenCvSharp4.runtime.win`. **Accepted rather than widened**: the gate is untouched, because the gate's claim is true and insufficient rather than broken. What keeps the phase text unwritten is that it should be written against a **spiked** blend rather than a community wrapper's account of one — Kokoro's voice blending through the ONNX path is measured before anything is designed around it. See [per-role-voice-providers.md](docs/plans/per-role-voice-providers.md) §1.2.
  - [ ] What accepting the MPL costs, and it is owed before the package is referenced - Three things, and none of them is a widened gate. **`NOTICE` gains an entry** naming Eigen, MPL-2.0 and where the source may be obtained, which is what discharges MPL-2.0 §3.2 — it requires telling recipients how to get the Covered Software's source rather than shipping it. **The entry says which commit**, because ONNX Runtime pins Eigen by hash in `cmake/deps.txt` rather than by version, and "Eigen" without one does not identify what was actually compiled in. **And the decision is cited from the code** so the next reader finds the reasoning rather than re-deriving it: this provider names §1.2, and §1.2 names its two verifiable facts — `EIGEN_MPL2_ONLY` compiled in unconditionally, and MPL-2.0's Larger Work clause. One check is outstanding and belongs here rather than to the ruling: **the shipped `onnxruntime.dll` was not disassembled**, so that it contains no other third-party code the notices do not mention is read rather than verified.

- [ ] **Phase 60 — A voice library worth casting from**
  - [ ] Why Cartesia, and it is not the reason the plan first argued - Queued 2026-08-26 on the Commander's ruling: *"Nothing should be predicated on 'OpenAI is good enough' — 12 voices is crap."* [per-role-voice-providers.md](docs/plans/per-role-voice-providers.md) had argued Cartesia as a **contingency** — the better Aboard provider *if* OpenAI drifted on system names — and the spike found no drift, so it stayed a case rather than a task. **The conclusion was sound and the question was wrong.** Sufficiency on drift was never sufficiency on variety: a ship's AI, a carrier, five NPC classes and four comms slots are being cast from OpenAI's thirteen voices plus Edge. This phase is about the size and quality of the pool a Guardian core is cast from, and it is written on that footing rather than on OpenAI's shortcomings.
  - [x] The voice-library size is the first thing to establish, not the last — **answered 2026-08-26: 924 voices, 417 of them English**, against ElevenLabs' 473 on this account, Edge's 322 and OpenAI's 13. Tagged with accents, country, gender (480 feminine, 443 masculine, 1 neutral), language and a tagline, which is enough for `VoiceCast.ForSender` to match sex — something OpenAI publishes nothing for. Ids are **opaque**, so `VoiceIdsAreOpaque` is true here as it is for ElevenLabs. Every voice is public and unowned, and an `is_pro` flag exists, so the usable count on a given tier may be lower and is not established. **The gate this item set is cleared at roughly twice the bar.** See [cartesia-voices-and-speed.md](docs/spikes/cartesia-voices-and-speed.md).
  - [ ] It takes no speaking rate, and the row must not offer one - **Measured 2026-08-26, and the first pass got it backwards.** `speed` belongs in `voice.__experimental_controls` rather than at the top level, which a refusal proved: `2.0` there is a precise `400` naming the field and the range, while `2.0` at the top level is accepted with a `200` and dropped. So Cartesia **validates** a speed and then **does not act on it** — three runs per setting put the largest difference between settings (1.19s) *below* the largest spread within one setting (2.14s), and `slowest` came out shorter than `normal`. A single-sample pass had shown a tidy 26% monotonic spread across the enum and would have shipped a slider controlling nothing. **This is Phase 58's case arriving at a different provider**, so it takes Phase 58's answer: a declared property on `TtsProviderInfo`, enforced in the picker *and* in the resolver, because a rule living only in a dropdown is one a text editor walks straight past. Accepted when no rate row is offered for a slot on Cartesia, and when a hand-edited `settings.json` naming one is treated the way every unusable value is. **`change-requests.md` 43 is untouched**: the ElevenLabs-range ruling governs the providers that have a rate, and this one does not join them — note its float is an *offset* in `[-1.0, 1.0]` rather than a multiplier, so it was never going to narrow that denominator anyway.
  - [ ] The concurrency cap is tighter than the seam assumes, and the spike did not measure it — Entry tiers cap concurrency at **2—3** on the vendor's own documentation, below what `ElevenLabsTtsProvider.MaxConcurrent` was written for, and this is a provider that may carry several NPC slots at once. **The 2026-08-26 spike made no concurrent calls**, so it neither confirms nor contradicts that figure and it is still read rather than measured. The gate goes in the client, beside ElevenLabs' and for its stated reason: the limit is a property of the account rather than of any one pipeline, and Phase 57's shared-client rule is what keeps that true when several slots name it. Accepted when the cap is honoured with several slots on Cartesia speaking in one exchange, and when exceeding it degrades the way a refusal already does rather than dropping a line.
  - [ ] It is eligible where OpenAI is barred, and that is the second reason - Phase 58 established a rule with teeth: a provider that **cannot be told a language** must not be offered for a slot carrying other people's words, because that text can be in any language and the provider would follow it. OpenAI is refused for all four comms slots in two places for exactly that. **Cartesia pins language explicitly**, so it is the second provider — after ElevenLabs — that can legally take a re-voiced slot at all. Accepted when the comms slots offer it, when the pinned language is the one the settings surface says, and when a hand-edited `settings.json` naming it is honoured rather than treated as unusable.
  - [ ] What it costs, in the unit `SpeechSpend` actually counts — **and the API will not say** (measured 2026-08-26: `/balance`, `/usage`, `/subscriptions/current` and `/account` are all 404, so there is no rate and no balance to read). The unit has to come from the published price page, read by a person and recorded rather than scraped. Phase 58 hit this and had to say so out loud: `SpeechSpend` counts characters, OpenAI publishes per minute, and the conversion is wrong by up to 40% *with content*, so no figure is quoted at all. **Establish which unit Cartesia bills in before assuming this one is easier.** If it bills per character the row quotes a real rate like ElevenLabs; if it does not, it takes the same *(not published)* treatment, and `EveryProviderThatChargesPublishesARateOrTheWordingSaysSo` is the test that will say so at the moment the provider is added — which is what that test exists for.
  - [ ] What this phase does not do - **No Azure.** Raised the same day as the value option and **declined**: *"it doesn't differ from the FREE option enough to warrant it."* Azure AI Speech is the paid, supported endpoint for the same neural voices Edge already reaches for nothing, and its Neural HD tier — the `DragonHDLatestNeural` voices — buys prosody rather than the variety this phase is about. The one argument that is *not* about voices is recorded rather than acted on: the Edge endpoint is undocumented, and Azure would be the insurance policy if Microsoft ever closed it. That is a resilience case and should be argued as one on the day, not smuggled in here. **No local voice**, which is Phase 59 and is a different promise — that one is about no text leaving the machine, this one is about how many voices there are to cast from.
